Preface
This book is concerned with the implementation of computer languages. It aims
to describe the design and construction of language compilers in sufficient detail
for the reader to be able to implement a language. The main language considered
is Pascal, but consideration is given to other languages where appropriate. It is assumed that the reader is familiar with Pascal. Knowledge of an assembly language,
although not essential, will aid understanding of the compiled code sequences in
the later chapters.
In chapters 1 to 5 a complete compiler for a simple language is presented. The
simple language is a small subset of Pascal. This compiler is written in standard
Pascal and generates code for a simple hypothetical machine. The source text of
the compiler is available from the authors and may be used as a basis for experimentation and practical coursework. An interpreter for the hypothetical machine
code is also provided to allow compiled programs to be run. These chapters form
a self contained introduction to compilers and can be read independently from the
rest of the book.
In chapters 6 to 15 the organisation of, the algorithms used in, and the form
of compiled code generated by a complete Pascal system are considered in some
detail. The ideas introduced in the simple compiler are developed, extended and
applied to the construction of a Pascal compiler. Each phase of the compiling
process is considered in tum. In the lexical phase the requirements of Pascal are
discussed and an alternative method of constructing lexical analysers using LEX
is also described. The organisation of the symbol table is described in the lexical
phase. In the syntactic phase the creation of a recursive descent parser is considered as well as alternative table driven parsing methods. An example of the use
ofYACC, a specific table driven method, is given. LEX and YACC are illustrated
using a revised version of the P compiler. Property lists are described in the semantic phase. The next phase to be considered is code generation. Whereas in
the introductory chapters a hypothetical machine was introduced, in this part of
the book the compiled code generated by a compiler for real machines is considered in sufficient detail to expose typical constraints imposed by real computers
which can be overlooked in hypothetical examples. Examples are given for DEC
PDP11 and Motorola MC68000 series processors. The final chapter discusses the
ix

X

Preface

differences between a compiler for Pascal and one for Modula-2.
The book aims to present a systematic, engineered but practical approach to
compiler writing. An attempt is made to give a balanced view of the total problem facing the compiler writer, avoiding the pitfall of devoting a disproportionate
amount of space to grammatical considerations and syntax analysis, topics which
are amenable to more formal treatment. The text is orientated towards practical examples and suggestions for both paper exercises and coursework on the computer
are provided. At the end of this text the hope is that the reader will have firstly,
a thorough comprehension of the correspondence between high level programming language statements and machine instructions. Secondly, he or she should
understand how high level language constructs are transformed or translated into
machine instructions. Finally, the reader should be able to tackle a compiler construction project, should the need arise.
The text is based on courses given for a number of years in the Department of
Computer Science, University of Manchester. It derives principally from a course
given to second year students who are familiar with programming in Pascal and
who have some knowledge of computer architecture. This course comprises 20
lectures plus practical laboratory assignments and examples classes where paper
exercises are undertaken. Not all the material given here would necessarily be
covered in the 20 lectures; some selection of the more advanced topics would be
made. If a short course is required the material in the first 5 chapters could be
presented alone in about 10 lectures.
Many people have presented the compiling courses given at Manchester at
various times. We would especially acknowledge the influence of the contributions
of Howard Barringer, Chris Kirkham and Richard Banach on the present book.
The referee also made a number of helpful suggestions.
The source of the P compilers may be obtained from the authors over the
JANET network at address: pcapon@uk.ac.man.cs or pjinks@uk.ac.man.cs. Alternatively write to the authors at: Department of Computer Science, University
of Manchester, Manchester M13 9PL, U.K. to obtain a copy on disc or tape for a
nominal charge.

1 Introduction - What is a compiler?
A computer programmer who writes a new program chooses one of the many
programming languages available. The language is not directly understandable
by his computer system so some software is needed to bridge the gap between
language and computer. This software is a compiler. There is nothing magical
or special about a compiler. Compiling is just a particular data processing task
in which the input or data is the source text, a program written in a language
like Pascal, and the output is an equivalent program, known as the object code
program, suitable for running on some computer. One form of object program
is a set of binary instructions for a particular computer such as the PDPll or
Motorola 68000. Even binary instructions can be regarded as a primitive language,
so a compiler is in fact a simple example of a translator. Translators are a class
of objects which accept input in one language and generate an equivalent output
in another language. This translation could be done by hand, with pencil and
paper, but it would be an incredibly tedious and error-prone task. Fortunately the
translation task for computer languages is sufficiently straightforward that it can
best be handled by a computer.
Compilation of computer languages is simpler than general translation of naturallanguages for two main reasons. The first reason is that both source language
and object language are specifically designed so that correct programs written in
them have an unambiguous meaning while incorrect programs are illegal and can
be rejected. Translation betwetm natural languages, such as English and French,
has to cope with enormous vocabularies, ambiguities, metaphors and grammatical irregularities. In phrases like "time flies", in which either "time" or "flies"
might be the verb, the correct grammatical construction must be guessed or deduced from the context. The phrase "near miss" is usually interpreted as meaning
"actual miss but near hit"; this cannot be deduced merely by considering the usual
rules of English. There are many such difficulties for natural language translators. Compilation, however, involves languages with no ambiguity, with very
few, precisely defined rules for composition of programs and with strictly controlled vocabularies, in which unknown words must ordinarily be defined by the
writer before use. If any ambiguities occur this is only because the language has
been badly designed.
The second reason that compilation is relatively simple is that the object Ian1

Compiler Engineering using Pascal

2

guages are usually simpler than the source languages. This is because they normally correspond closely to the set of operations that real machine hardware can
perform, whereas source languages are usually defined to be more understandable
to humans. This means that a single source language construct or statement is
usually translated to a series of simpler object code instructions which together
achieve the same effect. The translation process is essentially a process of decomposition.
A special and particularly simple case of computer translation is the mapping
of assembly language to binary instructions using an assembler. In this case the
mapping between the source assembly language and object binary instructions is
essentially one to one. The assembly language is a convenient representation of
the binary instructions, with a similar level of detail. Neither in a compiler nor an
assembler do several source statements ever need to be synthesised into a single
object code statement; the mapping need only be one to many or one to one.
A compiler can be thought of as a mechanism for increasing the number of
languages that a particular computer will accept and obey correctly. An empty
computer can only execute binary instructions, whereas with suitable compilers
it apparently executes Pascal or other languages. The compiler can therefore be
considered as part of the "virtual machine"; that is the totality of system software
and hardware working together to allow most users to perform tasks much more
easily than if they had to use the hardware directly.
In general a compiler is therefore required to:
Read in a source program created by a user.
o Determine whether the form of the program is correct.
o Inform the user if errors are detected.
o Generate an equivalent object program.
o

Only after this compiling process has taken place is the computer able to execute the object code equivalent of the user program and produce the results that
the user requires.
The design and construction of a compiler is no different from that of any
other large piece of computer software. It is properly regarded as an engineering
process. Within such an engineering process four stages can be identified:
o Analyse and specify the problem to be solved.
o Design the solution.

Construct the solution, based on the design.
o Test and validate the finished product.
o

The division of a compiler project into these stages will aid project planning and
reduce the likelihood of unexpected cost escalation or late delivery. It will help
ensure that proper attention is paid to each stage so avoiding such common mistakes as inadequate and incomplete validation. A more reliable product should
result from a solution properly engineered in this way.

2 A simple language and machine
In chapters 3 to 5 the design and construction of a simple compiler is introduced.
The general principles presented are illustrated using a complete actual compiler.
To avoid excessive detail in the compiler a trivial subset of Pascal, called P, has
been created. The compiler, which is implemented in Pascal, is designed to process programs written in P. The compiler generates a form of code for a computer
called the M machine. The complete description of P, by means of syntax and semantic rules, together with the specification of theM machine form a requirements
specification for the compiler, which must map from P to M.
An informal description of P will now be given, followed by the syntax and
semantics of the language. This is followed by a description of the M instruction
set and an example compiled program.
2.1

The programming language - P

VAR S, N,

T;

BEGIN
S := 0; {sum}
N := 0; {number of items}
T := READ;
WHILE T <> 0 {not end of file} DO
BEGIN
S := S + T; {sum numbers read}
N := N + 1;
T := READ
END;
WRITE (N);
WRITE (S);
IF N <> 0 THEN
WRITE (S / N); {average}
END.

Syntax of the P language

6

Compiler Engineering using Pascal

the average if the set was non-empty. A zero in the input terminates the set of
integers.
A P program comprises an optional VAR declaration followed by a block of
executable statements and a terminating fullstop. In the P language all identifiers
represent integer scalar variables, and all values and expressions are of integer
scalar type. Variables are introduced by the VAR declaration at the start of a program. P has various kinds of executable statements which can be used in forming
the body of a program. These are the assignment statement, which assigns the
value of an expression to a variable, the IF-THEN statement, which obeys the
block following the THEN if and only if the condition after the IF is true, and the
WHILE-DO statement, which repeatedly obeys the block following the DO while
the condition after the WHILE is true. A block in P is either a single statement or
a sequence of statements (or blocks) separated by semicolons and surrounded by
BEGIN and END. P has one built-in procedure, WRITE, which outputs the value
of an expression, and one built-in function, READ, which accepts an integer value
from the input and can be used in any expression, but might most often be used as
the right-hand side of an assignment. Identifiers are single uppercase alphabetic
characters and constants are single digits. Spaces, newlines and comments can be
inserted anywhere except between the characters making up an operator or word;
they must be used to separate consecutive alphabetic words, for example between
IF and A, and between Band THEN, in 'IF A=B THEN.. '. The allowable forms
of statements ofP, or the grammar rules ofP, are defined by means of syntax rules.
The syntax of P is given in figure 2.2 in a notation similar to that of the Pascal
standard.
In addition there are semantic rules concerned with the meaning ofP programs.
These rules are stated informally in English, as are the semantic rules of Pascal.
Some of the rules are static and qualify what programs are legal. The static semantic rules for P are that identifiers must be declared before use and they should
not be declared more than once. Dynamic semantics concern the meaning of the
program when executed. A dynamic semantic rule of P is that the operators +, -,
*and I have the meanings of ordinary integer arithmetic, as far as the underlying
computer arithmetic permits. Furthermore, the relative priorities of addition and
multiplication are as implied by the syntax, where factors are first multiplied to
give terms and terms then added to give expressions.

2.2 The simple instruction set - M
A hypothetical computer, the M machine, has also been designed. This computer
has a very simple instruction set. Each M instruction has a function part, and one
operand part, so the instruction set is essentially of the type known as one address. The functions and operand types are listed in figure 2.3. TheM computer
has a single accumulator register (ace), in which all calculations are performed,
and which is used for all movement of values. There are four types of operand.

store ace to stack, then load ace with operand

-ace

ace/

/ace

In the above instructions the allowed operands are constant, variable or special.
br
branch to label operand
breq
branch to label operand if ace = operand in last compare
bme
branch to label operand if ace <> operand in last compare
brlt
branch to label operand if ace < operand in last compare
brle
branch to label operand if ace <= operand in last compare
brge
branch to label operand if ace >= operand in last compare
brgt
branch to label operand if ace > operand in last compare
call
procedure (or function) call of label operand
In the above instructions the operand must be a label.

Functions and operands in theM computer

Compiler Engineering using Pascal

8

The simplest is just a constant integer value. The next is the variable operand, in
which the address of a memory location is specified. The variable value is held
in this memory location, which is within a data area, logically separate from the
code area, of the computer memory. Another operand type is the special stack
or unstack operand. The stack, which can be used as a temporary store for accumulator values in complicated expressions, is organised on a last-in first-out
basis and can hold as many values as necessary. It occupies another area of the
computer memory. All these operands can be used in arithmetic instructions. The
final form of operand is the label operand. This gives the address of a memory
location containing an instruction. It may only be used in branch instructions, or
in the call instruction to specify whether a read or write operation is required. For
convenience, M instructions will be shown in a mnemonic form rather than the
actual binary instructions of a real M machine. Figure 2.4 gives an example of M
ace= variable 1 (S)
ace+ variable 2 (T)
ace=> variable 1 (S)
Figure 2.4

Mnemonic M code corresponding to S := S + T

code corresponding to one statement of the trivial program. In this example the
operands variable I and variable 2 would correspond to suitable data area memory locations of M. The instruction set of M defines the form of object code to
be generated as output by the P compiler. When the compilation is complete the
compiled code could be executed on an M machine. Although this instruction set
is being introduced in the context of producing a simple compiler, it corresponds
closely to a subset of the instructions available on real machines.

2.3

Example M program

Examination of the correspondence between P statements and M instructions indicates the transformations that the compiler ought to perform, but without showing
how these might be done. Figure 2.5 gives theM program equivalent to the example P program in figure 2.1. It is in fact the output generated by the P compiler
given in chapter 4.
Variables declared in the P program correspond to specific memory locations
in theM machine. Thus S corresponds to variable 1, N to variable 2 and so on.
Expressions are evaluated in the accumulator register. However, in some programs
expressions may be complicated enough to require intermediate values to be held
on the stack while the next sub-expression is calculated in the accumulator. For
example, in the expression A * B + C * D the value of A * B must be stored while C
* D is evaluated. The latter sub-expression can then be combined with the stacked
value, although this may not be straightforward for subtraction and division. A

Compiler Engineering using Pascal

similar situation often occurs in comparisons. These require the first expression
to be evaluated in the accumulator and then stacked, the second expression to be
evaluated in the accumulator, and then the contents of the accumulator compared
with the stacked value. In an assignment the right-hand side is evaluated in the
accumulator and then this value stored to the left-hand side variable.
For the IF statement, the comparison is made as outlined above, followed by a
conditional branch. The condition in the branch must be selected such that, after
allowing for the use of the stack, if the comparison is false then the branch is
obeyed and the following code for the THEN block skipped. If the comparison
is true then the branch is not obeyed and so the following block is executed. The
WHILE statement is treated in a similar way, except that a branch back to the start
of the statement is placed at the end of the DO block. The conditional branch after
the WHILE condition skips the DO block and this extra branch back.
For the WRITE statement, the expression to be written is evaluated in the
accumulator, which is then stacked, followed by a call to the write procedure.
The write procedure removes the value from the stack. The use of READ in an
expression is handled by stacking the accumulator, if it contains anything, calling
the read function which returns its result in the accumulator, and then combining
the values in the accumulator and on the stack as required by the expression.

2.4 Problems
2.1 Write a P program to print prime numbers less than 100.
2.2 Hand translate your P program to M instructions.
2.3 P would be a more useful language if user defined procedures were allowed.
Extend the syntax of P to allow simple parameterless procedures and functions.
2.4 Why is the M instruction set not very suitable for the implementation of
arrays? Propose the smallest changes to M which would make the implementation of arrays practical.
2.5 Consider what changes would be needed to the syntax and semantics of P
to allow real as well as integer arithmetic.
2.6 In what ways does the arithmetic provided by a particular computer affect
the semantics of integer arithmetic operations?
2.7 Compare theM instruction set with any computer with which you are familiar. Write down code sequences, equivalent to those in figure 2.5, for your
chosen computer.

3 Compiler organisation

In the next three chapters the P compiler is considered in detail. This chapter
describes the principles of the compiling process and how the process may be
subdivided into phases. It is therefore concerned with the overall design of a
compiler. In chapter 4 these principles are applied to the construction of the actual
P compiler. Chapter 5 considers the loading and running of a compiled program.
Also in chapter 5 an interpreter for the compiled M code is presented, and the
validation of the completed system is considered.
Processing a program written in P or Pascal involves compilation, link-loading
and running. These stages are logically quite separate and do not necessarily always take place at the same time. At compile time the source text of a program is
transformed into object code; that is, compiled. At link-load time this object code
is linked or combined with any other pieces of code, such as the mathematical
functions and read and write procedures, and loaded or placed in memory as an
executable binary program. At run time, or execution time, of that program the
executable binary equivalent of the source is run, possibly reading data and writing results. Figure 3.1 shows the flow of information at compile time, load time
and run time. This figure also shows that, inasmuch as a compiler is just another
program, the compile time of some program is also the run time of the compiler.
Further consideration of link-load and run time are deferred until chapter 5.
The tasks that must be performed at compile time can be logically partitioned
into the phases described by the following sections of this chapter. First the unstructured stream of characters forming the program text must be composed into
meaningful words or symbols. This phase is called lexical analysis. In the next
phase, syntax analysis, the sequence of symbols must be formed into statements.
The structure of the statements is analysed by comparing the symbols present in
the input with the given grammar. The static semantics of the program are then
analysed to establish and validate the meaning of the program, after which the
object code can be generated. The complete P compiler, presented in chapter 4,
is subdivided into the same phases, and illustrates one way of implementing each
of the tasks.
11

Compiler Engineering using Pascal

Lexical analysis

The first phase is lexical analysis. Its input is a stream of characters, the source
program created by the user. Its output is a stream of lexemes. These lexemes are
sometimes called tokens, words or items. The lexical phase is sometimes called
itemisation or scanning. In this phase, characters that are not part of the text of
the program, such as comments and redundant spaces and new lines, are discarded.
The remaining useful characters are then grouped to form numbers, names, keywords, operators and punctuation marks. These groups of characters correspond
to the indivisible strings of characters defined in the syntax of the programming
language. If the syntax is described using bead charts, as in figure 2.2, the indivisible strings are the contents of each separate rounded box, such as 'VAR' from
the rule for program. In fact, in the P language numbers and names are restricted
to single characters, thus simplifying the lexical analysis. Keywords in P include
BEGIN, END and VAR. Lexical analysis of the P program in figure 2.1 results in
the characters being grouped as follows:
VAR

S

N

T

BEGIN

S

:=

0

N

·.-

0

and so on. Spaces have been used to give a visible separation between groups in
the above. Each group of characters, such as 'VAR', 'S', '0', ':=', ',' or ';', is
then used to generate a lexeme. Each lexeme is distinguished by its lexeme type;

Compiler organisation

13

certain lexemes, mainly identifiers and constants, also have an associated lexeme
value. A lexeme is therefore represented by a pair:
(lexeme type, lexeme value).
The lexeme type specifies the kind of the lexical item; whether it is a keyword
or constant or identifier or operator or punctuation mark. In the P compiler, the
lexeme type can be constant, identifier, any of the keywords such as 'BEGIN' or
'VAR', an operator type such as addop or relop, any of the punctuation marks such
as ';' or '(' , or unknown if the characters cannot be recognised as a valid lexeme
ofP.
The lexeme value identifies the particular identifier, operator, constant or whatever. In the P compiler, the lexemes from the example program of figure 2.1 would
become:
(var
) (identifier'S') (comma ) (identifier 'N') ...
(begin ) (identifier'S') (assign ) (constant 0) and so on
Since each of the possible keywords and punctuation marks is already a separate
type of lexeme, no further distinguishing lexeme value is necessary. If, in the
extreme, all keywords had been made the same type, the lexeme value would be
used to specify which keyword had been read. However, this turns out to be less
convenient for the syntax analysis phase of compilation. On the other hand, the
recognition of the syntax is aided by treating all identifiers as the same lexeme type.
As a general rule, where the syntax distinguishes between particular strings of
characters, such as the different keywords, separate lexeme types are appropriate,
but where no distinction is made, such as for identifiers, a single lexeme type is
used.
Rather than comprising a group of actual characters, the lexeme value may be
an index into a table of keywords, or a pointer into a list of identifiers. In fact,
the identifiers are often put into a kind of dictionary at this stage. This dictionary,
which is known as a symbol table, contains character sequences representing all
identifiers encountered so far in the program. As each new identifier is processed
it is compared with the known identifiers in the symbol table. If it is not found
it is added to the table. In either case the lexeme becomes a pointer to the actual
characters in the table.
The lexical analyser can usually be coded in a straightforward way, by discarding comments, spaces and newlines, and then using the next character to decide
what type of lexeme follows. For example, if the character were alphabetic, the
lexeme would be an identifier or a keyword. If it were a numeric character, the
lexeme would be a constant. A quote character might precede a string and any
other non-alphanumeric would be the start of an operator or a punctuation mark.
A possible schematic outline of the process of getting the next lexeme is shown
in figure 3.2. More detail is given in the lexical analyser in the actual P compiler
which is described in section 4.1. The outline algorithm is equally appropriate for
both P and Pascal.

Compiler Engineering using Pascal

14
PROCEDURE getlexeme;
BEGIN

WHILE NOT useful input character DO
skip character;
CASE input character OF
digit:
recognise numeric lexeme;
alphabetic: recognise identifier or keyword lexeme;
"'"·
recognise string lexeme;
other:
recognise operator or punctuation lexeme;
END;

return lexeme;
END;

Figure 3.2

Outline ofgetlexeme

One further job must be performed during this phase. When an error is found
a meaningful error message must be printed. The user will be able to identify the
cause of the error more readily if the source line which caused the error is also
printed. In a simple compiler, in which all the phases are performed on each line
before starting on the next, all that is needed is a copy of the current line. In
this case the lexical analyser usually retains the source line in a buffer, which is
output when an error is detected. Similar action is taken if a complete listing of
the source program is required. In a more complicated compiler in which more
than one complete pass is made through the source program during the analysis
phases of compilation, the lexical analyser must include in its output sufficient
extra information so that an incorrect line of the program can be output in a later
pass. This information may be lines of source text, or perhaps keys into a random
access file containing the source.
The simple approach to designing a lexical analyser used here is quite adequate
in a simple compiler and will be used for P. It is not the only possibility; there are
software tools available which help one to write lexical analysers. They consume
a formal definition of the lexical items and produce a table driven scanner. One
widely available tool is the LEX Lexical Analyser generator on UNIX 1 systems
[Lesk75], the use of which is described in chapter 7. Such tools result from the
fact that this phase of compilation is well understood, with significant theoretical
input from finite state automata.
3.2 Syntax analysis
This phase takes as its input the stream of lexemes from the lexical analyser, and
checks that they form legal sentences or statements in the source language. The
process of syntax analysis is also known as parsing. The syntax of a programming
language is usually specified formally, either by Backus Naur Form (BNF) as used
1 UNIX is a trademark of AT & T in the U. S. and other countries

Compiler organisation

15

in the Pascal standard or some similar notation, or equivalently in the form of twodimensional charts (bead charts) as used in textbooks on Pascal [Wilson82]. Such
charts were used to define P in figure 2.2.
As part of the checking, the analyser performs some recognition of the structure
of the program. For example, it recognises what type of statements are present, and
what types of subclauses or expressions they contain. This structural information
must be made available for use in later phases of the compiler. This may be
achieved implicitly, by passing the information through suitable procedure calls,
or explicitly, in an output data structure. Such structural output is called a parse
tree or an analysis record. For example, the analysis of the statement:
A:= B + C * D;
might generate the tree given in figure 3.3.

/signmen~
identifier A

/
identifier B

addop + ~
/

mulop * ~

identifier C

Figure 3.3

identifier D

Tree representation of an assignment statement

If the source statement is not syntactically correct, the parser will detect an
error. For example, a syntactically incorrect P statement is:

A := B + C := D + E ;
and the error detected would be that an ':=' is not allowed in the right hand side
of an assignment statement; that is, after one':=' has already been detected. The
actual error made, however, might have been to type a '+' instead of a ';'. Thus,
the correct line might be:
A := B ; C := D + E ;
This illustrates a significant problem with syntactic analysis. Although the analyser will detect the presence of a syntactic error, it may not be able to identify
correctly what the writer intended, or even precisely where the error occurred.
There are many good, fast classes of algorithms for parsing. One of the simplest is that used in the P compiler, described in chapter 4, namely the top down
recursive descent method. In this method a set of procedures in the compiler imitate the use of the syntax bead charts to recognise a program. The parser contains
one procedure for each of the bead charts. These procedures use the next input
lexeme to decide which is the correct branch of the chart to follow. They try to

16

Compiler Engineering using Pascal

PROCEDURE prog;
BEGIN
IF lexeme = varlexeme THEN BEGIN
getlexeme;
{gets the next lexeme and places it in the variable 'lexeme'}
idlist; {recognise an idlist}
END;
block; {recognise block}
IF lexeme <> dot THEN error; { check dot present }
END;

Figure 3.4

Procedure to recognise program in P

match the input to the contents of oval boxes directly. Where a rectangular box
occurs another chart must be consulted so a procedure call is made. The called
procedure, corresponding to the referenced chart, recognises the relevant part of
the input before returning to the original procedure.
In the P compiler, there are procedures to recognise a program, a block, a
statement, a comparison, an expression, a tenn, a factor and an identifier list. For
example, the procedure prog(program) checks for a 'VAR' lexeme, and if it finds it
calls on idlistto recognise the list of identifiers. It then calls on block to recognise
a block, and checks that the finallexeme is a '.' (figure 3.4). One consequence of
this style of parsing is that expression can call tenn, which can call factor, which
can in turn call expression recursively, as outlined in figure 3.5. Thus, the parser
makes a recursive descent through the syntax charts of the language starting from
the top-most, that for prog; hence the name 'top down recursive descent'.
It is evident that lexical analysis and syntactic analysis are each performing a
part of the recognition process. It is not so clear why the division between lexical
and syntactic is necessary, or precisely where the division should be made. There
are both theoretical and practical reasons for the division. The theoretical reasons,
which will become apparent in chapter 6, relate to the grammars used to describe
the language. The practical reasons relate to efficiency and clarity. First consider
efficiency. Lexical analysis is a simple iterative process which can be implemented
very efficiently. It will be shown in chapter 6 that syntactic analysis requires the
use of recursion, or a stack, and is intrinsically slower. Although lexical analysis
could be dispensed with, it is efficient for some of the lowest level of recognition to
be handled by the lexical analyser. Consider secondly the question of clarity. The
compiler has a better structure as a result of the division because, for example, the
syntax rules of Pare expressed more simply when words like 'VAR' are treated
as indivisible lexemes. This results in it being easier to make choices such as
that embodied in 'IF lexeme = varlexeme' in figure 3.4. This issue will also be
considered further in chapter 8.
Syntax analysis is the phase of compilation which has seen most research effort
in the past and, as a consequence, there are many tools which help in the production

of parsers. These parser generators take a formal description of the language,
such as BNF, and produce either table-driven or recursive descent analysers. One
such parser generator is YACC which is generally available on UNIX systems
[Johnson75]. Parsing methods will be discussed in more detail in chapter 8.

3.3

Semantic analysis

The semantic phase completes the static analysis of the source program, by ensuring that the grammatically correct statements passed to it by the parser 'make
sense'. For instance, the syntax of assignment statements in Pascal does notrequire that the identifiers have been declared, that they are variables, that they are
of similar type, and that the operations can be performed on those types. All these
restrictions are defined by the static semantics of Pascal, and the corresponding
checks are performed by the semantic analyser. To perform this checking, the
semantic analyser completely processes the declarations and creates equivalent
information known as property information. This information is stored in a data
structure known as a property list. The property list is usually linked to the symbol table set up by the lexical analyser, so that given any identifier the associated
properties may be found. Once the properties are created any parse trees associated with declarations may be dispensed with. Some part of the property list may
need to be carried forward to help provide diagnostic information for the user in
the event of an error at run time.
As well as checking the 'meaning' of the statements, information such as the
types of operands and operations is added to the structural information created
by the parser, ready for passing on to the next phase. For example in Pascal, a
'+' operator may be found to be operating on integers, or reals, or a mixture, or
subranges, or even on sets. The correct sort of addition, such as real addition, can
then be specified. As all variables in P are of type integer the semantic phase of
the P compiler is very simple and merely comprises a check that identifiers have
been declared once before use. The correct interpretation of operators is part of
the dynamic semantics of the language and must be treated in the code generation
phase. In P this is trivial because all operations are integer and correspond directly
to machine functions.
Unlike syntactic rules, the semantics of a programming language are difficult to
specify rigorously, and so are usually specified informally, and often incompletely.
Partly because of this, few tools are available to assist in this phase of compilation.
The large amount of work on the formal specification of semantics, which has been
undertaken in recent years, has had relatively little impact on practical compiler
construction, and is outside the scope of the present book. Interested readers are
referred to [Gordon79].

Compiler organisation

19

3.4 Code generation
When all the analysis phases have been completed for some part of the source
program, the synthesis of the object code can start. Code generation takes as its
input the structural information, created by the syntactic analyser and modified by
the semantic analyser, and generates as output an equivalent program in the object
code. Examples of the sort of M code program that might be generated from P
programs were given in section 2.3.
A selection of possible styles of compiled code will be discussed in chapter 10.
This section will describe the methods by which code could be produced in some
of these styles. As the majority of the object code corresponding to a P program is
generated to evaluate expressions and assignment statements, only this part of the
overall problem will be discussed. When the control and data structures of Pascal
are considered the chosen method will need extending.
The simplest form of compiled code for expressions and assignments uses
the stack as much as possible. That is, when the compiler detects an operand
such as a constant or a variable, instructions are planted to put it on the stack. In
the M machine, which is not optimal for this style of code, this would actually
cost two instructions, one to load the accumulator and one to put the contents of
the accumulator onto the stack. However, it is a simple modification to use the
accumulator as if it were the top element of the stack. To do this, the compiler
plants code to load the accumulator with each operand it finds, using the stack
and load instruction if the accumulator is already in use. When the compiler finds
an operator such as an addop or mulop, an instruction is planted to evaluate the
operation, using the accumulator and one operand off the stack, and leave the
result in the accumulator. For an assignment, an instruction is planted to store the
accumulator to the variable. For example, for the statement:
A :=B +C;
the M instructions generated are:

ace=

B

generated on recognising B in factor,
>ace= C {stack the accumulator and reload it with C}
generated on recognising C in factor,
ace+ unstack
generated as a result of recognising + in expression and
ace=> A
generated as a result of recognising A := in statement
The advantage of this sequence is that it is very easy to generate. The disadvantage,
that it is not optimal for the M machine, will be discussed shortly. No attempt has
been made to show which memory locations are used for the operands so, for
example, 'B' should be interpreted at the memory location of M which will hold
the value of B at run time. In the P compiler the 26 required variables will be
allocated fixed memory locations but in Pascal it will be necessary to decide on a

20

Compiler Engineering using Pascal

memory allocation as declarations are processed.
There is a slight complication in the use of a stack, in that straightforward
subtraction and division orders would find their operands the wrong way around
on the stack. 'Reverse' operations must be used which, for example, subtract
the contents of the accumulator from the operand and put the result back in the
accumulator. Hence the expression:

B-C
is compiled as:

ace=

B

>ace=
-ace

C
unstack {accumulator is set to unstacked operand - ace}

This style of code is a variant of zero-address or stacking code, which usually has
one instruction per operator and one per operand.
The number of instructions generated can be reduced by using a one-address
style, which corresponds to the instruction set picked for the M machine. This
style attempts to pair together operations and operands, so that each instruction
can do twice as much work as in the zero-address style. The method of generating
code is slightly less transparent, but the savings can be significant. For the above
example only three M instructions are required, as shown below, a saving of 25%
in the number of instructions.
ace=
ace+
ace=>

B
C
A

Some work has been done on designing tools to assist in code generation. One
common technique is to specify an imaginary computer and instruction set, such
as M, which is designed to be easy for a compiler to generate. A second, 'back
end', translator then has to be implemented which transforms this intermediate
code into the actual instructions of the destination machine. The benefits are,
firstly, that the formal framework of the intermediate code helps to control some
of the problems of translation to and from it. In particular, the back end translator
can minimise or discard the analysis and checking phases, assuming that these
have been correctly performed by the compiler, and concentrate on efficient code
generation. Secondly, and much more importantly, once a back end translator for
a new machine has been produced, every compiler that generates the intermediate
code will be able to generate code for the new computer. Similarly, if a new
compiler is written that generates the intermediate code, it is capable of generating
code for every computer for which a back end translator is available. This is a
significant help in solving the portability problem, that of moving a compiler to a
new computer.
A variant of this technique is to write an interpreter for the intermediate code
instead of a back end translator. An interpreter is a program which simulates the
actions of the imaginary computer and which can be used to 'run' the compiled

Compiler organisation

21

code without further translation. This is a simpler program to write than the translator, but suffers the penalty that programs run much more slowly. This technique
is often used on small computers, as the intermediate code is usually more compact than any real machine instructions. It may also be used during the process
of transporting a compiler onto a new computer. Given that the M computer does
not actually exist, chapter 5 presents a simple implementation of this technique.
There an interpreter is used to simulate the actions of the M computer.
3.5

Code optimisation

General purpose code generation algorithms do not necessarily produce optimal
instruction sequences. It may therefore be worthwhile to put some effort into optimising object programs that are going to be run repeatedly or for long times.
This can be done by the addition of one or more code optimisation phases to the
compiler. Optimisation is not always beneficial since the extra cost at compile
time might outweigh the benefit at run time. For example, programs under development will usually be better run unoptimised, in order to minimise time spent in
compilation.
Optimisations can be over a few statements or instructions, known as peephole
optimisation, or over many statements, such as the bodies of control constructs or
even the whole of a program, known as global optimisation. The optimisations
can be further classified as machine dependent, such as making the best use of
registers, functions, and operands available in a particular machine, or machine
independent transformations of the source program, such as reordering expressions
to be simpler to evaluate, or detecting subexpressions which are used more than
once and saving them in temporary variables instead.
An example of a situation where a simple peephole optimisation would be of
benefit is the statements:
A:=
B *C;
E :=
A/F;
which would be compiled to the unoptimised M instructions:
ace= B
ace*
C
ace=> A

ace= A
ace/
F
ace=> E

The fourth instruction, where the accumulator register is reloaded with the contents
of A, is redundant and therefore could be discarded.
Global optimisations include the elimination of repeated evaluation of common
subexpressions and the deletion of non-executable code. Loop optimisation is
often beneficial as the body of a loop may be executed many times. One such

22

Compiler Engineering using Pascal

optimisation is the removal of invariants, expressions which have the same value
every time the loop is executed, to outside the body of the loop. Many global
optimisations are best performed before code generation, on a parse tree. Peephole
optimisations are usually performed during or after code generation.

3.6 Phases and passes
The preceding sections have introduced a partitioning of a compiler into a series
of discrete phases summarised in figure 3.6. This partitioning is by no means the
only one possible, but it has captured some ideas about the problem of translation; namely, that it is necessary to know about grammar and that the text to be
translated must be understood, at some level, before the actual transformation. As
an example of a different partitioning, much of the work done in the semantic
phase in recognising and checking the types of variables and expressions could be
moved into the syntactic phase by extending the syntax (as has been done in Algol
68). Alternatively, the division between lexical and syntactic analysis could be
removed as both deal with parts of the recognition problem. There are good reasons for not removing such divisions; it would obscure the compiler structure and
lead to less efficient compilers. Whatever the detailed breakdown in any particular
compiler almost all real compilers are partitioned into phases. Such partitioning
has proved an invaluable aid to systematic compiler construction.
The final organisational problem is how the various partitioned phases can be
fitted together to build a complete compiler. The most important concept used
in discussing the problem is that of the pass. Each time that the compiler scans
through the entire source program, or some internal representation of it, in order
to apply a new transformation or perform some analysis, it is said to have made
a new pass through the source. One organisation would be for each phase to be
associated with a separate pass. Thus there would be a lexical pass, which reads
the source file and creates a file of lexemes. This would be followed by a syntactic
pass, which reads the file of lexemes and generates a file of parse trees, or perhaps
one enormous parse tree. This would be followed by a semantic pass, a code
generation pass, and possibly optimisation passes. There can also be extra pruses
associated with a 'back end', translating an intermediate code to the final object
code. For example, it is common in the UNIX system for the compilers to generate
object code in the form of written assembly language. Extra passes are needed to
assemble this into binary code.
Sometimes problems with the semantics of a language may mean that this
phase requires more than one pass. For example, Algol 60 and Algol 68 do not
impose the Pascal requirement that the declaration of an identifier must always
precede its use. Compilers for these languages therefore need two passes; one to
find all the declarations of identifiers and another to check all their uses. Organisation into several passes can also help if there is a problem fitting the compiler
into a limited computer memory. Instead of the whole compiler being in memory

at once, only the tasks associated with one pass are needed at one time.
Phases can also be combined into fewer passes. This is sometimes unavoidable, as in the case of some older languages which are not amenable to a clean
separation between lexical and syntactic analysis. In these cases the input stream
of characters may have to be scanned in advance in order to determine what a
particular group of characters represents. For example, in FOR1RAN, because
spaces are not significant, the following statement:
DO 10I= 1,100
would have to be scanned as far as the comma before the compiler or a human
could deduce that the 'DO' is the start of a DO statement, and not part of an
assignment to 'D0101'.
It can simplify and speed up the total compiler not to have to generate intermediate files between phases. Because of this, one form of classification of
computer languages is by the minimum number of passes that are necessary to
compile them. For example, because of the problem mentioned above, Algol 60
is a two pass language, whereas Pascal and P are one pass. This only characterises the analysis phases (lexical, syntactic and semantic), and source language
independent considerations in code generation and optimisation may force up this
minimum. For example, in order to perform global optimisations, the compiler
must generate an internal representation of the code for the program and make
repeated passes over it. However, the simplest Pascal compilers are one pass, as
is the P compiler given in the next chapter.
An alternative way of organising a compiler is to perform each phase in tum
on each statement of the source program. In this case there are no intermediate files nor more than one complete pass through the input. However, there are
intermediate data structures loosely linking the phases together, with each phase
operating independently of the others. This approach is most appropriate with
languages, such as FOR1RAN, where there is some definite upper bound in the
length of a single statement (10 lines each of 72 characters), but is less sensible
with languages like Pascal where, because of the block structuring, a statement
can contain an indefinite number of other statements.
A simple way of organising a one pass compiler, and the method actually used
for the P compiler, is to base everything around a top down recursive descent
syntax analyser. In this method, whenever the syntax analyser requires a new
lexeme it calls on the lexical analyser, which scans the next part of the source
text and generates and returns the next lexeme. Similarly, while the syntactic
analysis is proceeding, as each useful piece of input is recognised, calls are made
on semantic and code generation procedures. Thus semantic actions are performed
and the corresponding instructions generated and appended to the object program.
Many Pascal compilers are organised in this way.
To summarise, the phases of compilation indicated in figure 3.6 may be implemented in a single pass compiler. Alternatively, because of problems with the
language specification, or with the size of the computer, or because optimisations

Compiler organisation

25

will be performed, or because of some other considerations such as the use of
tools to help generate parts of the compiler, the compiler may be implemented in
multiple passes.

4 The P compiler
The principles outlined in chapter 3 are now applied to the construction of a P
compiler. Separate subsections describe the implementation of the various phases,
lexical analysis, syntax analysis, semantic analysis and code generation, in turn.
No code optimisation is attempted in the P compiler. The compiler is one pass,
with a simple procedural interface between the four phases.

4.1 Lexical analysis
The form of the lexical analyser is outlined in figure 4.1. The main program is
a test harness which allows the lexical analyser to be run in isolation from the
remainder of the compiler. It merely reads a sequence of lexemes and prints out
the corresponding lexeme values. Its purpose is to allow the compiler writer to
establish that this part of the compiler is working correctly and it will be discarded
when the syntactic phase is added. Figure 4.2 shows the declarations used in
the lexical phase. The possible lexeme types are elements of an enumerated type,
lexemetype, with the corresponding lexeme values defined by a variant record type
Jexemevaltype. Line is used to hold the current input line while lexemes holds a
character equivalent for every possible lexeme.
The main part of the lexical analyser is the procedure geilexeme. Figure 4.3
shows the coding of geilexeme. This is very similar in outline to the algorithm
given in section 3.1. It first skips any spaces and comments. It then tests the first
useful character; if this is a digit, it inputs and creates a constant lexeme. If it
is alphabetic, it inputs either an identifier, or if there is more than one alphabetic
character, a keyword lexeme. Otherwise, it inputs and creates a lexeme consisting of non-alphanumeric characters. There are no strings in P, so no allowance
has to be made for them. It is less clumsy in the Pascal coding to use a series
of tests rather than the case statement of section 3.1. The new lexeme is placed
in the global variables, lexeme of type lexemetype, and lexemeval of type lexemevaltype. These variables always contain the current lexeme ready for use by
the syntax analyser. A global variable is used because it is inconvenient to pass
the lexeme repeatedly between the many syntactic routines which may examine
it.

Figure 4.1

Overview of the lexical analysis phase

There are two auxiliary functions used by getlexeme: nextch and getch. The
code for these is shown in Figure 4.4. They perform the line buffering and listing mentioned in section 3.1. Nextch returns the next character in the input line
without moving over it. Getch returns the same character, but a1so moves over
the character so that the next input character is returned by the next ca11 to nextch
or getch. After a ca11 to nextch, another ca11 to nextch or getch would return the
same character. The implementation of getch includes a ca11 to nextch. Nextch
checks to see if its line buffer is empty, in which case it inputs and echoes a new
line of text. It then returns the next character from the buffer. Since the compiler
is written in Pasca1 and because of the way that characters are buffered by nextch,
newlines are already converted to spaces at this stage. Observant readers may
notice that because nextch and getch manipulate Jinepos and linesize they are not
pure functions. A modular language would have the advantage of being able to
hide this from the rest of the compiler but in Pasca1 the use of impure functions
appears the best way of expressing what is required.

procedure getlexeme;
var ch: char;
name: nametype;
charno: integer;
begin {getlexeme}
{ ignore spaces and comments }
while nextch in[' ','{']do begin
ch := getch;
if ch = '{' then
repeat
until getch='}';
end;
ch := getch;
if chin ['0' .. '9'] then begin {try to recognise a constant}
lexeme := constant;
lexemeval.constval := ord(ch) - ord('O');
end
else if chin ['A' .. 'Z'] then
{ try to recognise an identifier or keyword }
if not (nextch in ['A' .. 'Z']) then begin
lexeme := identifier;
lexemeval.idval := ch;
end
else begin
{try to recognise read,write,if,then,var,begin or end}
for charno := 2 to namechars do
nama[charno] :=' ';
charno := 2;
name[l] := ch;
while nextch in ['A' .. 'Z'] do
if charno <= namechars then begin
name[charno] := getch;
charno := charno + l;
end
else
ch := getch;
lexeme := beginlexeme;
while (lexeme <= endlexeme) and (name <> lexemes [lexeme] ) do
lexema :s succ (lexeme);
if lexeme > endlexeme then begin
lexeme := unknown;
error(6); {unknown name}
end;
end

function nextch:char;
begin
if linepos>linesize then
if eof then begin
error(l); {end of file encountered}
goto 999; {fatal error}
end
else begin
linesize := 1;
while (not eoln) and (linesize<linewidth) do begin
read(line[linesize]);
write(line[linesize]);
linesize := linesize + 1;
end;
writeln;
if eoln then begin
line [linesize] . - ' ';
readln;
end
else begin
linesize := linesize - 1;
error(2); {line too long}
end;
linepos := 1;
end;
nextch := line[linepos];
end;
function getch: char;
begin
getch := nextch;
linepos := linepos + 1;
end;

Functions nextch and getch

infonnation about the line buffer to output an 'j' pointing to the last character
used, followed by a message indicating the nature of the error. The only reason
that a line buffer is used in the lexical analyser is to make it possible to output
source lines containing errors along with pointers to where the errors occurred.
The implementor must choose a suitable length for the line buffer. Pascal and P
place no restriction on the length of a line, but the practical limits in any given
computer system, such as the width of printing devices, can be used to detennine
a sensible length. Getlexeme will report an error if it finds a non-alphanumeric
character that is not part of the syntax of P or if it cannot find an '=' after a ':'.

if a source line is longer than the size of the line buffer. Encountering an end of

procedure error (n :integer);
begin
write('j':linepos- 1);
case n of
1
writeln('end of input encountered');
2
writeln('line too long, max width=' linewidth);
3
{unexpected symbol};
4
writeln('unknown character');
5
writeln('= expected after :');
6
writeln('unknown keyword');
7
writeln('lexeme skipped after error');
8
writeln('variable already declared');
9
writeln('variable not declared');
10
writeln('code overflow');
end;
errors := true;
end;

Figure 4.5

Error reporting

file is treated as a catastrophic error, in that no attempt is made to cope gracefully
with the fault. Instead, the lexical analyser makes a non-local goto, to label 999,
to terminate the compilation. The lexical analyser could be modified to return a
special end of input lexeme, which could be tested for everywhere by the rest of the
compiler in order to generate slightly more informative error messages. However
this was not done, to avoid unnecessary complication in the P compiler. Note that
the error routine can cope with all the errors that might be detected in any phase of
compilation, rather than just the potential lexical errors. In the case of error 3 no
message is output for an unexpected symbol, as it is better for routine checkorskip
(figure 4.8) to print a list of expected symbols instead.
There is no attempt to create a symbol table in this phase, although this might
be done in the lexical phase of compilers for languages larger than P. This is
because what is required from the lexical analyser is a simple encoding of the
identifier name that can fit into the value part of a lexeme, and be used subsequently
to retrieve information about the name. As P only has single character identifiers,
these single characters are already a sufficiently compact encoding for the lexeme
value. More information will be needed in the semantic analysis phase, because
of the property information required about identifiers, but the single character can
still be used as an index into an array representing the property list.
All that remains in the lexical analyser is the initialisation of lexemes and
several other variables as shown in Figure 4.6.

4.2 Syntax analysis
The syntax analyser is implemented as a recursive descent parser, with one procedure for each syntax bead chart, as outlined in section 3.2. As a consequence
of the chosen split between lexical and syntactic analysis, some of the bead charts
do not require corresponding syntactic procedures. For example, the lexical analyser deals with identifiers and constants, and also classifies some of the operators
as addop, mulop or relop. Hence the charts for addop, mulop, relop, identifier,
constant and comment do not have procedures.
There is no explicit output from the parser. This is because, as mentioned in
section 3.6, the later phases of compilation will be combined with the parser, partly
by embedding pieces of code and partly by calls on semantic or code generating
routines from within the parser. A table driven parser, of the type described in
chapter 8, could have been used but for straightforward languages the recursive
descent approach is quite adequate.
The only auxiliary syntactic routine is the Boolean function checkorskip, described below, which is used to check lexemes and to aid error recovery. In general, each of the main parser routines contains code which mirrors the body of the
corresponding syntax chart. This code matches expected lexemes from the input
and makes calls on other syntax routines. It also contains while or repeat loops
to implement loops in the syntax charts and if then else statements to implement
alternatives within the syntax. For example, since an expression consists of a term
possibly followed by further addop-term pairs, the expression routine first calls the
term routine to recognise the initial term. The optional addop-term pairs are then
recognised using a while loop so that for each subsequent addop lexeme recognised
a further call is made on the term routine. This is shown in figure 4.7. Similarly,

procedure expression;
begin
term(stopset + [addop]);
while lexeme = addop do begin
getlexeme;
term(stopset + [addop]);
end;
end;

Figure 4. 7

The syntactic routine expression

within statement the next lexeme may have one of several different types, each
indicating a different path through the chart, so a choice is made in the routine by
a series of if-then-else's. A part of a path within a syntax chart without branches
or loops corresponds to a linear piece of code which accepts each of the items on
the syntax chart path in tum.

The P compiler

35

The need for helpful treatment of the user program following errors may mean
that the body of a parser routine differs from its bead diagram, although it will
still only recognise valid input as being correct. A simple case is the omission
of a single lexeme. For example, idlist will continue if a ',' is omitted between
identifiers, although it does also generate an error message. Similarly comparison
will always try to input two expressions, even if the separating relop is missing,
again generating an error message.
A more significant change caused by difficulties in error recovery is that all
the main parser routines, except prog which recognises the whole program, have
as a parameter a set of lexeme types. The set of possible lexeme types which can
legally start the next part of the input, after any text corresponding to a rule has
been processed, is known as the follow set. This set is normally used as all or part
of the second parameter, stopset, for calls to checkorskip. Checkorskip is shown
in figure 4.8. This routine checks that the type of the current lexeme is in okset, the

function checkorskip (okset, stopset: lexemesettype): boolean;
var alexeme: lexemetype;
orflag: boolean;
begin
if lexeme in okset then
checkorskip := true
else begin
checkorskip := false;
error(3); {okset expected}
orflag := false;
for alexeme := firstlexeme to lastlexeme do
if alexeme in okset then begin
if orflag then
write(' or');
or flag : = true;
write(lexemes[alexeme]);
end;
writeln(' expected');
if stopset <> [] then
while not (lexeme in stopset) do begin
error(7); {lexeme skipped after error}
getlexeme;
end;
end;
end;

Figure 4.8

The routine checkorskip

first parameter, and returns the value true if so. Otherwise, it generates an error
and skips input lexemes until one with a type in stopset, the second parameter,
is encountered, and returns false. Thus when prog calls block, it gives dot, the
lexeme type which terminates the block in this context, as the parameter. This

36

Compiler Engineering using Pascal

change is important because a programming language is normally specified by
what is known as a context free grammar (as discussed later in chapter 6). That
is, a syntactic element such as block is the same, whether it is being used directly
from prog, or whether it is being used in statement after a 'DO' or 'THEN'. This
allows the corresponding routines to be written in a simple way, in that they do
not need to know the context in which they are called. However, to give good
error diagnostics the routines do need to be given some contextual information,
to allow recovery to a sensible point in the text after an error. Thus, in the prog
context, the parameter is set so that block recovers to a '.' after an error. In the
statement context it will cause recovery to a ';' or an 'end'.
One consequence of this is that the parameter is often passed on, possibly with
some extra lexeme types, to other routines called by a parser routine. Thus when
expression calls term, term calls factor, and factor calls expression, the parameter
is passed on with an extra 'addop', 'mulop' and ')' respectively.
The final difference between a simple encoding of the grammar of P and the
actual parser routines is in the treatment of faults such as a missing 'BEGIN'.
Although the syntax only allows for a single block the procedure prog searches
repeatedly for blocks until a fullstop, or the end of input, is encountered. Second
and subsequent blocks cause an error message to be generated, but this technique
has the advantage that the whole program text will be parsed for further errors, even
after a missing 'BEGIN' or an extra 'END'. Further discussion of the treatment
of syntax errors is postponed to chapter 8.

Figure 4.9

4.3

The syntactic analysis phase

Semantic analysis

It was stated in chapter 2 that the only static semantic rules for the P language
are that identifiers must be declared before use, and should not be declared more
than once. The semantic phase consists in the main of two routines, declid and
checkid, which check these two rules. Declid is called when an identifier is to be
declared in idlist. It makes sure that the identifier has not already been declared.
It then allocates storage using nextvariable, which indicates the next free position
in the data space. This action, of allocating data space, could be postponed to
the code generation phase, as these data addresses are only of importance during
code generation, but is included here for convenience. Whether it is thought of
as a semantic action or as part of code generation, the actual code in the final P
compiler which performs storage allocation would be found in the same place, in
declid.
Identifiers may be used in expressions or on the left hand side of an assignment.
Within expressions identifiers appear as one alternative of factor.Checkidis called

4.4 Code generation
The P compiler is organised so that those parts of the compiler that perform lexical and semantic analysis and code generation are called from, or embedded in,
the routines that perform the recursive descent parse. The routines which actually
plant the compiled instructions are plant, plantllccload, savelabel, saveforwardlabel and plantforwardlabel. These are called from the syntactic routines, which
are extended to make the decisions about what code to plant. The most important
is plant which is called each time an M code instruction is generated. The routines
are shown in figure 4.11. These routines include statements used to output a trace
of the process of code generation. The binary version of the compiled code is
usually the main output generated by a compiler. In the case of the P compiler the
generated code is placed in the array store which simulates the part of the M computer's memory which contains code. It does not therefore appear as an explicit
output from the compiler, and the code planting routines reflect this.
For convenience, figure 4.12 shows a memory location of theM computer
as a record structure of type inst. Strictly speaking the information in a memory
location must be encoded as a binary pattern. Consequently, if the binary code
of a real computer were being generated the assembling of the binary instructions
would be more elaborate than shown here, but nevertheless straightforward. Tables
would be used to map from the functions and operands in abstract terms to the
binary patterns in a particular machine. The function and operand parts would
then be merged to give the bit pattern for the complete instruction.
The parser routines which call the code planting routines are statement, comparison, expression, term and factor. Because each M code instruction comprises
an operator and an operand the parser routines remember the last operand or operator recognised until it can be combined into an operator-operand pair. In this way
reasonably efficient M code is generated. In factor, each operand is recognised and
returned to term. Term remembers the first such operand and looks for a mulop.
If it finds one, it calls plantaccload, which generates code to load the first operand
into the accumulator. Term remembers the mulop, and calls factor for the next
operand. Term then plants an instruction corresponding to using the remembered
mulop to combine the new operand with the accumulator. Expression behaves
similarly, except that it calls on term and recognises addops. To do this, each of
factor, term and expression returns a result parameter ( optype) indicating whether

42

Compiler Engineering using Pascal

procedure plant(fn: functiontype; optype: optypetype;
opval: integer) ;
begin
if codepos >= maxcode then begin
error (10);
codepos := 0; {not very satisfactory, but adequate}
end;
with store [codepos] do begin
funct := fn;
case optype of
specialop:
begin
accmode := specialop;
accval .- opval;
end;
labelop:
if fn = call then
callval := opval
else
brval := opval;
constop:
begin
accval := opval;
accmode := constop;
end;
varop:
begin
accval := opval;
accmode := varop;
end;
end;
write ('plant ');
listaline (codepos);
codepos := codepos + l;
end;
end;
procedure plantforwardlabel(pos: integer);
begin
writeln('label: used from', pos);
store[pos] .brval := codepos;
end;

begin
writeln('label used');
saveforwardlabel := codepos;
end;

begin
writeln('label:');
savelabel := codepos;
end;

procedure plantaccload (optype: optypetype; opval: integer);
begin
if optype <> specialop then
if accinuse then
plant (stackaccload, optype, opval)
else
plant (accload, optype, opval);
accinuse := true;
end;

Figure 4.11

The code planting routines

they have collectively recognised a simple operand (variable or constant), or else
indicating that the subexpression recognised is more complicated and will be held
in the accumulator at this point at run time. Thus in the statement A := B + C, the
first call of factor returns the variable B to tenn. Tenn returns this directly to expression, which actually plants an instruction to load B into the accumulator. The
second call of factor returns the variable C to tenn which returns it to expression.
Expression plants an instruction to add C into the accumulator. It then returns to
statement a result (special op in optype) indicating that the accumulator is loaded.
Statement then plants an instruction to store the accumulator to the variable A
which it previously noted as being on the lett-hand side of the assignment. The M
code generated for the above example by this method would be:

ace= B
ace+ C
ace=> A
The above account is not the complete story, as the methods for dealing with
more complicated expressions have still to be described. However, it does indicate
the sequence of actions by which the above code would be generated by the P
compiler. A more complicated example is:
A := B - C * D + E ;

In this situation when expression encounters the '-', it will call plantaccload to
plant code to load the accumulator with B. However, when tenn is called the
second time it encounters the'*' and needs to load the accumulator with C. Plantaccload is called by tenn but since the compiler variable accinuse indicates that
the accumulator is already loaded with B, a stack and load order is planted instead
of a simple load. Subsequently, when the stacked value is subtracted from or
divided into, the reverse instruction must be planted. In this example, expression
can tell that B has been stacked, and thus plant a reverse subtract order, because
the result from tenn will indicate that it has reused the accumulator to evaluate a
subexpression (C * D).
Notice also in this example that expression has to deal with a sequence of
three terms. B must be loaded into the accumulator the first time an addop is en-

44

Compiler Engineering using Pascal

countered. The second and any subsequent addops require no such load, as by
then the accumulator already holds the first part of the expression. Plantaccload
is called each time that an addop is encountered. The first time it is called, its
parameter optype indicates that B is to be loaded. However, because plantaccload
resets optype, and optype is not subsequently changed by expression, in later calls
to plantaccload the parameter optype indicates that nothing needs to be loaded.
Thus, depending on the situation, plantaccload is capable of planting a load order,
a stack and load order or no instruction at all. A similar mechanism is used to
avoid trying to plant a load order if the first term of an expression is more complicated than a simple operand and is thus already evaluated in the accumulator.
For example, for the expression 'A *B+C', when expression encounters the '+' it
calls plantaccload as usual to load the result from term. However, in this case, the
term 'A *B' is already in the accumulator, as indicated by optype, and plantaccload again will plant no code. In this method an expression comprising a single
operand is not loaded into the accumulator so routines such as statement that call
expression must call plantaccload afterwards just in case. The M code generated
by this method for the previous example above would be:
ace= B
>acc=C
ace* D
-ace unstack
ace+ E
ace=> A
The routines savelabel, saveforwardlabel and plantforwardlabel are used to
generate code for control structures. Savelabel is used to save a code address for
later use in planting a branch back to a previously compiled instruction. The address saved is that of the next instruction to be planted. This is similar to declaring
a label for subsequent use within an assembly language program. The other two
routines perform a similar function for forward branches. If a branch to an as yet
unknown code location is needed, saveforwardlabel is called, followed by a call to
plant a branch to a null address. Just before the destination code is finally planted,
plantforwardlabel is called to change the null address in the branch instruction to
the address of the next instruction to be planted. For example, the while loop:
WHILE condition DO block
would cause the compiler to make the following calls on the various code generation routines:
labell := savelabel;
{plant code to evaluate condition}
label2 := saveforwardlabel;
plant (branch if condition false, null);
{plant code for block}
plant (branch, Iabell);
plantforwardlabel (label2);

code is given in figure 4.12.

procedure factor (stopset: lexemesettype; var optype: optypetype;
var opval: integer);
begin
optype := specialop;
if checkorskip(startexp, stopset) then
if lexeme = identifier then begin
checkid;
optype := varop;
opval := variables[lexemeval.idval];
getlexeme;
end
else if lexeme = constant then begin
optype := constop;
opval := lexemeval.constval;
getlexeme;
end
else if lexeme = lbracket then begin
getlexeme;
expression(stopset + [rbracket], optype, opval);
if checkorskip([rbracket], stopset) then
getlexeme;
end
else if lexeme = readlexeme then begin
getlexeme;
if accinuse then
plant(accstore, specialop, stack);
plant(call, labelop, readproc);
accinuse := true;
optype := specialop;
end;
end;
procedure term (stopset: lexemesettype; var optype: optypetype;
var opval: integer);
var operator: mulvaltype;
roptype: optypetype;
ropval: integer;
begin
factor(stopset + [mulop], optype, opval);
while lexeme = mulop do begin
plantaccload (optype, opval);
optype := specialop;
operator := lexemeval.mulval;
getlexeme;
factor(stopset + [mulop], roptype, ropval);
if roptype = specialop then
plant(reversemul[operator], specialop, unstack)
else
plant(forwardmul[operator], roptype, ropval);
end;
end;
