Problems

4.1 In P only single digit constants are permitted. Modify the lexical analysis
phase of the compiler to permit multidigit constants.
4.2 In P a '+' or '-' sign is not allowed at the start of an expression or bracketed
subexpression. Modify the syntax of P to allow this. Make the corresponding changes to the syntactic phase of the compiler. The unary '-' introduced
in this way requires changes to the code generation phase. Design and implement the required changes.
4.3 Design and implement a repeat .. until construct for P, similar to that of
Pascal.
4.4 Modify the P compiler to permit upper and lower case letters to represent
distinct identifiers.
4.5 Consider what would be involved in introducing an exponentiation operator,
f, into P, where a f b means ab. How would you give l a higher priority
than multiplication or division?
4.6 Rewrite the code generation phase of the P compiler to generate the assembly language of a real computer with which you are familiar. Alternatively
you could generate directly the binary code of the chosen computer.

5 Loading and running programs
5.1

Link-load time

In a simple compiler, binary instructions can be generated directly in memory as
an executable program and the program then run immediately after compilation.
In this case, the mathematical, input and output routines built into Pascal have to
be added by the compiler. Such a system is called compile and run because the
logically separate operations of compiling, linking in these additional routines,
loading the binary program into memory and running the program are coalesced.
One potential drawback is that the program must be recompiled every time it is
run. This may not be a disadvantage during program development, but in other
circumstances users require more elaborate facilities. They may require to run a
program many times without the expense of recompilation. It may be necessary
to combine a user program with other procedures written and compiled separately,
possibly in other languages. Many users require access to portions of very large
sets of procedures, such as statistical, equation solving, or graphical libraries, again
without recompiling them every time. It may also be useful to be able to write
large programs as a series of separately compiled modules, with well defined and
checkable interfaces. Unfortunately standard Pascal does not have a module facility, but this is an important feature of languages such as Modula 2 and Ada and
will be discussed in chapter 16.
These requirements are satisfied by link-loading, where binary programs and
procedures from many different compilers are accepted and combined into a complete, executable program. In a simple system, this task would be performed by a
single program, the link-loader, and the resulting program would be left in memory ready to run. In a more complicated system, the task may be divided into two
parts. In this case the first part, the linker, merges the code fragments to form a
program but writes the result back to a file. The second part, the loader, actually
reads the program from the file and places it in memory. A common reason for
making this distinction is to save the expense of relinking if a program is to be
run many times without being recompiled. Another reason for separating the two
subtasks is to allow a computer with a small memory to handle larger programs
than would otherwise be possible, in the same way that a compiler may be split
into several passes to save space.
52

Loading and running programs

53

One of the problems that must be overcome is that when the compilers generate code, they may not know where in memory the loader is going to place that
code in the final linked program. Because of this, compilers are often designed to
produce 'relocatable code'; that is, code that can run anywhere in memory after the
minimum of changes. This usually involves making the maximum possible use of
relative addressing within a procedure or module. The problem of references to
procedures in other modules or libraries can be minimised by planting code which
uses a single table of addresses, associated with a list of names produced by the
compiler, which can be filled in at link-load time.

5.2 Run time
During link time, various routines will be added to the compiled code to produce
a complete program. Out of all the routines that might be used, there is usually
a small subset required by almost every program. These are routines used by
compilers to implement some of the features of programming languages which
would otherwise require a lot of code to be planted. In Pascal, these would be
routines to implement some of the input/output actions (READ and WRITE for
each type, EOLN, EOF, RESET, REWRITE, GET and PUT), the heap (NEW
and DISPOSE), various of the mathematical functions (SQRT, EXP, LN, COS,
SIN, ARCTAN) and others (PACK and UNPACK). Some smaller machines may
als() have similar routines to implement some of the data types and operations,
such as real arithmetic. These routines are collectively known as the run time
support system. They are usually written, or perhaps copied from an existing
support system, as part of the process of implementing the code generation phase.
In a simple system not using a loader it is the run time support system that is
automatically included with the user program by the compiler.
A program run will usually terminate naturally and return control to the operating system after tidying up after itself. The final act of a program may be to call
an operating system or run time support routine to recover the space occupied by
the heap, variables and code. If a program does not terminate naturally, because
it contains some logical fault which caused the program to crash, different action
should be taken. Some diagnostic information should be given to the user, either
automatically or by request from the terminal. This should include the routines
currently active and the current position within each, in the sequence in which
they were called. It should also indicate the values of the variables of each active
routine. Finally, some indication as to the type of error is needed. All of this
output should be in terms relating to the source program. Reference should be
to procedure and variable names, and source lines. In particular, error messages
should relate to the source, not the object code. Thus 'attempt to exponentiate too
large a number' is much more useful than 'arithmetic overflow'. This implies that
sufficient information must be saved by the compiler and loader for such references to be possible. This information is used by the part of the run time support

54

Compiler Engineering using Pascal

concerned with error handling. This will be discussed further in chapter 15.

5.3 An M-code interpreter for the P compiler
As noted in section 3.4, one of the simplest ways of implementing a compiler is to
make it generate hypothetical code not intended for a real computer. Instead, the
code is designed solely to allow a simple encoding of the source language. The
problem then is to find a way of running the resulting object code. The simplest
method of doing this is to write a small program, an interpreter, which behaves as
if it were the imaginary computer. This is analogous to microprogramming some
machine to behave as if it were the target computer. A drawback of this approach
is that an interpreter is commonly 10 to 100 times slower than a real computer.
This may not matter for small programs or in other special circumstances. As this
method permits simpler implementations of compilers, it has been used for the P
compiler.
The interpreter code is given in figure 5.1. The code shown is added to the
compiler with the additional declarations and the modified main program which
calls interpret after a successful compilation. There are two arrays, vars and machinestack, which simulate the data memory and the stack respectively. There are
also three scalars, ace, pc and sf which represent the corresponding machine registers accumulator, program counter (the pointer to the next instruction in store)
and stack front (the pointer to the top of the machinestack).
These data items are acted on by four routines, interpret, getop, push and pop.
Interpret obeys a single M-code instruction. It calls on getop to obtain the value
of the operand for the accumulator load and arithmetic orders. The operand of a
store order must be a variable which is assigned to directly by interpret. The result
of a compare order is held in a global variable branchset, ready for subsequent
conditional branch orders to use. Stack and unstack operations are implemented by
calls to push and pop respectively. Interpret sets a global Boolean, stoprun, when
it encounters a stop order. Assuming that no compile time errors were detected,
the main body sits in a loop calling on interpret until stoprun is set.
An error routine, runerror, is provided, which is called if, for example, an
underflow or overflow condition is detected. This causes an error message and a
dump of the various simulated registers and memory locations to be printed before
ending the interpreted run. The version provided is deliberately simple, in that it
generates messages containing compiled addresses rather than source line numbers
and variable names. It is a straightforward exercise to improve on this.

Loading and running programs

55

program run (input,output);
{ the full compiler given in chapter 4 must be included to
complete this program }

const
maxstack
100;
maxvar
26;

=

var
branchset: set of breq .. brqt;
machinestack: array [O .. maxstack] of data;
pc: -1. .maxcode;
ace: data;
sf: -l .. maxstack;
stoprun: boolean;
vars: array [l .. maxvar] of
record
defined: boolean;
varval: data;
end;
procedure runerror(message: packed array [lo .. hi:integer] of char);
var i : integer;
begin
writeln;
write ('***runtime error- ');
for i := lo to hi do
write (message [i]);
writeln (' at address ',pc:l,' ***');
writeln;
for i := 1 to maxvar do
if vars[i].defined then
writeln ('variable ',i:2,' = ',vars[i].varval:l);
writeln;
writeln ('accumulator
',acc:l);
writeln;
writeln ('stack front
', sf);
for i := 0 to sf do
writeln ('stack item', i, '=', machinestack [i]);
goto 999;
end;
procedure push (d : data);
begin
if sf
maxstack then
runerror ('stack overflow');
sf := sf + 1;
machinestack [sf] := d;
end;

=

Compiler Engineering using Pascal

56

function pop : data;
begin
if sf
-l then
runerror ('stack underflow');
pop := machinestack [sf];
sf .- sf - l;
end;

function getop : data;
begin
with store [pc] do
case accmode of
specialop:
if accval
unstack then
getop := pop
else
runerror ('illegal operand');
constop: getop := accval;
varop:
if vars[accval].defined then
getop := vars[accval].varval
else
runerror ('undefined variable');
end; { case }
end;

5.4 Testing and validating the compiler system
The final stage in the engineering of the P compiler is to ask whether the completed
compiler meets the specification. As this question does not have a simple answer
it should be broken down into a set of more specific questions as follows. This
helps to ensure that the validation process is tackled in a systematic way.
o Does the compiler accept syntactically correct P programs?

Does the compiler reject syntactically incorrect P programs?
o Does the compiler check the static semantic rules of P?
o

o Does the compiler generate correct M instructions?

o Does the M interpreter function correctly?
o Do P programs produce the correct results?

Even though P is a very small language there is an infinite number of possible P
programs. It is impossible to verify more than a tiny fraction of these. Nevertheless
by reasoning systematically, either formally or as here informally, it is possible to
be confident that the P system is performing correctly.
It was shown earlier that the lexical phase can be tested independently of the
rest of the compiler. There is a limited number of possible lexemes so it is easy to
test the correct treatment of these. It is also easy to construct programs which test
every path in the P syntax, but not every possible combination of paths. Treatment of incorrect programs is more difficult; feeding the compiler information
which is not a P program at all can be instructive, as well as programs containing
supposedly typical mistakes. As P has only two static semantic rules, concerning
the declaration of identifiers, programs can be constructed to test that these are
correctly applied. It is somewhat tedious to inspect the M instructions generated
by the compiler, but a check that each P construct generates appropriate code is
not difficult The M interpreter in probably best tested in conjunction with some
compiled P programs. A suite of programs with known results can be used for the
final checks that the whole system is working correctly.
In chapter 15 the problems of validating a much larger system, that for Pascal,
is considered. In the meantime, observe that whenever a change is made to the P
system the complete set of tests must be rerun. This establishes that the change
does not have any of the unexpected side effects which so frequently occur if this
precaution is ignored.

5.5 Problems
5.1 Suppose that P programs were to be interpreted a statement at a time, so that
the user could observe the sequence of executed statements and changes to
the values of variables. What additional information should be handed to
the interpreter by the compiler to make this possible? What changes should

62

Compiler Engineering using Pascal

be made the the interpreter, and in particular to the interpreter main loop
which appears near the end of figure 5.1?
5.2 Design in detail the changes outlined in problem 5.1. Implement and test
the changes to the compiler and interpreter.
5.3 Construct a P program to test as many paths as possible through the P syntax.
The program does not need to produce any sensible result but it should obey
all the rules of P. At least one path in the syntax will not be tested by this
program. State why not and write a second program to test this remaining
path.
5.4 Modify the compiler and interpreter to produce an improved form of the
run time diagnostics shown at the end of figure 5.2. The new version should
output the names of variables, rather than the variable numbers, and source
line number of the error, rather than the code address.

6 Language grammars
In the remainder of this book the principles outlined in the P compiler will be
developed and extended to the full Pascal language. Each phase of compilation
will be treated in turn. However, before the lexical and syntactic phases can be
discussed in detail, additional notation and definitions must be introduced. This
will enable the recognition of syntactically correct programs to be treated in a
rather more rigorous way than in chapter 3. The language grammars used for
lexical and syntactic rules will first be described.

6.1

Grammars for describing programming languages

In general a grammar can be defined by specifying four things:
o A set of symbols Vr. These are the terminal symbols which form the words,

or lexemes, of the language being defined.
o A set of symbols VN. These are the non-terminal symbols which are used
in forming the grammar rules. These are the syntactic elements of the language.
o A particular element S of VN, known as the starting or distinguished symbol.
o A set of grammatical rules or productions. The most general form of such
a rule is a --+
where a and are strings of symbols taken from Vr and

p,

p

VN. a must contain at least one symbol but P may be empty.

An example, from P, of a simple rule is:
addop --++

This merely defines the non-terminal symbol addop to be the terminal symbol '+'.
It can be read either as "addop produces+", or as "+ may be reduced to addop". It
does not preclude alternative rules for addopsuch as addop--+ -. The general form
of rule does not restrict the left-hand side to be a single non-terminal, as in this
example, but allows any string of symbols from VN and Vr. This is too general to
define computer languages conveniently, or to construct practical syntax analysers,
so in practice more restricted forms are used.

63

64

Compiler Engineering using Pascal

Noam Chomsky classified grammars according to the form of rules allowed.
The most general type, with rules as above, was the type 0 or phrase structure
grammar. The first restriction was that rules should be of the form:
~1Ath ~ ~/3th

where A is a member of VN and ~1 , th and f3 are any strings from VN and Vr. This
defined the type 1 or context sensitive grammar, so called because the rule A ~ f3
could be applied only in the context ~1. th .
The second restriction was that rules should be of the form:
A~/3

where A is an element of VN. This defines a class of grammar known as a type 2
or context free grammar. The syntax rules for a computer language can usually be
expressed using such a grammar. This is certainly the case for the rules of P and
of Pascal.
The most restricted form of grammar is that typically used for the lexical process, the type 3 or regular grammar. In a regular grammar only two forms of rule
are permitted:
A~aB
A~a

where A and B are in VN and a is in Vr.
The essential difference between
type 2 and type 3 grammars is that type 2 grammars allow recursive definitions
of arbitrary complexity, whereas type 3 do not. Thus a type 2 grammar can be
used to describe an arithmetic expression with bracketed subexpressions, such as
(a- (b +c))* d, and ensure that the opening and closing brackets match, whereas
type 3 cannot. A simple example of a similar problem is recognising a string such
as aa.. aabb.. bb where an arbitrary number of as is followed by the same number
of bs. This can be expressed by the type 2 grammar rules :
s~aSb
s~

It cannot be expressed in terms of type 3 rules. This is because the only recursion
allowed in type 3 rules is tail recursion where a rule is of the form A ~ aA. The
recursion in S is middle recursion, as the recursive element is both preceded and
followed by terminal symbols. Sometimes type 3 grammars are defined in terms of
left recursive rules, such as A ~ Ba, instead of tail recursive, but middle recursion
is never permitted.

Two further definitions are useful in distinguishing different forms of context
free grammar and in constructing practical parsers. These are the first and follow
sets of terminal symbols for a particular non-terminal. The first set comprises
all those terminal symbols that can start a string produced from the non-terminal.
Given the rules:
A~abc

A ~defg

Language grammars

65

where a,b,c,d,e,f and g are all elements of Vr , first(A) comprises just a and d.
However given the additional rules:
A~Bbc

B ~gggg

g must be added to the set. In general the process of adding elements like g to the
set is achieved by constructing what is known as the transitive closure of the set
of first symbols. This is achieved for an arbitrary A as follows:
o For each rule with A on the left-hand side, where the first symbol on the
right-hand side is in Vr, add the symbol to the first set.
o For each rule with A on the left-hand side where the first symbol, B say, on
the right hand side is in VN apply both steps to each rule forB, provided B

has not been already considered. This latter proviso avoids the algorithm
looping for ever on recursive rules.
Computing the transitive closure is merely repeating these two steps for all the
consequential rules B until no further rules remain to be considered.
The set of symbols which can followan application of a rule A can be computed
in a similar way.
o For all rules:

B ~ tPI.AtP-z
where tP2 is not empty add first(tP-z) to follow(A)
o For all rules:
B~t/hA

find all the rules with B on the right hand side and repeat both steps. This
step should only be applied once to each B, to avoid problems with recursive
rules.

6.2 A notation for context free grammars
There are many essentially equivalent notations for describing context free grammars. These notations are derived from Backus Naur Form (BNF). Even the bead
diagrams originally used to describe the syntax of Pascal and used in chapter 2
for P can be shown to be equivalent to BNF. The version of BNF used in the Pascal standard (subsequently referred to as PBNF) will now be described. A PBNF
syntax definition consists of:
o Terminal symbols (those of the programming language) indicated by text
enclosed in " characters.
o Non-terminal symbols (the syntactic elements of the language) indicated by
unquoted text.
o A distinguished symbol, by convention often the symbol on the left-hand
side of the first rule given.

Compiler Engineering using Pascal

66

Productions or rules of the language in the form allowed for context free
languages.
Rules consist of a non-terminal symbol, an '=' character which replaces the~
used above, and a definition string terminated by a '.' character. Thus the simple
rule given above becomes:
o

addop= "+".
Definition strings, on the right-hand side of rules, consist of sequences of terminal
and non-terminal symbols. Rather than write out a separate rule for each possible
right-hand side, BNF uses 'I' characters to indicate that the parts are alternative
definitions of the non-terminal symbol. An example of the use of 'I' might be in
a definition of 'addop' as '+' or '-':
addop = "+" I "-" .
The PBNF version of BNF uses bracketing on the right-hand side of definitions
to avoid unnecessary repetition and recursion in the definitions. These brackets
are a convenient extension of BNF but can be removed if required as described in
section 8.3. '[' ']'characters indicate zero or one instance of the contents, while
'{' '}'characters indicate zero or more instances. Also,'(' ')'characters can be
used to surround a portion of the definition divided into subaltematives by the 'I'.

proqram

Figure 6.1

PBNF grammar for P

Figure 6.1 shows the PBNF grammar for P corresponding to the bead diagrams
of figure 2.2. The one rule not given is that for comments which is difficult to
express concisely in BNF. Since comments are removed at the lexical stage this
difficulty has no practical consequences.

Language grammars

67

In practice, the terminal symbols of the grammar used to create the syntactic
analyser will correspond to the lexemes produced by lexical analysis, including
addop, mulop, relop, identifier and constant. The rules for these will be part of
the specification of the lexical analyser. One of the terminal symbols, the end of
input symbol, and one of the non-terminal symbols, the starting or distinguished
symbol (program), may be treated specially.
The most important differences between versions of BNF are as to whether
the various forms of brackets or the alternative separator are permitted. If the
alternative separator is not allowed, alternative definitions are given by allowing
several different rules to define the same non-terminal symbol.

6.3 Regular expression notation
Regular grammars are sufficiently powerful to express typical lexical rules. Figure 6.2 shows a regular grammar to recognise a few example keywords and a
stylised form of identifier. The keywords recognised are do, or and of and the

S-HIT
s~ou

s~av
T~o
u~r

U~f
V~bV
V~b

Figure 6.2

Regular grammar example

identifiers are of the form abb.. b where an a is followed by at least one b. Since
only one or two symbols are allowed on the right-hand side of a regular grammar
rule this notation is rather verbose. Much more compact and logically equivalent
is the regular expression notation. Whereas the first form of regular grammar rule
has an element of VN on the right-hand side, regular expressions only have elements of Vr on the right-hand side. Regular expressions achieve an equivalent
expressive power by allowing any number of terminal symbols on the right-hand
side. Furthermore the 'I' operator may be used, as in PBNF, to express alternation.
( and ) are allowed and {string} is used to represent any number of occurrences of
string, again as in PBNF. In fact regular expression notation is the same as PBNF
except that the " marks around terminal symbols are omitted, because there are
no non-terminals in the definition of a regular expression, and [ ] brackets are not
used. A regular expression corresponding to the grammar of figure 6.2 is:

dolo(flr)lab{ b}

Compiler Engineering using Pascal

68

The conversion from regular grammar to regular expression is straightforward,
and will not be described here. The basic technique is to expand nonterminals on
the right-hand side with the corresponding definitions and to use the { } repetition
to replace tail recursion. Regular expressions will be used again in chapter 7 in
the automatic construction of a lexical analyser.

6.4 Formal machines to parse grammars
The recognition process for regular grammars and context free grammars is well
understood from a theoretical point of view. One formal theoretical model of a
computer is a Deterministic finite state automaton or sometimes DFA for short.
It has been shown that this particular model is capable of recognising a regular
grammar. A DFA has a control box and an input device. The input device is
capable of reading one input symbol at a time. The control box is at any time in
one of a number of states. The input of a symbol causes the DFA to perform a
state transition, that is move from the present state to a new state. The new state
chosen depends both on the present state and on which symbol was read. The
DFA always starts in a particular state known as the starting state. It terminates
when it reaches one of several states marked as terminating or accepting states.
A DFA can be represented informally by a diagram in which a circle represents
a state, and an arc a transition from one state to another. Circles are labelled with
a state number and arcs with the symbols which cause the corresponding state
transition. A double circle represents a terminating state. A DFA for the regular
expression given previously is shown in figure 6.3. The DFA is constructed from

0

a
b

Figure 6.3

DFA to recognise the regular expression

the regular expression by numbering every point within the expression to give the
states. For example:

69

Language grammars
od!o2lo3(f4lrs)la6b7{ b7}

The only tricky point in the numbering is the treatment of { } repetition where the
state after the last symbol before the } is a resumption of the state before the {, in
this case state 7. The arcs are then determined by what can follow any state. So
in state 3 if the next input symbol is Fthe automaton moves to state 4, but if it is
R it moves to state 5.
Formally a DFA comprises:
o A finite, non-empty set of states K.

A set of symbols VT. These are the terminal symbols which form the words
of the language being recognised.
o A mapping from K x VT into K.
o A particular state So of K known as the starting state.
o A subset of the set of states K known as the terminating states.
o

It is often convenient in a computer implementation to represent the DFA in
the form of a table. The table has a row for each state and a column for each
possible input symbol. The mapping is represented by entries in the table giving
the state to which to move when a particular combination of current state and input
is encountered. By convention state 0 can be the starting state and terminating
states are represented by accept entries in the table. The example given in figure 6.3
can therefore be represented by the table in figure 6.4, where the numbers represent
the next state on the given input and a represents accept. The table is constructed

I state I a I b 1d I f I o I r I
0

3

1

6

2

1

2

a

a

a
a
a

a
4
a
a

a
a

a
a

a

a

a

3
4
5
6
7

Figure 6.4

a
a
a

a
a
7
7

a

a

5

Table representing the DFA

in a similar way to the diagram. The states in the regular expression are numbered
and then table entries corresponding to the transitions or arcs are made. So in state
3 if the next input symbol is fthe automaton moves to state 4, hence 4 is placed
in row 3 column f, but if it is r it moves to state 5, hence 5 is placed in row 3
column r. Finally terminating states are treated by entering a for all input symbols
except those which already cause some other transition, such as b in state 7 in the
example. Entries which remain blank correspond to error conditions.

70

Compiler Engineering using Pascal

A DFA relies on the unique mapping of input symbol and present state to new
state. It is however possible to write a regular expression in which two alternatives
start with the same symbol, such as doloflorlab{ b}. Such an expression cannot be
mapped directly into a DFA as the mapping is no longer unique. The expression
can however be rearranged so that this situation does not occur. An alternative
is to use another machine model, the non-deterministic finite state automaton or
NFA. In this model a particular input symbol may lead to one of several states
rather than a unique state. Thus several possible paths may have to be explored
until one proves to be correct and the others erroneous. Fortunately an NFA can
always be converted automatically to an equivalent DFA, albeit with more states.
This conversion achieves a result similar to rearranging the regular expression, so
NFAs will not be considered further here.
Although DFAs and NFAs are quite satisfactory for recognising regular grammars they do not have the power to deal with every aspect of a context free
grammar. The examples of an expression such as (a- (b +c))* d and the string
aa..aabb.. bb cannot be parsed correctly by these machines, nor can the grammars
describing them be converted to regular expression form. The essential additional
requirement is a stack. A stack enables the parser to keep track of the relationship
between the different grammar rules being applied. This relationship results from
the existence of elements of VN in any position on the right-hand side of rules. A
stack will be used in every parsing algorithm in chapter 8. There is a formal machine equivalent of a DFA with a stack which is known as a push down automaton.
Further consideration of context free grammar parsing is postponed to chapter 8.

6.5 Problems
6.1 Write a grammar to recognise a string of the form aa... abbb.. bc.. cc, that is
any number of as followed by any number of bs followed by any number
of cs. What is the simplest type of grammar suitable for this?
6.2 Repeat the previous question but now with the same number of a, band cs.
Is the grammar of the same type as previously?
6.3 Write a regular expression to recognise unsigned numbers as defined in Pascal. Typical examples of such numbers are: 123, 12.002 and 1.2E-2. Construct a corresponding DFA.
6.4 The syntax of Figure 2.2 assumes that lexical analysis has already reduced
keywords to a single lexeme. Rewrite the rule for statement so that the
syntax analyser could read the characters individually. Is there any difficulty
in deciding which path to choose in the revised syntax?
6.5 Write a regular expression to recognise the word symbols of Pascal. If your
expression corresponds to an NFA convert it to a DFA.

7 Lexical analysis
In this chapter further aspects of lexical analysis are considered. Firstly, the extra problems that programs written in Pascal, rather than the simpler P language,
cause during lexical analysis are examined. Secondly, the use of tools to generate
lexical analysers automatically is discussed. Finally, section 7.3 describes methods for saving all the identifiers to be found in a Pascal source program in a symbol
table. This both reduces the total amount of data held in lexemes and provides a
simple mechanism for storing identifier declarations and matching them to their
corresponding uses during the semantic phase.
7.1

Lexical analysis for Pascal

In general, the algorithms outlined in chapter 4 are sufficient to perform the lexical analysis required for Pascal. However, simply because Pascal is a much larger
language than P, there are some complications. For example, although a decimal
digit still introduces a constant, the constant may be either real or integer, depending on whether it contains a decimal point or exponent or not. Consequently the
part of the lexical analyser which inputs constants must be able to deal with either
case. One way of treating this would be to hold the number as an integer until
the decimal point or exponent is encountered, and then convert it to real before
continuing. However, this can cause problems if the integral part of a real number
could cause an integer input algorithm to overflow. A better method, assuming
that reals can be represented exactly to at least the accuracy of integers, might be
to assume that the number is real until it is discovered to be integer. An alternative method would be to exploit the fact that an input buffer is used, and scan it
looking for a decimal point or exponent before choosing either an integer or real
input algorithm.
In order to simplify the lexical analyser, constants are often defined to be unsigned rather than signed. This avoids the dyadic plus or minus operator, as in
a := 1 - 2, being confused with a signed constant, as in a := -2. The syntax may
permit unsigned constants to be preceded by the monadic operators '+' or '-'. The
syntax analysis then distinguishes between monadic and dyadic operators.
Another problem concerns character strings. As these can be of any size up to
71

72

Compiler Engineering using Pascal

a complete line of text, some structure is needed to hold the value part of string
lexemes. In any one-pass compiler this is not too much of a problem, as a single
reasonably sized global array of characters would be enough to hold a string. In a
multi-pass compiler, however, a different solution is necessary. The string could
be held directly as part of the lexeme value. However, increasing the size of every
lexeme value might dramatically increase the space taken up by an intermediate
file of lexemes. Alternatively, another large data area could be set aside to contain
all character strings, with the lexeme values simply being pointers into it.
Similar problems occur because Pascal identifiers can consist of any number
of alphanumeric characters. This and other problems to do with identifiers will
be discussed in more detail in section 7 .3. One problem which will be discussed
here is that of distinguishing between Pascal identifiers and keywords. The keywords are called word symbols in the Pascal report, and are sometimes also called
reserved words. Distinguishing keywords was very easy in P because the identifiers could only be single characters. However, in Pascal, there is no such simple
test, and each potential identifier has first to be checked against the list of keywords. This check is invoked so frequently that it should be made as efficient as
possible. One method is to use a slightly more complicated data structure than the
simple array of strings in the P compiler. In this method, the table of keywords
is sorted by length and an auxiliary array used to point to the boundaries between
the groups of the the same length (figure 7 .1). Thus any given word nee-d only be
compared with those keywords of the same length. If the word is larger than the
maximum keyword length, or if it is not found in the subset of keywords, then it is
an identifier. If it is found, the corresponding lexeme type is copied from the second field of the table entry. This is slightly generalised from the P compiler, where
the index itself is the lexeme type. The nth value of array index gives the index
for the start of those keywords in array kwordtable which comprise n characters.
Observe that the first and second entries of index are the same ( as there are no
one letter keywords) and that the lOth entry gives one after the end of kwordtable.
Field word of kwordtable holds the character strings to be compared and field lexeme gives the appropriate lexeme type for each reserved word. Words of length n
are in kwordtable from index[n] to index[n+1]-1. The total number of entries in
kwordtable is 35.

7.2 Automatic generation of lexical analysers
There are programs which can be used to generate lexical analysers automatically
from a description of the expected lexical strings and the corresponding lexical
types and values. Their construction relies on the grammar recognised by the
lexical analyser being of the shitple form known as a regular grammar, discussed
in chapter 6. It was stated there that a regular grammar can be parsed by the
actions of a finite state automaton. The analysers are often in the form of a standard
algorithm driven by data tables representing the automaton which recognises the

particular grammar. The standard algorithm is in fact an interpreter for the finite
state automaton.
One such analyser-generator system is LEX, which is available with UNIX.
The rest of this section will develop a lexical analyser for the P language using the
LEX system. LEX has several features that are unnecessary complications in this
context, and most of these will be ignored in the following discussion. For a full
description of LEX, see [Lesk75].
In the notation used by LEX, each entry in the input is in two parts. The lefthand side gives a grammar rule for the lexical string to be recognised. In these
rules simple strings are merely contained in " characters, while more complicated
strings are specified using regular expressions. On the right-hand side is the corresponding action, contained in curly brackets, which returns the lexeme type and, if
defined, the lexeme value. For example, in the P compiler, the string 'BEGIN' is
converted into a lexeme with type beginlexeme and no value. To achieve a similar
effect using LEX, the corresponding entry is:
"BEGIN"
{ return (beginlexeme); }
A slightly more complicated example is '+', which has a value of add as well as
a type of addop:
"+"
{ yylval.lexemeval.addval =add; return (addop); }
Here the value variable, yylval, has been declared as a record with two variants.
The variant used here is for genuine lexeme values corresponding to terminal symbols in the language syntax and declared like lexemeval in the recursive descent
parser already described in chapter 4. The other variant is for non-terminal symbols used by the table driven parser YACC, as described in the next chapter.

Compiler Engineering using Pascal

74

The form of regular expression used for more complicated table entries varies
slightly from that described previously. Square brackets are used to allow one
of a number of possibilities, thereby avoiding excessive use of 1. Thus a regular
expression to recognise any vowel might be:
[AEIOUaeiou]
and to recognise any alphabetic character:
[A-Za-z]
where the '-' means any character in the range between the two surrounding characters, including the characters themselves. LEX does not use { and } to indicate
repetition in regular expressions. Instead, if a character or set of characters can
appear one or more times it is followed by a '+', and if it can appear zero or more
times it is followed by a'*'. Thus, in Pascal, an unsigned integer number would
be:
[0--9]+
and an identifier:
[A-za-z] [A-Za-z0--9] *
In P, identifiers are a single upper case alphabetic character, with the lexeme
value equal to the actual character:
{ yylval.lexemeval.idval = yytext [0]; return (identifier);}
[A-Z]
where 'yytext' is an array containing the actual characters making up the current
lexeme when the generated analyser is running. Thus, in this case, yytext [OJ is
the first and only character recognised, so it must be returned as the lexeme value
(all arrays used in LEX and YACC start with index zero). Similarly, constants in P
are single digits, with the lexeme value equal to the corresponding decimal value
of the digit:
[0--9] { yylval.lexemeval.constval = yytext [0]- '0'; return (constant);}
An alternative to the previous line would be to use the built in conversion routine
atoi, which converts any number of numeric characters to an integer:
[0--9] { yylval.lexemeval.constval = atoi (yytext); return (constant); }
LEX has a few further facilities for constructing string expressions. Parts of
the lexical string can be bracketed, so that the scope of various operators is defined. For example, the following expression could be used to recognise identifiers
composed of even numbers of alphabetic characters:
([A-za-z][A-Za-z])+
Another useful facility is provided by 'I', which can be used to separate alternatives. Thus, to recognise either all upper case or all lower case keywords, expressions of the form:
"BEGIN"I"begin"
could be used. Names can be given to strings and the name in curly brackets used
instead of the string in the entries. For example, the following could be used to
recognise Pascal identifiers:
[A-Za-z0--9]
alphanumeric

%%
[A-za-zl{ alphanumeric}*

{create identifier lexeme}

Lexical analysis

75

where the'%%' line separates declarations from the entries.
It is not always necessary to return a lexeme when a left-hand string is recognised. For example, within a P program, if a space, tab (\t) or newline (\n) is
encountered it must be ignored, as it is simply a separator:
[\t\n]
{;}
A comment, contained in curly brackets, must also be ignored:

"{"[l}]*"}"

{;}

where the 'T' immediately after the opening square bracket means 'any character
except those listed here'. All characters up to but not including the first terminating curly bracket are matched by 'n}] *'' and the actual enclosing curly brackets
matched by"{" and"}".
Most of the remaining complications are to do with sensibly detecting some
typical errors. For example, an unquoted '.' will recognise any one character except a newline, and so can be used after the entries for all the genuine lexemes, to
deal with any unexpected characters:
{ ylerror ("unknown character"); return (unknown);}
where 'ylerror' is a compiler procedure which should output a suitable error message. This error entry will not match any valid characters because the LEX system
has two rules which help it resolve such clashes. The first rule is that it will always
try to match the entry which recognises the longest actual input string. The second
rule is that if two or more entries match the same (longest) input strings, then the
first such entry is picked. In this instance, all valid lexemes are matched to earlier
entries rather than the final unknown entry.
There are two other entries which pick up those Pascal-like constants and identifiers which are not legal in P:

[A-Zl{ alphanumeric}+l[a-zl{alphanumeric}*
{ ylerror ("illegal identifier");
yylval.lexemeval.idval = ' ';return (identifier); }
[0-9] [0-9]+ { ylerror ("illegal constant");
yylval.lexemeval.constval = 0; return (constant); }
The rules for resolving clashes ensure that no lexemes are recognised as being
invalid if they are in fact correct, as the entries describing errors come after those
describing correct lexemes.
The majority of the entries for the LEX version of the lexical analyser for
P have now been described. Unlike the earlier recursive descent P compiler, the
analyser does not echo its input to the screen, nor print error messages at the correct
point below the echoed line. To do the former, it is only necessary to add an extra
command 'ECHO' to each action, e.g.:
{ECHO; return (semicolon); }
";"
or equivalently:
";"
{ printf ("%s", yytext); return (semicolon); }
The problem of identifying the current position in the echoed input is more difficult
in the LEX system, although possible. It would require the compiler writer to

76

Compiler Engineering using Pascal

produce extra routines, similar to those in the recursive descent version of the
compiler, to handle the input of the P source and the printing of the error messages.
These routines would have to be given special names, meaningful to the LEX
system, such as input, unput, and yyerror. As this style of echoing and error
reporting is not considered natural within the UNIX toolset, and as it would have
unnecessarily complicated the resulting program, it was omitted.
One further problem needs to be resolved. The table driven syntactic analyser,
YACC, described in the next chapter, will only finish parsing the input at the end of
file marker. To be consistent with the recursive descent version of the P compiler,
which stops at the final '.' so that run-time data can be included in the file, it is
necessary to generate an end of file marker, a zero-value lexeme, just after the dot
lexeme. To be able to do this, the lexical analyser produced via the LEX system
must know whether the previous lexeme produced was a dot or not each time it
generates a new lexeme. To do this, a special 'start state', DONE, is introduced
in the declaration section:
%START
DONE
This start state is initially inoperative, but is switched on by the lexical action for
' '.
""
{ BEGIN DONE; return (dot); }
As soon as this start state is switched on, a new table entry is activated:
<DONE>(.I\n)
{ return (0); }
This entry always matches any further input once the DONE state is switched on,
so the next lexeme returned aftera 'dot' lexeme is always zero, and so the syntactic
analyser always terminates at this point in the input. This is rather clumsy, as a
mistyped '.' elsewhere in the P program will also terminate the parser. A better
solution might be for the interpreter to read data from another file.
The set of lexical table entries so far described forms the middle third of a
complete LEX input, as shown in figure 7 .2. The three parts of the input are
separated by lines consisting of:
%%
The extra code at the top is effectively the set of type declarations for the lexemes,
as used in the Pascal version, and for the types used in the interface between LEX
and YACC. These type declarations are contained between lines:

%{

and:

%}

In particular, the line:
=ltinclude "y.tab.h"
causes type declarations, automatically generated by YACC, to be included at this
point.
The bottom third of the LEX input is a set of variable, procedure and function
declarations. These are used to provide some default actions which the YACC
system requires, such as yyerrorand yyerrlval, which help in the reporting of and

Lexical analysis

77

recovery from syntactic errors. A similar routine for the P compiler, ylerror, has
already been mentioned above. They are also used to provide an interface between
the LEX and YACC systems and the Pascal semantic and code generation phases.
Thus callparse enables the YACC generated code and Pascal to communicate and
makelexeme translates a YACC type into a Pascal type.
One further confession needs to be made. LEX is oriented towards a compiler
written in the programming language C, and is not ideal for a compiler written
in Pascal. Hence most of the code in the input to LEX, including the lexical
actions, should be written in C. The approach taken here is that since the actions
are simple, involving only one or two lines of code, explicit C code is used. If there
were more complex actions the C code could call appropriate compiler routines,
written in Pascal.

7.3 The symbol table
Because Pascal identifiers can consist of any number of alphanumeric characters,
rather than the single character allowed in P, two significant difficulties arise. The
first is similar to that noted for literal character strings: that is avoiding wasting
space if lexemes are to be capable of holding the longest possible string. Although,
in principle, the characters forming an identifier could be used directly as the
lexeme value, as in the P compiler, this is clumsy and can waste space. Instead,
some mapping is needed to replace the actual characters of an identifier lexeme
by a smalllexeme value. However, unlike literal strings, identifier names occur
repeatedly. Savings in space can be made if the characters making up each different
name are saved only once, no matter how many times that name occurs in the
source program.
The second, more significant difficulty arises because, as a consequence of
the longer names allowed, a Pascal program can contain many more different
identifiers than can a P program. During the semantic phase of the compiler, the
declaration and uses of each identifier must be matched. It is most important that
the lexeme value can be used to locate that declaration which corresponds to the
identifier. Any search must be fast, as it has to be performed for each occurrence

80

Compiler Engineering using Pascal

of each identifier, and it must be able to cope with the large numbers of identifiers
to be found in the typical Pascal program.
These two difficulties are usually resolved by creating a symbol table during
lexical analysis. This is a data structure which holds all the identifiers and which
can be searched easily to insert a new identifier or match repeated identifiers. It
is sometimes called a namelist. The lexeme values associated with identifiers
now become pointers or indices, giving the location of the actual characters of
the identifier within the symbol table. Although the symbol table is created in
the lexical phase some of the associated information concerning the properties of
identifiers is not available until the semantic phase. For convenience the symbol
table is described here, together with some indication of its use by the semantic
phase, so that the description is complete and in one place. The Pascal program
in figure 7.3 will be used to illustrate the symbol table methods discussed.
PROGRAM my;
VAR x, y, z : inteqer;
PROCEDURE p (VAR x : inteqer);
VAR xx, y : inteqer;
BEGIN END;
PROCEDURE q (VAR n, y
inteqer);
VAR a, b, yy, z : inteqer;
BEGIN END;
BEGIN END.

Figure 7.3

Example Pascal program

The simplest possible data structure would be a linear list. However, as the
search time would be proportional to the number of names present, this would
be inefficient for all but the smallest programs. If the names are organised into a
sorted binary tree data structure, the search time can be reduced to be proportional
to the log of the number of identifiers. This best case relies on the tree being
balanced, that is with all branches of as near as possible the same length. A
compiler using simple binary trees cannot easily maintain this optimum as the tree
is built, so actual performance will be somewhat worse. Nevertheless, this method
is a significant improvement on the simple linear list. An example balanced binary
tree holding the identifiers from the program in figure 7.3 is given in figure 7 .4.
Other forms of tree could be considered, but a better method for creating the
symbol table, which significantly reduces the time for each search, is to use a hashing algorithm. For this method, each string is converted, by a hashing function,
to a number which can be used as an index into an array, the hash table. A simple hashing function would be to add together the ordinal values of the characters
comprising the string and take the remainder on dividing by the table length. The
major problem with this method is that of clashes, when more than one identifier
has the same hash value (the value calculated by the hashing function). Significant effort has been put into designing functions which minimise this problem but

Lexical analysis

81

q

a

/

b

/~

n

Figure 7.4

"'x

/"'x
"'x

X

p

XX

z

Balanced tree representation of symbol table

it can never be eradicated, particularly for large source programs. One solution
is to have a linear list of actual identifiers with the same hash value, pointed at
by the corresponding element in the hash table. This solution is demonstrated in
figure 7.5 for the example program of figure 7 .3. The table in this example is 128
entries long. The hash function is computed by just summing the ordinal values of
the individual characters of the name (assuming the ASCII code). This is a poor
choice of function, as it leads to common clashes such as cc with bd and with db.
It does, however, provide a simple illustration of the solution to clashes, as the
identifiers p and xx have the same hash value. The hash table would be an array
of pointers, with nil indicating an unused location. The list items would include a
pointer to the next item, with nil indicating the end of the list, and either the actual
characters of the name or a pointer to them.
During the semantic phase each symbol table item will be made to point to the
property entry of the corresponding identifier, which contains details of its declaration, as the declaration is encountered. As some identifiers may be declared
in several places, as with the x and yin the example program, the identifiers may
need to point to several sets of properties. This is most simply achieved by linking
all the properties together in a list. For efficiency, the properties of the most recent
declaration are usually placed at the head of the list. This would ensure that the
declaration defined by the scope rules of Pascal as being referenced by subsequent
use would always be found first. For example, for the program in figure 7 .3, when
the semantic analyser was processing the body of procedure p, the symbol table
entry for identifier x would point to the properties declaring it to be a parameter
of p, which would in turn point to those declaring it to be a variable of my.
In a single pass compiler it is possible to discard names on reaching the end
of the procedure in which they were declared. This is" because when the end of
a procedure is recognised all information about the declarations within it can be
discarded. As well as saving space, this may also result in faster searching of
the symbol table, by reducing the length of lists of names with the same hash
value. For example, for the program in figure 7 .3, once the semantic analyser has
recognised the end of procedure p, the identifiers declared within it and nowhere

Lexical analysis

else could be discarded. Here this in fact only applies to xx, as x and y are also
declared in the main body of the program.
Overall, the hashed lists structure is efficient, if only because it can be searched
easily, which must be done for each occurrence of each identifier. The deletion of
names at the end of a procedure, although important, happens much less frequently
and is not sensible unless the analysis phases are merged, and so is of less weight
in any overall assessment. One disadvantage of the hashed lists method is that
the large hash table needed for long programs will be wasteful of space and of
initialisation time when used with small programs.
There are three remaining details. The first is that Pascal has a set of predefined
identifiers, such as integer, real, sqrtand so on, which need to be treated in a similar
way to the user defined identifiers. The predefined identifiers can be thought of
as a set of declarations encountered just before the program heading. They can
therefore be put into the symbol table and property list by the compiler initialisation
as the first set of identifier declarations. These predefined identifiers can then be
overruled by user definitions as required by Pascal. This set does not include the
reserved words, which have already been catered for. It might speed the reserved
word search if they were included in the hash table but this would have to be done
in a way which prevented redefinition by the user.
The second detail is that, if sensible runtime diagnostics are to be generated,
the identifiers and their properties must be saved, in a form suitable to be printed
when the program fails. Thus, as identifiers are deleted from the symbol table,
whether at the end of a procedure or just at the end of semantic analysis, they
should be saved in such a form. The use of the saved information will be described
in chapter 15.
The final detail concerns the storage of names in the symbol table. At the start
of this section, it was concluded that it was not sensible to store the characters of
names directly in identifier lexeme values. Similarly, it can be a waste of space
to store the names directly in the symbol table, in the lists of identifier entries.
Instead, the symbol table entries contain pointers to the characters, which will
be held in a single amorphous data structure. However, because Pascal does not
handle variable length strings gracefully, it is difficult to implement these pointers directly. Instead, the 'pointer' will usually be the index number of the first
character of the identifier in a large character array. It is also important to note
the length of the identifier string. This could be done by another integer, either at
the start of the string or in the symbol table entry, or by a special character stored
at the end of each name string. As an alternative to using these pointer substitutes, at the expense of implementing a non-standard version of Pascal, it might
be decided to truncate the identifiers to some maximum length. Thus, if only some
small number of characters is used to hold each name, this might occupy a similar
space to the pointer substitutes and therefore be held directly in the symbol table
entries, simplifying and compacting the data structures. In fact early versions of
the Pascal language did restrict the number of significant characters in an identi-

84

Compiler Engineering using Pascal

fier in this way. With the present language standard the restriction on Pascal is
unreasonable, unless the implementation would otherwise be impossible because
of limited memory space, such as on some microcomputers.

7.4 Problems
7.1 An alternative method of dealing with clashes in a hash table is to place the
clashing entry in the next free space in the table. Create the equivalent of the
hash table of figure 7.5 using this method. What is the maximum number of
entries in the table that must be tested to ensure that a particular name is not
present? What happens if the table becomes full? What happens if names
are deleted at the end of a procedure?
7.2 Modify the specification of P to allow multicharacter identifiers. Implement
the change using a hash addressed symbol table. The change should be
implemented in a way which minimises alterations to other parts of the P
compiler. The identifiers should be placed in the table in the lexical phase
and subsequent use of the table should be through the declid and getid procedures.

8 Syntax analysis
In chapter 6 the grammar of the P language was formally defined. It was shown
that a context free grammar was needed to treat bracketed subexpressions, and in
fact nested blocks, correctly. Pascal is defined in the Pascal report using a context
free grammar expressed in PBNF. Once the grammar of a language is formally
defined there are various algorithms which can be used to parse programs written
in the language. The recursive descent method used in chapters 3 and 4 is just
one of these. A property of context free grammars is that programs can be parsed
using an algorithm with a stack mechanism, formally equivalent to the push down
automaton mentioned in chapter 6.

8.1 Syntax analysis methods
The parsing methods fall into two classes, top down and bottom up. Top down
methods start by trying to recognise the distinguished symbol, usually program.
Recognition is performed by successive expansions, replacing non-terminal symbols by the right-hand sides of the corresponding rules until a terminal symbol is
obtained, which should be the same as the current input lexeme. The main difficulty is deciding which alternative, or how many instances of a repeated string to
use. When the current terminal symbol has been recognised as the current input
lexeme, both can be discarded and parsing continued with the next symbol and
lexeme.
An example of top down parsing is recursive descent, as outlined in chapter 3.
This method has one procedure for each non-terminal symbol, which checks correctness of input against syntax, calling other procedures (possibly recursively)
when further non-terminal symbols are referenced. This involves an implicit stack
being built from procedure activations. An iterative, table driven, equivalent to
this recursive method exists which explicitly uses a stack. The problem of which
alternative expansion to use can be solved by looking ahead in the input; it is then
helpful if the syntax is defined so as to make this as simple as possible. Methods which look ahead are known as predictive methods, since the parser predicts
which alternative to choose by examining the next few lexemes of input.
Pascal is defined so that the compiler need only look one lexeme ahead to be

85

Compiler Engineering using Pascal

86

able to decide on the correct alternative. In fact there are a few small difficulties
with the syntax of Pascal given in the report. For example, one problem is to
distinguish a procedure-statement from an assignment-statement when both start
with an identifier. This is easily circumvented by a small rearrangement of the
syntax rules.
Grammars which can be parsed top-down with n symbols of lookahead are
called LL(n) grammars. 'n' is the maximum number of input lexemes that have to
be looked at, including the current lexeme, to decide which expansion of a nonterminal symbol to use. A grammar where only one symbollookahead is required
is called LL(l). A form of grammar which is easy to define, and which satisfies the
LL(l) condition, is the simpleLL(l) grammar, or s-grammar. This is a context free
grammar such that for every non-terminal A in VN no two alternatives start with
the same terminal symbol. Also, no alternative is just empty with no symbols.
Another way of regarding this is that the sets of first symbols from Vr, for the
different alternatives of A, are disjoint. The top-down parsers described here are
suitable for LL(l) grammars.
LL is so called because it scans input left to right, and constructs a left-most
derivation. That is, the left-most non-terminal is always expanded first. For example, given the syntax:
E=T"+" T.
T=F"*"F.
F= id.
the expression:
a*b+c*d
is parsed by applying the grammar rules in the following order:
E

=> T "+" T
=> F "*" F "+" T
=> id "*" F "+" T
=> id "*" id "+" T
=> id "*" id "+" F "*" F
=> id "*" id "+" id "*" F
=> id "*" id "+" id "*" id

It is assumed here that the lexical analyser has reduced each of a, b, c and d to
an id. A corresponding parse tree can be generated and is shown in figure 8.1,
with the numbers in brackets indicating the order in which the rules generating the
nodes are applied. The numbering confirms that this is a left-most derivation.
Examining this grammar and example parse reveals that the language was so
restricted that no choice was possible and consequently no lookahead was needed.
A more realistic language would allow any number of factors and terms in an
expression. The presence of"+" or"*" as the next input lexeme should then be

used to guide the parse to apply the correct rules. This technique is implicit in the
P compiler code of figure 4.9, and will also be used in the examples of sections 8.2
and 8.3.
Instead of looking ahead, a different approach, suitable for grammars which do
not display the LL(n) property, is to explore each alternative in turn, backtracking
after each incorrect expansion. Although the backtracking algorithms accept a
wider class of grammars they are harder to implement and sometimes inefficient.
They are not considered further in this book.
The other major class of parsing methods, bottom-up or shift reduce parsing,
proceeds by searching the input for right-hand sides of rules and successively
reducing them by replacing them by the corresponding non-terminal symbols from
the left-hand side. The normal action of such a parser is to shift input lexemes onto
a stack until a reduction is possible, hence the name 'shift reduce'. Implementation
of these methods is often based on the properties of LR(n) grammars described
later in this chapter. 'n' is again defined as the number of symbols lookahead
required, but in this case beyond the right-hand end of the string being reduced.
Only LR(l) will be discussed here. LR is so called because it scans input left
to right, and generates a right-most derivation, in reverse. That is, the left-most
sequence of symbols which make up the right-hand side of a rule is replaced by
the left-hand side first. For example, given the same trivial expression syntax as
in the previous example, the expression:
a*b+c*d
is parsed by applying the grammar rules in the following order:

Algorithms for LL(1) parsers may be programmed recursive descent or table
driven while those for LR(1) parsers are always table driven. The tables are automatically generated from the particular syntax to be recognised.

8.2

Creation of an LL(l) recursive descent parser

It is quite easy, given the syntax of a language in BNF or as syntax diagrams, to

create a recursive descent parser. This can be done by hand or automatically using
straightforward rules.
The following set of rules can be applied to a set of syntax graphs to produce
a corresponding set of procedures. These procedures can then be used as part
of a complete program with suitable declarations, lexical analyser and semantic
analyser added. The rules give the procedure bodies. Each procedure name is the
name of the corresponding syntax rule or non-terminal being defined.
Rule 1: Translate a sequence of elements:
-S1-S2-S3- ... -S,.into the compound statement:
begin T(S 1); T(S2); T(S3); ..... ; T(S,.) end
where T(S) is the translation of elements S, given by these rules.
Rule 2: Translate a set of alternatives:

Consider the language defined by the syntax graph in figure 8.3. This language
is a small part of the P language. The rule for program has been rewritten to
avoid the empty subalternative. After this revision the grammar is an s-grammar
and inspection of the symbols which can possibly start each alternative confirms

8.3 Table driven LL(l) parsing
LL(l) parsers do not have to be recursive descent, they can equally well be table
driven. A top down table driven method will now be described. The iterative
algorithm presented, which uses an explicit stack, is directly equivalent to the
recursive descent method.
For table driven methods, it is most convenient to work from the rules rather
than the syntax charts of figure 8.3, and these are shown in the original PBNF
form in figure 8.5. The method described here requires that all repeated groups
are expressed using recursion instead of { } or [ ] brackets. Square brackets, which
indicate zero or one occurrence of the contents, are replaced by using a rule with
an empty alternative. Curly brackets, which indicate zero or more occurrences
of the contents, are replaced by using recursion. A decision must be made as to
whether a rule like that for 'expression' should be expressed recursively by:
expression = term I expression addop term .
or by:
expression

=term I term addop expression .

In general, the first form, known as left recursion, is best suited for bottom-up
parsing, and the second form, known as tail or right recursion, is best suited for
top-down parsing. The rules corresponding to the syntax given in figure 8.5 are
as given in figure 8.6. The rule for program has been changed into two rules in
order to remove the need for square brackets to represent optional items. The rules
for idlist and block have been replaced by two rules to remove the need for { and
} brackets. The additional rules introduced for roidlist and roblock use recursion
to achieve the 'any number of times' effect. In making these changes care has to
be taken to avoid destroying the LL(l) property. This requires that the rules for
roidlistand roblockare given in the tail recursive form. The rules are numbered
with every alternative being treated as a separate rule.
The LL(l) parsing table is shown in figure 8.7. This table guides the parse by
showing which rule is to be applied at each step of the process. The rule to be

end;
if lexeme = ";" then getlexeme else error;
end;
begin
getlexeme; program;
end.

LL(J) parsing table

applied depends on the current goal, shown down the left-hand side of the table,
and the next input lexeme, shown along the top of the table. The table may be
constructed automatically from the syntax by considering each grammar rule in
turn. The left-hand side of the rule determines the row in the table. For each
possible right-hand side alternative the rule number is entered under those input
lexemes which are in the corresponding first set. The existence of rules with blank
right-hand sides means that the grammar chosen is not an s-grammar. Changes
similar to the revised bead chart for program could have achieved this, but it is
not really necessary. Instead these rule numbers are entered under those lexemes
which can follow an application of the rule. So, for example, when searching for
a phead, 'VAR' can start rule 2 while 'BEGIN' and'S' can follow rule 3. Blank
entries correspond to occurrences of lexemes at illegal points in the input. If, in
constructing the table, any position needs more than one entry the syntax is not
LL(1). The rules for phead are an example of the use of lookahead. If 'VAR'
appears in the input the first alternative of 'phead' (rule 2) is chosen, otherwise
the second, in this case empty, alternative (rule 3) is applied.

8.4 Table driven LR(l) parsing
The most commonly used bottom up parser is the LR(1) parser. This is a state machine algorithm based on the idea of a push down automaton. It moves between
a number of states as the parse proceeds, stacking and unstacking information as
required. Information in its stack (or, equivalently, its current state) tells what rule,
or rules, it is trying to evaluate, and therefore what next input lexemes are legal.
The parser uses the combination of its current state and the next input lexeme to
decide whether to shift the input lexeme onto the stack, or to reduce some items
already on the stack to the equivalent left-hand side of a rule, or to finish the parse.
This decision is encoded in a parse table called the action table. After each action
the current state and top of stack item are used to decide which state to move to
next; this information is given in the goto table.
Consider again the grammar for the trivial P subset. This is shown in figure 8.12 in a slightly revised form using left instead of tail recursion. In figure 8.11
a pair of tables for this grammar is shown. The description SLR(l) means simple
LR(l) and relates to the way the tables are constructed. This will be discussed

LR(l) parsing algorithm

Compiler Engineering using Pascal

shortly. The first table is an action table, in which the action to be taken next depends on the current 'state' and the next input lexeme. The meaning of the entries
in the table is as follows. A means 'Accept' or stop the parse after successful
recognition of a program. S means 'Shift' or take the first lexeme from the input
and put in on the stack. An integer means 'reduce' using the rule of this number,
i.e. take symbols, and states, off the stack corresponding to the right-hand side
of the rule. Then, resuming the state exposed on the top of the stack, stack the
left-hand side of the rule. The second table is the goto table which is used to show
which state to move to depending on the current state and the stacked item. In both
tables blank entries represent errors. In the tables, and in figures 8.14 and 8.15,
the names of non-terminal symbols have been reduced to a few characters, and
terminal symbols to their initial character, in order to save space. $END, in the
tables, represents the end of input. The LR parsing algorithm uses these tables in
a simple way repeatedly performing an action and then moving to a new state, as
shown in figure 8.13.
Figure 8.14 shows the steps in parsing the program:
VARA; BEGINS; SEND.
In this figure a shift and the subsequent goto are shown as a single step, whereas
the goto after a reduce is shown as a separate step. This enables the states resumed
from the stack after a reduce action to be shown, marked with a* . Figure 8.15
shows the parse of a faulty program which terminates very quickly when a blank
table entry is found.
Construction of SLR parser tables
There are various ways of constructing tables for use by LR(1) parsers. The simplest, the SLR method, will be described here. It relies on first constructing a deterministic finite state automaton (DFA) which will recognise what are known as
viable prefixes of the grammar. The DFA states are shown appended to the grammar rules in figure 8.16. These states are known as LR(O) states; the 0 derives
from the fact that no lookahead is involved in their construction. The construction
of the states is similar in principle to that for the regular expression in section 6.4
but must allow for the greater generality of a context free grammar. An algorithm
is as follows:
o Add an additional rule (in this example program' =program) with the dis-

tinguished symbol on the right-hand side.
o Assign state 0 as the starting state and state 1 as the final terminating state

in the additional rule.
o Wherever a non-terminal appears on the right-hand side of a rule the state

preceding the non-terminal is also applied to the rule for the non-terminal.
Thus since starting to recognise program' is state 0, starting to recognise
program is also state 0. In fact the closure of the first set of non-terminals

Detenninistic finite state automaton

Syntax analysis

101

must be computed. In this example this results in phead also being added
to state 0.
o Take a rule which already has a starting state. Move over the right-hand
side of the rule, one symbol at a time, numbering each unnumbered state.
At each stage take the closure of any non-terminals. This can result in the
start of the application of some rules corresponding to several states, for
example 2, 13 and 17 for block.
o For a given starting state and a given grammar symbol the resulting state is
always the same. Therefore when assigning each state check for an identical
symbol in the closure. Where such a symbol occurs assign the same resulting
state. Hence, for example, the state after roidlist, in the recursive rule for
roidlist, is determined from the rule for idlist, in other words state 7 followed
by roidlistalways results in state 8.
o Repeat the previous two steps until all rules have been dealt with.
Figure 8.17 shows the resulting DFA. It represents part of the information in
the parse tables. It can be used to recognise sentences up to the point when a
reduction is required but does not show how to make a reduction or what state to
move to on reduction. It represents the 'shift' and 'goto' parts of the LR parser
which do not need to use the information on the stack. A push down automaton,
using the stack, is essential for the reduction part of the parsing.
The parsing tables, i.e. the action and goto tables, are constructed as follows:
Construct the states for the DFA, technically the collection of sets of LR(O)
items for the grammar, as outlined above.
o For every instance of a state followed by a terminal symbol set the action
to shift and the goto to the new state.
o For every instance of a state followed by a non-terminal symbol set the goto
to the new state.
o For every state at the end of a rule set the action to reduce by placing the
rule number in the table in every column where the symbol at the top of the
column is in the follow set of that rule.
o Set the action on state 1 and end of input to accept; accept could be set on
any input if leaving some input unread is not regarded as an error.
o Treat all blank entries as error states.
o

If filling in the reduce and goto entries in the tables produces two entries for the
same position the grammar is not SLR(1) and the parser cannot be created using
this method.
SLR(1) is a rather restricted method of table construction; more general methods are the LALR or lookahead LR grammar method and the canonical LR method.
Because the SLR method does not use any lookahead in constructing the DFA, it
sometimes leaves insufficient information to decide whether a shift or a reduce
is needed and this results in conflicts in the table. The LALR and canonical LR

102

Compiler Engineering using Pascal

methods overcome this by constructing a different automaton based on so-called
LR(1) states. SLR grammars have the advantage that the parsing tables are easy to
construct and relatively small. LALR tables are harder to construct but the same
size as SLR. Canonical LR tables are much larger. As SLR grammars are rather
restricted, but LALR grammars are almost as general as LR grammars, the LALR
method is generally preferred. Note that the parsing algorithm, LR(1), is the same
in all cases, only the method of table construction and the size of the resulting
tables varies.
Even the relatively small SLR or LALR tables may have a few hundred states
and up to a hundred possible input lexemes for typical Pascal-like languages. This
gives tables of the order of 10,000 elements each but with many duplicate and
blank entries. There is considerable scope for compaction of the tables if memory
space is likely to be a problem.

8.5 An automatically generated syntactic analyser for P
In this section the use of a program which generates syntactic analysers automatically, from a description of the language grammar, is described. As shown in
sections 8.3 and 8.4, these analysers usually consist of a standard stack-based algorithm driven by data tables specific to the particular problem. One such analysergenerator system is YACC, which is available with UNIX and compatible with
the lexical system LEX described in section 7.2. YACC uses the LALR table
construction method, mentioned above, and a standard LR(1) parsing algorithm.
This section will develop a syntactic analyser for the P language using the YACC
system. YACC has several features that are unnecessary complications in this
context, and most of these will be ignored in the following discussion. For a full
description of YACC, see [Johnson75].
As the major input to the YACC system is a description of the grammar of
the language in question, in this case P, it is necessary to convert it into a suitable
form. The best starting point is the PBNF syntax given earlier in figure 6.1. From
this the rules already dealt with by LEX (addop, mulop, relop, identifier, constant,
comments and blanks) are deleted.
The next step is to rewrite the rules in order to avoid any need for bracketing,
in a similar way to the rewriting described in section 8.3, as the YACC notation
does not permit brackets. As YACC is based around an LR(1) parsing algorithm,
which is bottom-up, left recursion will be used. The results of the transformations
outlined are given in figure 8.18.
The final step is to rewrite the rules using YACC notation, which is a variant
of BNF different from the one used in the Pascal standard, as given in figure 8.19.
The major difference between this figure and the previous one is that the actual
character strings in double quotes have been replaced by the names of the lexemes
that will be returned by the LEX version of the lexical analyser. Other minor

Although this is a complete YACC description of the syntax of P, all that the
