programs is discussed in section 8.6.
It is also necessary to include sufficient extra code in the generated parser for
any subsequent compiler phases to be performed. Such code can be included by
inserting it, in curly brackets, in any convenient positions in the right-hand sides of
the YACC syntax rules. For example, to call a version of the semantic procedure
declid used in the recursive descent compiler, passing it information about each
identifier declared:

where '$n' indicates the use of some value which describes the n'th symbol in the

that rule, the terminal symbol identifier, and '$3' in the second line refers to the
third symbol in that rule, again an identifier. The value of a terminal symbol is
in fact the lexeme value placed in yylval by the lexical analyser, as described in
section 7 .2. The value of a non-terminal symbol can be set by assigning to '$$' in
an action:

in this instance giving idlist the same value as ids, whatever that is. This 'syntactic'
value is in fact placed in a variable yyval, declared to be of the same type as
yylval. The final value of yyval is returned to the following compiler phases by
the function callparse mentioned in section 7 .2.
It is possible to embed calls on the semantic and code generation procedures
directly into the YACC actions, so that in some ways they would look very similar
to the syntactic analysis routines used in the recursive descent P compiler. However, to illustrate as many different features of compilers as possible, a two-pass
compiler has been written, with the YACC actions building a single parse tree.
The subsequent compiler phases, written in Pascal, operate on this parse tree.
The tree building routines used by the parser, also written in Pascal, are makelexemenode and makenode. Makelexemenode is used to create leaf nodes of the
tree. Makenode is used to create inner nodes of the tree, given pointers to several
other nodes. When makenode is used to make a list it naturally adds items to the
head of the list thereby creating it in reverse order. This does not matter when
creating a list of all the identifiers declared. However the list of statements in a
block must be correctly ordered so makenode cannot be used. Two other routines,
startlist and makelist, which respectively initialise the head of a list and add the
next item to the tail of the list, are used to create an ordered list. The text for these
routines, and associated routines which print the tree and generate code from it, is
given in chapter 10.
The set of syntax rules and actions so far described form the middle third of a
complete YACC input, as shown in figure 8.20. As with LEX, the three parts of the
input are separated by lines consisting solely of'%%'. The extra code at the top
contains all the declarations, including those for various Pascal-compatible objects.
These include NULL, corresponding to the pointer nil, the declaration of nodetype
for values corresponding to non-terminal symbols (trees) and lexemevaltype for
terminal symbols or lexemes, and the various Pascal routines used in the actions.
The line:
%start program
informs YACC that the objective for the generated parser is ultimately to recognise
input corresponding to the rule for program. The four lines starting with •%union'
inform YACC of the variant record type for yyval and yylval. The lines starting
with '%token' inform YACC of the names of the LEX lexemes and declare the
type of the corresponding yylval returned, if any. The lines starting with '%type'
declare the type of the yyval generated by the corresponding syntactic rule, if any.
Like LEX, YACC is orientated primarily to compilers written inC so the top
and bottom thirds of the YACC input are in fact C code. To be consistent with
the objective of writing the compiler using Pascal the syntactic action routines are
written in Pascal with the C code being restricted to a call of the corresponding
Pascal procedure.

YACC parser for P language

A summary of the organisation of the creation of the table driven compiler is given
in figure 8.21. The compiler comprises three parts, input to LEX, input to YACC
and Pascal code. The input to LEX is processed using LEX and the resulting tables,
together with the standard interpreter, compiled using the C compiler. Similarly
the YACC input is processed and compiled. The compiled code from the three
parts must then be linked together to form the complete compiler.

8.6 Syntax error recovery
In all the parsing methods the detection of the first syntax error is straightforward.
Deciding how to recover and give the user information about further errors without
introducing spurious error messages is more difficult. A variety of techniques is
used. In top down methods it is possible to introduce error recovery routines which
skip over unwanted input lexemes and resume the parse at an appropriate point.
Ideally the contents of the stack, whether implicit or explicit, will be adjusted to
reflect the new goals when the parse is resumed, otherwise the mismatch between
the goals on the stack and the input will cause spurious error messages to be
generated. This sort of technique was used in section 4.2. In bottom up methods
the equivalent action would be to discard some of the information on the stack
and also, when necessary, some input lexemes. Even with the best possible error
recovery certain user mistakes, such as missing out a BEGIN, often result in a
large number of unavoidable error messages. In the case of the missing BEGIN,

Syntax analysis

Creation of the table driven P compiler

if the corresponding END is matched to the previous unmatched BEGIN, many of
the identifiers in the remainder of the program may appear to be undeclared.
It is possible to put some of this error recovery on a more formal basis by modifying the syntax. Additional syntax rules are introduced, either by the user or automatically, to allow incorrect programs to be parsed with suitable error messages
being produced as the parse proceeds. For example, the routine block in figure 4.9
could be thought of as being derived from the syntax chart in figure 8.22. The error
nodes discard input until one of the set of possible follow lexemes is encountered
(eg. BEGIN, END, semicolon, the start of a statement, or whatever may follow
the block). The error nodes use contextual information, the set of follow lexemes,
to determine how much input to discard, so the syntax diagram of figure 8.22 is
not strictly context free in the treatment of the error nodes.
Similar modifications can be made to the table driven methods. For example, a simple technique is for the blank entries in tables corresponding to error
conditions to be replaced by indications of error messages and the recovery to be
attempted. To modify the syntax it is necessary to add extra incorrect alternatives
to each syntax rule, with corresponding error messages and recovery actions. This
can be quite difficult for a non-trivial grammar. Attempting this in conjunction
with standard YACC requires significant knowledge of the internal workings of
YACC. However, variants of YACC have been produced, which generate parsers

Compiler Engineering using Pascal

110

block

BEGIN

END

Figure 8.22

Syntax with error recovery paths

which automatically deal with most errors. In particular, such parsers will attempt

to detect all the errors in the input program, output suitable error messages and
perform recovery.

letter identifiers. Bracketed subexpressions may be nested to any depth. An
example of the sort of expression to be allowed is:

a and( bore and not( a ore))

these bead diagrams.
8.4 Convert the PBNF syntax to a form without [ ] and { } bracketing. Write
down both a tail recursive form and a left recursive form.
8.5 Write down a top down derivation of the expression:
(a orb) andc

Base the derivation on the tail recursive syntax of problem 8.4. Draw the

8.6 Repeat the previous question for a bottom up derivation based on the left

9 Semantic analysis
Program text in most languages can be split into two distinct parts. One part comprises declaratives while the other consists of statements. In Pascal, the declaratives are textually separate from the statements, appearing as the first part of the
program block and of procedure blocks. The declaratives comprise definitions, of
types and constants, and declarations, of labels, variables, procedures and functions. It is unnecessary, in a Pascal compiler, to distinguish rigorously between
definitions and declarations because, for example, a variable declaration may include type definitions. The declaratives supply information to the compiler about
the objects which are used and manipulated by the statements. They will normally
be processed before statements involving the same objects. This is made easier by
the Pascal rule that the defining occurrence of an identifier must ordinarily precede
any applied occurrence.

9.1 Declarative processing
Declarative processing must include checks for some semantic errors, such as ensuring that identifiers are not declared more than once in each block. This is the
Pascal equivalent of the rule, in P, that no identifier is declared twice. However,
because Pascal contains many more sorts of identifiers than P, there must also be
checks for consistent usage even within the declarations. For example, constants
and types can be used in declarations of variables and other types. The semantic
analyser must therefore save details from the declarations to check further declarations and statements. During this phase, as each declaration is encountered, the
corresponding identifier in the symbol table will be made to point to a property
entry containing details of the declaration. This information includes the class of
identifier (constant, type, variable, procedure and so on), and details of its declaration, such as the type of a variable or the value of a constant. Further information,
such as the location of variables and procedures is added during code generation.
The property list entries are described in section 9.2 below. As some identifiers
may be declared in several places, the symbol table entries may in fact point to a
list of property entries, with the entry for the most recent declaration at the head.
These property entries need to be manipulated as new declarations are encountered

111

Compiler Engineering using Pascal

112

or when procedures are finished with and the corresponding declarations become
redundant. Similar manipulations may be used when record variables are encountered, such as in a with statement, whereupon the fields of the record effectively
become the most recent declarations of the corresponding identifiers. Further manipulation of the property list may be necessary if, instead of Pascal's convention
of identifiers being automatically available non-locally, identifiers are explicitly
made available. This happens in Euclid. In chapter 16 a similar problem, the explicit export and import of identifiers between modules in Modula-2, is described
and a solution outlined.
It is necessary to associate with each declaration some indication of the procedure (or function or program) within which it is declared. This could be a pointer
to the properties of the owning procedure but instead an integer, known as the static
level or textual level is used. The static level is useful because it is needed during
code generation, when accesses are made to non-local variables. It is simply the
number of procedures, or equivalent, textually surrounding the declaration. Thus,
PROGRAM my;
VAR x, y, z : integer;
PROCEDURE p (VAR x : integer);
VAR xx, y : integer;
BEGIN END;
PROCEDURE q (VAR n, y
integer);
VAR a, b, yy, z : integer;
BEGIN END;

BEGIN END.

Figure 9.1

Example program showing levels ofproperties

in figure 9.1, x, y and z are all at Ievell, inside program my. Within procedure p,
at level 2, are x and y again and xx. Within procedure q, also at level 2, are n, y.
a, b, yy and z. If a procedure had been declared within p or q, its variables would
have been at level 3. By convention, the identifiers for procedures p and q are
considered as being at static level 1 and for program my to be at level 0. At any
given point in the text of the program there is only one set of declarations currently
in use for each static level and so the level is a unique identification. For example,
when procedure p has been dealt with and procedure q started, the variables within
pat level2 are no longer relevant, and the declarations within procedure q become
the identifiers at level 2. This exactly corresponds to removing the property entries for the declarations within p when the analyser has recognised the end of the
procedure.
On starting the compilation, the property list must be initialised to contain only
entries for the predefined identifiers, such as integer, sqrt and write, which are
regarded as being at level zero. As each procedure or equivalent is encountered,
the semantic analyser must calculate the new static level. Subsequently, as each
identifier is encountered, the semantic analyser must decide whether it is being

Semantic analy!fis

113

declared or used, usually after having been previously declared.
If the identifier is being declared, the symbol table is searched, and the new
properties added, after checking that any existing properties do not represent a
previous declaration within the same static level. If the identifier is being used, the
symbol table is searched, and the property entry corresponding to its most recent
declaration located. If a property entry is not found, for Pascal this would usually
be an error, and a suitable message would be generated. For other languages such
as FORTRAN, and in some special cases in Pascal which are described below, this
might not be an error. Whether or not an error has been detected, the most suitable
action when a declaration is found to be missing is usually to insert a dummy
declaration into the property list, either to satisfy the language requirements, or
just to inhibit future occurrences of the same error message.
In Pascal, there are five different forms of declarative. Because of the details of
the semantics of Pascal, the processing of each form of declarative differs slightly
from the overall outline above. One major difference is that, strictly speaking, all
the declarations for a particular static level must be processed before any attempt
is made to use declarations from surrounding levels.
const

=

n
10;
procedure p;

var
m: 1 .. n;

n: integer;

Figure 9.2

An example of an mega! Pascal declaration

Thus, for example, in the Pascal fragment in figure 9.2, the variable m is declared incorrectly, as then it uses must be taken to refer to the variable declaration
in the same static level, the integer n , rather than the constant declaration in the
previous level. Such a case, where a declaration contains an apparent forward
reference to another declaration, is always a semantic error, except in the special
case of pointer types, which will be discussed later. In practice, to process declarations using an extra pass, merely to detect such errors, would be very awkward.
Instead, it is usual to assume that subsequent declarations in the same level cannot
upset things, but retain sufficient extra information for suitable error messages to
be generated if necessary. Because several levels can be involved, this extra information is most simply represented by generating empty declarations at the current
level, which will block any further declarations of the same name at that level.
One further oddity in the semantics of Pascal must be dealt with here. It is that
an identifier that is a parameter can be redeclared within the procedure block even
though this makes the parameter unusable. It will become clear in chapter 12 that
it is convenient for the compiler to treat the formal parameter list and the block

114

Compiler Engineering using Pascal

declarations as being at the same level even though Pascal treats these as separate
levels. Therefore, each time a new declaration is made and the symbol table and
property list are examined for a previous declaration at the same level, it is not an
error if a previous declaration of a parameter at the current level is found when a
non-parameter is being declared.
Each form of Pascal declaration and definition is now considered in more detail.

Label declaration
Label numbers will have been treated as constants by the lexical analyser, and
thus are not in any symbol table. The semantic phase must note that the relevant
integers are labels and associate them with the properties of the current static level.
One method for doing this is to have a list of label numbers and property entries
pointed at from the properties of the owning procedure. A check for redefinition is
made against existing labels for that level. The compiler will not know the correct
address of the label. This information can only be completed when the labelled
statement is met during code generation.

Constant definition
The information to be recorded for a constant is very simple. The constant identifier has an associated type, for example integer, real, set, or string, and a corresponding value.

Type definition
As well as noting in the property entry that the identifier represents a type, it must
be associated with further information describing the structure of the type. However, type definitions (and variable declarations) may include an indeterminate
amount of information, equivalent to several simpler type definitions. For example, a type definition of a two dimensional array is equivalent to two definitions,
each of a one dimensional array type. The first would involve the first index,
with each element being of the second type, which would use the second index.
Furthermore, each index defined by a range of values is equivalent to a definition
of a subrange type. It should also be noted that each of these additional implicit
type definitions would be of an anonymous type, not directly linked to any user
defined identifier. Therefore, the information describing the structure of the type
must be held separately in groups of type entries, linked to the property entry for
the identifier. The type entries contain information about the form of the type,
for example whether subrange, enumerated, (packed) array or record. For some
types further details are needed, such as the index type and the element type for
an array.

Semantic analysis

115

The processing of a record type will involve processing the field definitions,
during which the displacements of fields within the record are determined in a
similar way to the displacements of variables, as described below. Thus the total
size of the record can be evaluated. The description of the type is held in one or
more property entries. Because field identifiers are only 'visible' when the owning
records are being used, each record type can have fields with the same names as
any other identifiers, including fields of other records. However, no two fields at
the same level inside a single record definition may have the same name, although
fields at different levels (e.g. within sub-records) may. Several methods have
been proposed for dealing with his problem, including generating separate symbol
tables for the fields of each record definition. However, such a solution requires
that extra information be available when the symbol table is being built. As this
information would naturally be obtained from the syntactic or semantic phases, it
is not necessarily available during the lexical phase, when many compilers create
their symbol tables. The method that is used here is to insert all identifiers in the
same symbol table and permit many different property entries to be generated for
each identifier. In particular, several property entries describing fields of records,
all declared in the same level, can be attached to a single identifier, as long as the
semantic rules outlined above are enforced. The correct field for any individual
record type can always be found by making the property entry of each field point
back to the entry for its owning record type. Thus, in an executable statement,
when an identifier is used as a field name, instead of the properties corresponding
to the most recent declaration being located, it is necessary to follow the list of
properties of the name until a field definition is found which points to the correct
owning record type. In particular, in the scope of a with statement, a list of all the
record types mentioned between the with and the do must be kept. This is so that
the list of property entries for each subsequent identifier can be searched for a field
definition within one of these record types. If such a field definition is not found,
only then do the semantics of Pascal allow the most recent (non-field) definition
to be used.
A further complication comes from the semantics of the definition of pointer
types and variables. Because of the problems of declaring pointers in dynamic
data structures such as lists and trees, a pointer type can be defined as a forward
reference to a named type that has not been declared yet. An example of such
type
p = lx;
X

""

0 .. 100;

Figure 9.3

Example of a forward reference in a Pascal type definition

a definition is given in figure 9.3, where the type that p points to, x, is not defined until later. When such a type is subsequently declared, the property entry
corresponding to the pointer type must be linked to the new property entry. The

116

Compiler Engineering using Pascal

simplest method for dealing with this is: when the pointer type is declared, if the
named type that it points at is defined in the current level, then the new pointer
property entry can be linked correctly. If such a named type does not exist, then the
pointer is possibly a forward reference. In this case, a dummy type entry should be
generated, which can be subsequently overwritten when its definition is encountered at the same level. If a subsequent definition is not found by the end of the
type definitions, the property entries for surrounding levels can be checked. If a
suitable entry is found, then the pointer entry can be set pointing to that property
entry and the dummy entry discarded, otherwise a fault is reported.

Variable and parameter declarations
The type of the variable must be associated with the identifier. A position for the
variable must be allocated in the area set aside for the particular procedure. The
displacement of the identifier from the start of the area must be remembered and
the total space used so far updated by the size of variable allocated. Parameter
declarations are treated similarly, but with an indication in each property entry of
whether the corresponding parameter is a varparameter.

Program, procedure and function declaration
The class of identifier, program, procedure or function, must be associated with the
identifier. The number and types of parameters and the result type of a function
must be stored in the property list, with the actual starting address being added
during code generation.
The property list is manipulated at the start and end of each such declaration.
A new static level must be created before the parameter list and any declarations
within the procedure declaration are processed. This new level and all the declarations in this level, except the parameter list, must be removed at the 'end'
statement matching the procedure 'begin'. It is possible to link all the properties
at the same level together, pointed at by the owning procedure, to speed up this
deletion. After all the declarations inside a procedure have been processed, there
must be a check for objects which have not been fully declared, such as 'forward'
declared procedures and functions, and types used to declare pointers. At the end
of the procedure itself, there must be a check for any undefined labels.

9.2 The property list
When a hash addressed symbol table as described in chapter 7 is used, each identifier points at a list of associated property entries, with one entry for each declaration
of the identifier. As mentioned above, to help remove sets of properties at the end
of procedures, the property entry for a procedure could be made to point to the

Semantic analysis

117

entries for all the declarations within it. Also, to be able to find the enclosing procedure when a procedure is finished with, each procedures properties should point
to the procedure within which it is declared. To be able to generate run time diagnostic information, property entries should also point back to the corresponding
identifiers in the symbol table. Alternatively, instead of using these housekeeping
links, the symbol table can be partitioned between procedures, so that at the end
of each procedure the partition and its associated properties can be more easily
processed. However, as already noted in section 7 .3, this would greatly increase
the processing time for each identifier and so is not used here. To illustrate the
use of these housekeeping links, the property list entries for the program fragment
in figure 9.4 are given in figure 9.5. The details of the actual properties are not
shown, merely the way in which the housekeeping links relate one set of properties
to others.
program myprog;
var d, a : integer;
procedure p;
const max
10;
type x = l .. max;
var b: integer;
a: array [x] of integer;
begin
{property list shown at this point}
end;

of two main types of components; the property entries, which describe each identifier, and the type entries, which describe the types either explicitly or implicitly
declared. The label entries are used to hold label declarations, as mentioned above.
To illustrate the use of property and type entries for some typical declarations, the
program fragment in figure 9.4 would give rise to the property and type entries in
figure 9.7. The housekeeping links have been omitted for clarity. The full property list would consist of figures 9.5 and 9.7 superimposed. Symbol table entries
appear on the left, followed by the corresponding property entries. Type entries
appear to the right of figure 9.7. The class field differentiates between classes of
identifiers in the property entries, and the form field differentiates between forms
of type entries. The level field contains the static level of the declaration, and the
Ttype field contains a link from the property entry to its corresponding type entry.
Many of the other fields of the entries are used straightforwardly, as illustrated in
figure 9.7. The more unusual fields are described below.

the same name in the current level.

The conformant form of type entry is used for conformant array parameters, as

9.3 Statement processing
The exact form of statement processing depends on the way in which information
is passed to the code generation phase. If an explicit parse tree is used, perhaps
because the compiler consists of more than one pass or contains global code optimisation, then the semantic analyser modifies the trees. If, on the other hand,
the various phases communicate informally within a single pass, as for the recursive descent P compiler, then the semantic analyser just has to make information
available within each parser procedure. In either case, semantic checks have to be
performed before code generation.
The fundamental semantic check is that every identifier must be declared and
must be a variable, parameter, function, procedure or constant. A similar check
must be made for label numbers in goto statements. Each class of identifier is only
allowed in certain circumstances. For example, procedure names cannot appear
within expressions, constants cannot be assigned to and so on.
After the properties of the identifier have been located and the class checked,
further consistency checks must be made. These can be divided into several cases.
The strictest case is for var parameters, where the actual and formal parameters
must be of identical type and further, the actual parameter must be a variable,
or component of a variable, rather than an expression. Less strict cases are for
assignment and value parameters, where the assigned expression usually, but not
always, must be of the same type, and can be composed of constants, variables and
functions. File types cannot be used in this way. Thus integers can be assigned to
reals, and subranges to each other or to or from the full range type. However, the
code generator may have to plant conversion or range checking orders. Similar
checks have to be made for the operands of the various operators within expressions. With some of the relational operators, most types are allowed, as long as
they are identical or at least similar, but they always return Boolean results, whatever the operand types. For example, pairs of arrays or records of the same type
can be tested for equality. For the arithmetic and Boolean operators only limited
types are allowed, specific to each operator. However, it must be remembered, for
example, that the + operator can be applied to any combination of reals, integer
and their subranges, or to sets of the same type.
One further semantic check is necessary for Pascal. That is, that a variable
used to control a for loop must be a local variable, declared in the current level,
and must not be modified within the scope of the loop. This must be checked in

122

Compiler Engineering using Pascal

two places; within the body of the loop, and within the bodies of any procedures or
functions that might make a non-local access to the control variable. The checking
is achieved by marking the property entry of each variable that is modified nonlocally, either in an assignment statement or as a varparameter in a procedure call.
If such a marked variable is subsequently found to be used as a for loop control
variable, this can potentially be an illegal modification, and the Pascal standard
requires that a semantic error be indicated even if the potential modification does
not result in an actual modification. As each for loop is encountered, the property
entry for the control variable must be checked to ensure that it is declared in the
current level and neither marked as being already in use as a control variable of a
surrounding for loop nor as being modified non-locally. The property entry will
then be marked to indicate that the identifier is currently used as a control variable.
Each time that a variable is assigned to or used as a var parameter, its property
entry must be checked to ensure that it is not marked as being currently in use as
a control variable. The mark in the property entry is removed at the end of the for
loop to indicate that it is no longer in use as a control variable.

9.1 Write a set of Pascal declarations that uses all the different forms and classes
given in figure 9.7, and then give the corresponding property (and type)
entries.
9.2 How would you change the semantics of Pascal to simplify semantic analysis? What useful features of the language would be lost because of this
simplification?
9.3 If the semantics of Pascal were modified so that each access to a field of
a variant record would cause the case field to be checked, how would you
modify figure 9.6?

10 Code generation
The P compiler merely generated code for a simple hypothetical computer which
was then interpreted. For substantial programs the run-time inefficiency resulting
from use of the interpretation technique may be unacceptable. The generation of
code to run directly on real computers must therefore now be considered. Actual
examples of code for the Motorola 68000 series and DEC PDPll series will be
given. As well as the requirements of the particular instruction sets the requirements of features of Pascal not found in P will also be considered.
Many factors influence the style and method of code generation in a compiler.
One of the more important is the nature of the target machine instruction set.
Another is whether fast compilation or fast run time is required. A simple compiler
might produce reasonably efficient object code using simple algorithms such as
those described earlier in section 4.4. If very efficient object code is required the
compiler will have to spend much more time in optimisation to generate the best
code sequence practicable.

10.1 Instruction sets
One classification of instruction sets is by the number of different operands allowed in each instruction. In zero address code, all the operations except stack
and unstack take their operands from the stack and place a result on the stack.
This code corresponds to the notation known as reverse polish. In one address
code, the accumulator register and one other operand are available to each operation. The result of the operation is left in the accumulator. In two address code,
two general operands can be used in each instruction with the result being placed
in one of the operands. In a typical three address instruction, values from two of
the operands are combined and the result placed in the third.
For example, the code generated for:

a:= b + c;
in the various styles might be:

123

124

Compiler Engineering using Pascal
0 address

1 address

2address

3 address

stack b
stack c
+
unstack a

ace= b
ace+ c
ace=> a

a<=b
a<+c

a<=b+c

It is possible to compare, in abstract, the potential run time efficiencies of the

various styles of coding, assuming that the majority of object code consists of
expressions and assignments. At first glance, these styles of code generation are
quite different, but metrics can be found to compare them. The first and simplest
is just the number of instructions, even though they vary wildly in complexity.
This gives us some idea of the maximum possible throughput in a computer. The
second metric is the number of store accesses for operands in each sequence. This
gives us some idea of how fast the store would have to be in our imaginary computer. The final metric is the number of code components (operands and operators)
there are in each of the code sequences. This metric both gives us some idea of
the complexity of the processors of our imaginary computers, and indicates some
multiple, depending on word size, of the number of instruction accesses, which
could be combined with the operand accesses to give the total throughput required
from the store.
Oaddress

1 address

2address

3 address

instructions
4
2
3
1
operand accesses
9
3
5
3
code components
7
4
6
6
The similarity in the number of code components is reasonable, as there is
some minimal information theory encoding to which every actual encoding is an
approximation. The most important metric from the point of view of speed is the
number of operand accesses. The difference between one or three address code
and the other two encodings can mean a factor of two in the throughput due to
store limitations. Note that in real zero address computers, access to the top few
elements of the stack is usually speeded up by holding them in registers. The
initial conclusion from this example is therefore that either the one address or
three address style of code generation should be used, all considerations of the
real machine instruction set aside.
One of the practical considerations is the ease of generation from a high level
language. It has been shown, in section 4.4, that the zero and one address styles
require reasonably simple methods to generate them. Two and three address styles
actually require quite a complicated method to generate, involving the generation
of a tree to represent each expression before it can be analysed to generate good
code. However, for the implementor prepared to invest in the production of a
highly optimising compiler this is less important.
Overall, for this example, in the absence of any constraints imposed by a real
machine, the style of code that is both easy to generate and efficient to run is one
address instructions. The PDP11 will be used as an example of how a similar study

Code generation

125

can be performed for a real machine. This cannot be used for three address code
but can cope with the other styles. The code sequences for the example would be:

MOV b, a

The theoretical arguments carry over to this real machine, if anything reinforcing the choice of one address code as optimum. It should be noted that in the few
cases where two address code would be better, such as a := a+ b or a := b, one
address code can be improved to be as good as two address by simple peephole
optimisations.
Consider now the case of the 68000. In this machine only a variant of one address code is possible as the arithmetic instructions require that one of the operands
is already in a data register. A stack in memory is supported but only one operand
of a typical arithmetic instruction may be obtained from the stack. The only real
exception to the one address nature of the code is the use of the two operand
MOVE instruction which is suitable for simple assignments; it is a simple peephole optimisation to exploit this instruction. The code for the above example might
be:
MOVEb,DO
ADDc,DO
MOVE DO, a
This sequence is very similar to the PDP11 one address code and the number of
instructions, operand accesses, instruction words and total memory accesses can
in fact be the same in certain circumstances.
Similar results can be found for most expressions, indicating that the one address code is a good compromise for both run time and compile time efficiency on
many computers, especially if simple optimisations are included. Of course, there
are some computers, such as the CDC Cyber series, where the one address style
is quite inappropriate and a tree must be used to generate three address code.

10.2 A tree based code generation method
There are two main strategies for code generation. The first generates code as the
program is being parsed and might therefore be termed "on-the-fly". This was the
method used in the recursive descent version of the P compiler. The second builds
an expression tree which can subsequently be recursively traversed. The YACC

Compiler Engineering using Pascal

126

generated version of the P compiler is an example of the latter, and the associated
tree based code generation is presented below. If efficient 2 or 3 address code is to
be generated or global optimisation performed, a tree must be used. Only peephole
optimisations can be performed with on-the-fly code generation. If a tree is used,
it may be generated during the analysis phases, or it may be created specifically
for code generation after analysis has been completed. Either implies extra passes
in the compiler to generate code. The use of a tree means that expressions can
be reordered to save instructions, and that a set of registers can be allocated more
effectively.
For example, given the expression:
x*y+j*k
an equivalent tree is shown in figure 10.1. Figure 10.2 defines a Pascal data structure for representing an expression tree. Each interior node (expnode) of the Pascal tree contains pointers to left and right subtrees, while leaves of the tree just
contain an operand (lexemenode). For convenience in the YACC program, the
interior nodes do not hold the operators themselves but point to leaf nodes which
contain the operators. The procedure plantexpression is a recursive tree walking
procedure which generates 1 address code while traversing the tree representing an
expression from left to right. There is no attempt at optimisation in this procedure.
The code generated is identical to that produced by the on-the-fly generator in the
original recursive descent version of the compiler presented in chapter 4. The M
instructions generated for the example would be:

ace*
>ace=
ace*
ace+

X

/

X

y
j

k
unstack

*

/
~y

type
nodecasetype = (prognode, blocknode, assignnode, writenode,
ifnode, whilenade, expnode, lexemenode);
pnode = fnodetype;
nodetype = record
next: pnode;
serial: integer;
case nodecase: nodecasetype of
prognode
(pidlist: pnode); {block in next}
blocknode : (contents, lastb: pnode);
{lastb used only to build tree}
assignnode,
writenode,
ifnode,
whilenode : (pexp, pete, lasts: pnode);
{assign: etc=var, if/while: etc=block, write: etc=unused}
expnode
(lexp, operator, rexp: pnode);
{ for expression and comparison - doesn't use next }
lexemenode: (lexeme: lexemetype; lexemeval: lexemevaltype);
{ for operator, constant, identifier, read
- doesn't use next except for identifiers in idlist }
end;
procedure plantexpression(tree, action: pnode; var iflabel:integer);
{ action = nil means ace := tree,
action<> nil means ace :=ace 'action' tree}
var
optype: optypetype;
opval: integer;
begin
if tree <> nil then with treef do
case nodecase of
prognode, blocknode, assiqnnode, writenode, ifnode, whilenode:
writeln ('non-expression node in plantexpression');
expnode:
begin
{ stack ace if loaded, load ace with tree }
plantexpression (lexp, nil, iflabel);
plantexpression (rexp, operator, iflabel);
if action <> nil then { perform action }
reverseactionunstack (action, iflabel);
end;
lexemenode:
begin
case lexeme of
mulop, addop, relop:
writeln ('operand expected in plantexpression');
identifier:
begin
checkid (lexemeval.idval);
optype := varop;
opval := variables[lexemeval.idval]
end;
constant:
begin

Compiler Engineering using Pascal

128

optype := constop;
opval := lexemeval.constval;
end;
readlexeme:
begin
if accinuse then
plant(accstore, specialop, stack);
plant(call, labelop, readproc);
optype := specialop;
opval := unstack;
end;
end;
if action
nil then begin { load ace with operand }
if optype <> specialop then
{ i.e. not already 'read' }
plantaccload (optype, opval);
end
else { perform action }
if optype = specialop then
{ i.e. just stacked & read into ace }
reverseactionunstack (action, iflabel)
else
forwardaction (action, optype, opval, iflabel);
accinuse := true;
end;
end;

=

end;

Code generation from a binary tree

Figure 10.3 gives the bodies of the four Pascal functions used by the YACC
program in figure 8.20. The two routines makelexemenode and makenode are used
to create leaf nodes and interior nodes respectively. The field serial is available
solely for debugging purposes. It indicates the sequence in which the various
nodes have been created. The two routines startlist and makelist are used to start
a list of blocks and statements, and to add a new block or statement to the end of
a list, respectively. They are used because the routine makenode, if used by itself
by the YACC program, would tend to create lists in reverse order.
function makelexemenode (pnext: pnode; plexeme: lexemetype;
plexemeval: lexemevaltype): pnode;
var
tnode: pnode;
begin
new (tnode) ;
with tnodef do begin
serial := nextserial;
nextserial := nextserial + 1;
nodecase := lexemenode;
lexeme := plexeme;

Code generation

129

lexemeval := plexemeval;
next := pnext;
end;
makelexemenode := tnode;
end;
procedure dumptree (tree: pnode; level: integer); forward;
function makenode (pnext: pnode; pnodecase: nodecasetype;
lnode, cnode, rnode: pnode): pnode;
var
tnode: pnode;
begin
new (tnode) ;
with tnodej do begin
serial := nextserial;
nextserial := nextserial + 1;
nodecase := pnodecase;
next := pnext;
case pnodecase of
prognode :pidlist := lnode;
blocknode :begin contents := lnode; lastb := nil; end;
assignnode,
writenode,
ifnode,
whilenode :begin pexp := lnode; pete := cnode; lasts := nil;end;
expnode
:begin lexp:=lnode; operator:=cnode; rexp:=rnode;end;
end;
end;
makenode := tnode;
end;
function startlist (head: pnode): pnode;
begin
if trace then
writeln ('startlist');
startlist := head;
if head <> nil then
if headj.nodecase = blocknode then
headj.lastb :=head
else
headj.lasts :=head;
if trace then begin
dumptree (head, 1);
writeln;
end;
end;
function makelist (head, item: pnode): pnode;
begin
if trace then
writeln ('makelist');
if head = nil then
makelist := startlist (item)
else if item = nil then

130

Compiler Engineering using Pascal

makelist := head
else begin
makelist := head;
if headj.nodecase = blocknode then begin
headj.lastbj.next :=item;
headj.lastb :=item;
end else begin
headj.lastsj.next :=item;
headj.lasts :=item;
end;
end;
if trace then begin
dumptree (head, l);
dumptree (item, l);
writeln;
end;
end;

The rest of the text of the semantic and code generation phases for the table driven
version of the P compiler is given in figure 10.4. The routine dumptree is available solely for debugging purposes. It recursively outputs the nodes of the tree,
indented to show the various levels. Procedure planttree recursively performs the
semantic and code generation actions for the tree, mainly dealing with the various
statements allowed in P, calling plan/expression from figure 10.2 whenever it encounters an expression. The two routines forwardaction and reverseactionunstack
plant code to deal with the various operators. The former deals with the simple
case of combining the operand with the accumulator. The latter deals with the case
when the accumulator is to be combined with a subexpression evaluated earlier
and placed on the stack.
The runtime system used to obey the compiled code is identical to that given
in chapter 5 associated with the recursive descent compiler.

procedure declid (idval: char);
begin
if variables[idval] > 0 then
error(8) variable already declared
else begin
variables[idval] := nextvariable;
nextvariable .- nextvariable + 1;
end;
end;
procedure checkid (idval: char);
begin
if variables[idval]
0 then begin
error(9); variable not declared
variables[idval] := -1;
{ to stop further error messages }
end;
end;

Problems

10.1 Modify the code of procedure plantexpression in figure 10.2 to optimise the
compiled code. A simple way to do this is to first inspect the left subtree. If
this comprises just a Jexemenode then evaluate the right subtree first, thereby
avoiding potentially unnecessary stacking of the left subtree. Otherwise
evaluate the left subtree first as before.
10.2 Compare the code generated by the unoptimised code generator with that
using the technique of the previous problem for the following expressions:
a+b*c

11 Compiled code: statements
This chapter outlines code sequences for evaluating expressions and performing
assignments for all Pascal types. It also describes sequences for control constructs
such as while, for and if statements. Examples are given both for the PDPll and
68000. For convenience, the compiled code is presented in assembly language
form, rather than pure binary instructions. Standard assembly language conventions are used wherever possible, but the reader is warned that, particularly for
the 68000, different assemblers may accept slight variations in the instructions
as presented here. It is assumed that code is being generated in a one address
style, on-the-fly, perhaps with some peephole optimisation. There is no attempt
to make optimal use of registers. One main register is used as the accumulator,
with occasional use of a second register in certain sequences, mainly for double
length operations or for Booleans. For the PDP11 R3 is used for evaluating fixed
point expressions with R2 occasionally used as the second register. Similarly DO
and sometimes 01 will be used in the 68000. As the compiled code sequences
are considered it will become clear that there are ample registers available on the
68000, but a shortage of registers on the PDP11 means that R2 will sometimes be
used for additional purposes.

11.1 Simple arithmetic
Code sequences for simple arithmetic will now be described. Integer arithmetic is
considered first, followed by subranges, user defined scalar enumerated types and
finally arithmetic on characters.
Integer
In the PDP11 the default size for integer arithmetic will be taken as 16 bits, the
word length of the computer. In the 68000 default integers will be the largest
size of integer which the hardware conveniently supports, namely 32 bits. These
decisions will be discussed further in chapter 13. The correspondence between
Pascal integer arithmetic operations and instructions is shown in figure 11.1 and
examples of typical sequences in figure 11.2. No attempt is made at this stage to
137

138

Compiler Engineering using Pascal

show how the operands x, y, i,jand kare treated. In the case of multiply and divide
on the 68000 it has been assumed that the 68020 32 bit instructions are available;
if this is not the case software subroutines would have to be called to emulate the
effect of these instructions. In the first two examples the intermediate result, x*y,
has been treated in the simplest possible way by stacking. In the 68000, where
spare D registers are available, it would have been more efficient to evaluate j*k
in another register as shown in figure 11.3.

Integer arithmetic instructions

Integer subrange
If the subrange is sufficiently restricted the subrange variable may occupy just one
byte. On the 68000, even if the subrange will not fit into one byte, it may be
small enough to fit into a 16 bit word. Otherwise it must be treated as integer
as above, except that an assignment to a subrange variable may need to be range
checked. On the PDP11 the range check requires a rather clumsy pair of tests,
but on the 68000 the CHK or CHK2 instruction forces an exception if the value
is out of range, as shown in figure 11.4. Use of the CHK instruction requires the
lower bound to be subtracted as CHK tests against a lower bound of zero. This can
be avoided using the CHK2 instruction, but use of the CHK2 instruction requires
that the bounds of the subrange are available in consecutive memory locations.
Where CHK2 is used the initialisation of these locations should be done when the
subrange is declared. If the subrange can fit into a byte (or a 68000 word) then,
in general, the equivalent byte (or 68000 word) operations may be substituted for
the operations described previously. However, the PDP11 suffers from not having
an ADDB or a SUBB instruction to add or subtract single bytes. Also neither
the PDPll nor the 68000 has a MULB instruction. In these cases the operand is
moved to a spare register, such as R2 or D1, and the equivalent word operation

performed, as shown in figure 11.5. In exceptional cases when R2 already holds
useful information, either another unused register must be chosen or the contents
of a register must be temporarily stacked. Similar action is needed when, for
example, a subrange held in a byte is added to a full length integer. In the PDP11
the expansion from 8 to 16 bits is implicit in the loading of the register but in the
68000 an explicit EXT instruction is needed to set the next 8 bits of the register.

Enumerated types
User defined enumerated types are implemented as positive integer values, starting
at zero. The only operations allowed are assignment, comparison, succ, pred and
ord, which are all treated in a similar way to the corresponding operations on
integer subranges.

Char type
Assuming the ASCII/ISO character set, a character has a value in the range 0
to.127, and is naturally held in a byte. The operations allowed are assignment,
comparison, succ, pred and ord. These will be as for integer subranges. Note that
ord and chr have no effect on the numerical value of an expression, only on its
type. Subranges of char are treated in exactly the same way as char.
Overflow
Many operations can cause an arithmetic overflow. Theoretically, after each such
operation, code should be planted to check the overflow flag, and signal an error
if it is set. However, in practice, this significantly increases the amount of code
generated and so is often omitted. It is possible to have two modes of code generation selectable by the user, one of which generates overflow checks and one

11.2 Boolean and relational arithmetic
Boolean
A Boolean can only take the values false or true. For simplicity, Booleans are
treated in a similar way to an enumerated type. Thus, they are usually stored in a
byte rather than a single bit. They are represented by positive integer values, 0 for
false and 1 for true, these being the values returned by applying ord to a Boolean
value.
The operations allowed on Booleans are assignment, comparison, succ, pred
and ord, and the logical operations or, not and and. Instructions for the logical
operations are given in figure 11.6. For simplicity, Boolean expressions should be
evaluated in the main accumulator. However, this is inconvenient for relational
arithmetic, as both a Boolean value and the intermediate non-Boolean values used
in the comparisons are often being accumulated. Therefore the second accumulator register, R2 or Dl, is used as a Boolean accumulator. Even the use of this
second register sometimes results in conflicts, for example if a div operation occurred in a relation. In these cases the Boolean accumulator will have to be stacked

Boolean arithmetic examples

further point to note is that the operations permitted on the Boolean type make it
almost impossible to assign a value other than false or true, represented by 0 or 1,
to a Boolean. Assignment, comparison, and ord are thus as for integer subranges,
except that no range checking is necessary, and a different register is used as the
accumulator. The one situation in which range checking must be retained is when
taking the succor pred of a Boolean.

Relational operations
The PDP11 has poor facilities for dealing with the result of a relational operation.
The difficulty is that having used a compare instruction to set the condition codes
the only effective way to use the settings is by means of a branch instruction. The
68000 is better in that it provides the Sec instructions, which allow a value, 0 or
-1, to be assigned to an operand depending whether a test result is false or true.

11.3 Set arithmetic
Examples are given in figure 11.10 of the implementation of sets with a small
number of elements. Within a variable of set type one bit is used to represent each
element of the set. The variables can be chosen to be byte, word or longword
(on 68000) sized, depending on the number of elements in the set. Thus, in the
figure, p, q and rare each represented in a single byte. Within the byte the least
significant bit, bit 0, represents red, bit 1 represents yellow and bit 2 represents
blue. The remaining 5 bits of the byte are unused. Thus the value corresponding
to red is.l, yellow is 2 and blue is 4 and combinations are obtained by adding these
values together. It follows that the other set values are:

The same registers used for integer arithmetic are also used for set arithmetic. In
figure 11.10, set union is achieved with an OR operation, set intersection with an
AND and the set difference p - q by p AND NOT q. The complications which
arise when the number of set elements is too great to fit into a word are discussed
in section 13.2.

provided for real arithmetic. In some systems all the arithmetic must be carried
out by software subroutines. In others a hardware floating point unit is provided

Real arithmetic
The PDP11 option provides arithmetic on 32 bit or 64 bit numbers. The examples
given here will assume 32 bit operation, which gives about 7 decimal digits of accuracy in the real numbers. Six floating point accumulator registers, ACO - ACS,
are provided. These are used as accumulators in place of RO- RS. The floating
point instructions provided combine a 32 bit pair of memory locations, specified
by the address of the first of the pair, with the contents of one of these accumulator registers. These instructions do not set the usual condition codes directly.
Instead special floating point condition codes are set; these are copied to the usual
codes before a conditional branch (see section 11.6 for further discussion of branch
instructions.) A summary of the relevant floating point instructions is given in fig-

11.5 Operation on structures
Structured types in Pascal are sets, records, arrays and files. The operations allowed on sets have already been considered. The only operation allowed on
records and arrays, in general, is the assignment operation. For one type of array, the 'packed array of char' or string type, comparison is also allowed. No
arithmetic or assignment operations are possible on files, hence further consideration of files is postponed until chapter 13.
The major problem with structures is that they cannot usually be held in the
accumulator registers. Instead, the addresses of the structures will usually be saved
in registers and a small loop planted to perform the assignment or comparison.
Thus, if a and bare two arrays, each of 10 integers, the assignment 'a:= b' might
be compiled as shown in figure 11.14. In the PDP11 it usually takes more than one
instruction to calculate the address of a or b, because there is no LEA instruction.
Assignment of records can be performed in a similar way, once the size of the

condition flags have correct values as set by the final comparison. These flags are
used by the conditional branch instruction in the usual way.

11.6 Control structures
The code sequences generated for control structures will now be described. The
statements considered here comprise goto, while, repeat, for, if and case statements.
The simplest control structure is the goto. This causes two separate implementation problems. The first is that, although branch orders are smaller and
faster than jump orders, it may not always be possible to use them as they have
a limited range. The PDPll, and 68000 short form, allow a branch of -128 to
+ 127 words or bytes respectively, while the longer 68000 form gives a range of
-32768 to 32767 bytes. For a goto to an as yet unknown address it is impossible
to decide whether the destination is in range or not. This causes most problems
with conditional branches within other control constructs, for which there are no
equivalent conditional jumps. The simplest solution is to plant a branch whenever
the destination is known and within range, and otherwise to plant a jump. In the
latter case, for conditional branches, a branch on the inverse condition must also
be planted just before the jump to bypass it if the condition is false. Goto a label

150

Compiler Engineering using Pascal

in the current procedure thus becomes:
PDPJJ
68000
BRA label
BR label
if the label is in range, otherwise:
JMP label
Similarly, a conditional branch, which is used in the Pascal ifstatements and looping statements, might be either:
BEQ label
or:
BNEL
JMPlabel

L:

Alternatively, when the destination address is unknown, a branch can be planted
to an undefined location. Whenever some code is planted, the displacement from
the branch must be tested, and if it is nearly 127 words or bytes away it is assumed
that the short branch instruction alone is insufficient. The same applies as 32767
bytes is approached if the longer 68000 form is chosen initially. The simplest solution is then to plant an extra jump instruction and to set the branch instruction
pointing to it. Suppose, for example, the Pascal statement goto lxx involves a
long forward branch. Initially a BR or BRA instruction is planted but this has an
insufficient offset. The final code becomes:
PDPJJ
68000
BR 11
BRA 11
{about 120 words of code}
{about 32760 bytes of code}
BR 12
BRA 12
11: JMPlxx
12: {rest of code}
lxx: {destination of original branch}
In praetice, this overflow mechanism will rarely be used, and most jumps to unknown destinations will be planted as branches, saving time and space overall.
The second problem is how to treat a goto to a label in a different procedure.
As the consequences of the implied procedure exits are not yet clear, consideration
of this case will be postponed to section 12.5.
For the other control structures, it will be assumed that branch orders can
always be used. In fact, the problems of long branches from gotos all apply to
these control structures as well. The various structures use Boolean expressions
as discussed in section 11.2. Code for a typical while statement, and a repeat
statement, is shown in figures 11.16 and 11.17, respectively. Consider next the
for statement. It is convenient for the compiler to declare an extra variable, final

expression and is possible because, in Pascal, the user is not allowed to change
the final value during the loop. Even when the final value expression is simple the

The code for if statements is straightforward and figure 11.19 gives an example. The simplest implementation of case statements is as a series of nested
if-then-else statements, using code similar to that of figure 11.19. If the possibilities for the case index are sparse this is probably the best method. However, the
possibilities are usually dense as in the example in figure 11.20. Here the case
values are in the range 0 to n, where n is some relatively small positive number.

complication results if the order is permuted. The code avoids repeated tests of the
case index value. T is an address table for the case entries, with the first element
holding the address of the statement labelled by a value of zero, and so on. Unused entries will contain the address of an error routine or, if the implementation
includes the otherwise extension to Pascal, the code for the otherwise case.

12 Compiled code: run time stack
organisation
This chapter is concerned with the problem of where in the memory variables are
placed and how they are accessed, both locally and non-locally. Because the allocation of space is closely related to the calling of procedures, code for procedure
calls is also presented. In this chapter, procedure is used as a generic term for
procedure, function or program.

12.1 Variable storage and local access
In this section the local accessing of variables, in the procedure in which they are
declared, is considered. In the example program in figure 12.1 all the variables are
accessed locally except for those non-local uses of p and i indicated by comments.
Suppose fixed addresses were used for the variables, completely determined
at compile time as in the simple P compiler. For example, consider the simple
assignment:
i :=j;
Variable i could be placed at 1000 and variable j at 1004, so the code generated
for the assignment would be:
PDPll
68000
MOVE.L 1004, DO
MOV 1004,R3
MOVR3, 1000
MOVE.L DO, 1000
However, this means that space must be allocated for all the variables of the whole
program regardless of whether all the procedures in which the variables are declared are called or not. A further problem is that a recursive procedure needs
a fresh copy of its variables each time it is called. These problems can only be
solved by a storage method which allocates memory as and when required. However, it must still be efficient in terms of the number of instructions required to
access a variable.
The crucial observation is that, since the size and number of variables are
known at compile time, the relative positions of variables in any procedure can be

154

Compiled code: run time stack organisation

allocated at compile time. The compiler knows that an area of memory of known
size with known mapping of variables onto it but unknown starting position will
be used. This area of memory is called a stack frame, or sometimes an activation
record or data area, for the procedure. Parameters will be dealt with in a similar
way in section 12.4, although conformant arrays will have to be considered as a
special case. At run time, when the procedure is entered, the actual storage is
obtained and a frame base register is made to point to its starting position, with all
variables being accessed at offsets from this register. The register will be called
FB (frame base); for the 68000 A6 will be used as FB, and for the PDP11 R5.

stack frame. Assuming that memory location zero is at the top of the page, so that

Since addresses increase down the page the offsets from frame base are all negative. Because each stack frame item is a 16 bit word in the PDPll or a 32 bit
longword in the 68000, the offsets in the 68000, measured in bytes, are double
those of the PDP11.
The space required by a procedure is allocated on entry to, and recovered on
exit from, the procedure. Since procedure entry is on a last in, first out basis a stack
can be used for this purpose, hence the use of the terminology "stack frame". In
both machines there is a hardware supported stack, managed by the stack pointer
or SP register. The use of SP, pointing at the last used stack item, is shown
in figure 12.3. Hardware supported stacks usually grow to decreasing memory
addresses, that is up the page in the figures. This is why x, y and z appeared in
reverse order in figure 12.2.

FB and SP always point to the frame base and the top item of the stack frame,
respectively, of the currently active procedure. This implies that on procedure
exit FB and SP must be reset. SP is simply reset from FB. However, FB must be
reset to be the frame base of the calling procedure (the one being returned to) and
hence old FB values must be remembered on the stack. In fact, the second item
in the linkage area at the start of each stack frame is used for this purpose. These
old frame base values link the stack frames together in the reverse order in which
the procedures were called. The links are known as the dynamic cluJin. Code
sequences for procedure entry and exit are given later in section 12.3. Figure 12.4
gives a picture of the stack for the program in figure 12.1, showing the dynamic
chain of old frame bases when two procedures have been called, just before the

12.2 Non-local access
Not all variable accesses are local. Pascal allows variables of other procedures
to be accessed non-locally. A problem exists because the frame base is pointing
at the current procedure's stack frame, and cannot be used to access the variables
of the other procedure. Consider again the program in figure 12.1 and the stack
in figure 12.4. Superficially, variables of procedure one or the program can be
accessed from procedure two by using old frame bases one or two links, respectively, down the dynamic chain. In this case this happens to correspond to the
difference in static levels of procedure nesting in the source program. Thus, in
this case, satisfactory code might be planted by the compiler. However, suppose
the program were modified so that procedure two called itself recursively. This
would add another stack frame and link to the dynamic chain, hence two or three
links, respectively, would need to be used for non-local accesses. The problem
is that the dynamic sequence of procedure activations is not necessarily the same

158

Compiler Engineering using Pascal

as the static nesting sequence. There is no way of being certain, at compile time,
how many links in the dynamic chain must be traversed. It would seem that some
extra information must be placed in the linkage area to indicate which stack frame
belongs to which procedure.
It might be thought that the first stack frame at the required static level down
the dynamic chain would be the correct non-local frame but even this is not so.
Pascal allows procedures and functions to be passed as parameters. The calling
of such a procedure can result in a discontinuity in the sequence of static levels in
the stack frames. This is illustrated by the Pascal program in figure 12.5 and the
corresponding set of stack frames in figure 12.6, shown just as procedure param
program procaspara;
procedure pl (procedure p );
var x, y : integer;
procedure p2;
var z: integer;
begin
p;
end;
begin
p2;
end;
procedure apl;
var a, b : integer;
procedure param;
var s : integer;
begin
s := a;
end;
begin
pl (param);
end;
begin
apl
end.

Figure 12.5

Procedure passed as parameter

attempts to access non-locally variable a of procedure apl. The first frame at level
one in the dynamic chain is in fact that for procedure pl rather than for apl. Two
methods which can be used to overcome these difficulties are static chaining and
displays.

Static chaining
Instead of trying to rely solely on the dynamic chain, another chain is used, called
the static chain, which links the currently visible static levels. These are all the
stack frames which contain variables which the current procedure could legiti-

In order to cope with procedures passed as parameters, the head of the static
chain, which leads to the set of static links comprising the non-local accessing
environment for the procedure, is passed with the procedure address as the parameter. When the parametric procedure is finally called the correct environment can
be reinstated. This process is described in section 12.3. Code sequences for some
simple non-local accesses using static chains are given here. For example, in figprogram eg;

both p2a and p2b can access the variables of pl so static links from their stack
frames will point to the frame for pl. In Pascal, non-local accessibility is inherited, in the sense that anything accessible from pl is also accessible from p2a or
p2b; hence the static link from pl can be used by p2a and p2b to access variables
of the main program. The corresponding stack layout is given in figure 12.8. In

link.

MOV #2, R3

For the second statement, 'x := 2', the compiler knows that xis declared at static
level 1 and that the access is made at level 2; therefore it must plant code to go
one link down the static chain. To do this the compiler must use another address
register which will be called extra frame base register (XFB). In the 68000 this
might be AS while in the PDP11 R4 could be used. The code sequence is:
MOV #2, R3
MOVEQ.L #2, DO
MOV -2(FB), XFB
MOVE.L -4(FB), XFB
MOV R3, -4(XFB)
MOVE.L DO, -8(XFB)
Finally for 'g := 3' two links on the static chain must be traversed:
MOV #3, R3
MOVEQ.L #3, DO
MOV -2(FB), XFB
MOVE.L -4(FB), XFB
MOV -2(XFB), XFB
MOVE.L -4(XFB), XFB
MOV R3, -4(XFB)
MOVE.L DO, -8(XFB)
If these assignments occurred consecutively an optimising compiler might actually
note that the XFB register was already pointing at a known place on the static chain
and would not then plant the second instruction in the sequence for 'g := 3'.
Non-local accessing can be expensive if accesses are made far down the static
chain but fortunately this does not occur in many programs, except for access to
the variables of the main program. If another register is available (say A4 on
the 68000) a useful optimisation is to keep the frame base for the main program
permanently in this register (global frame base). This results in the variables of
the main program being accessed as efficiently as those of the current procedure.

The display method
This alternative method for non-local accessing is really another optimisation of
the static chain. It relies on keeping a vector of the old frame base values making
up the static chain, instead of the static chain. This vector, known as the display,
is indexed by the required static level to get the correct frame base for a particular
non-local access. This scheme has the advantage that locating the extra frame base
only takes one instruction. Considering the previous example, figure 12.9 gives the
stack layout using a display vector. In this figure display elements 1, 2 and 3 point
to the frame bases of p2a, pl and program eg, respectively. Because a display
element is set on entry to each procedure, it is necessary to save the previous
value in the linkage information so that it can be restored on exit. In figure 12.9,
three of these saved values are unknown, indicated by 'nil'. However, the linkage
information for p2a contains the previous display element 3, appropriate when p2b
was in scope. Although display element 3 is set here, it could only be used in a
non-local access if some further procedure, nested within p2a or p2b, were called.
The display is placed at some fixed address and has enough entries to cope with
the most deeply nested program likely, say sixteen.

Example code sequences for the previous non-local assignments using the display method are now presented. These assume that the display is placed at the
location "display". The vector can be indexed at compile-time by computing the
address of the required element. The first element is at the start of the display,
denoted in the examples by "displayl", the second is one word after, denoted by
"display2" and so on. The code for 'x := 2' becomes:
MOV #2, R3
MOVEQ.L #2, DO
MOV display2, XFB
MOVE.L display2, XFB
MOV R3, -4(XFB)
MOVE.L DO, -8(XFB)
For 'g := 3' the saving becomes apparent:
MOV #3, R3
MOVEQ.L #3, DO
MOV display!, XFB
MOVE.L display!, XFB
MOV R3, -4(XFB)
MOVE.L DO, -8(XFB)
The use of the display reduces the number of instructions required to perform
non-local accesses. The reduction is even greater if it is possible to keep the
display in registers, in the same way that with static links the global FB can be
placed in a register. Several A registers might be used for this in the 68000 but
the PDPll has insufficient registers.
The disadvantage of the display method is the number of instructions needed
to set up the display initially and maintain it with the correct entries. This is rather
more than the instructions needed to maintain static links, which are described in
section 12.3. Some saving can be made by optimising out the maintenance of the
display element for the innermost procedures of a static nest, p2a and p2b in the example, but the cost is still greater than with static links. Also it is quite costly when
procedures passed as parameters are considered. Since a copy of the environment
(or something to enable the re-establishment of the correct environment) must be
used, a copy of the display must be included as part of the parameter. Similar
problems occur with non-local gotos (section 12.5). Because of these complications the simpler static chain method is preferred in most Pascal compilers, even
though this results in more instructions for certain operand accesses.

12.3 Procedure calling, entry and exit mechanisms
The linkage information at the base of each area of the run time stack is created
when a procedure is entered. Two parts of this three word linkage area, the dynamic chain and the non-local access information (static chain or display), were
discussed above. ·The third word is used to hold the return address for the procedure; that is, the address to which the procedure must exit, usually the point
immediately following the procedure call, as shown in figure 12.10.
The mechanism required to create and dispose of this area is now given. There
are three stages to consider, the call sequence, the entry sequence, and the exit
sequence. These places are suitably commented in figure 12.11. The call and

fred { call sequence };
end.

Call, entry and exit sequence for a procedure

166

Compiler Engineering using Pascal

entry sequences between them must completely set up the stack frame. Assuming
the use of static links for non-local accessing, the required steps are:
1. Pass the parameters.
2. Compute the new static link.
3. Note the return address.
4. Jump to the first instruction of the procedure.
5. Note the old value of frame base (FB).
6. Create the new static link.
7. Update the frame base and stack pointer (FB and SP).
The parameters, if any, are placed on the stack before the linkage information, as
will be described later. Where there is a choice between placing similar instructions in the call sequence or the entry sequence the entry sequence is preferable.
This is because a procedure definition occurs once whereas there are usually many
calls. Placing instructions once, rather than many times, shortens the compiled
code, although the number of instructions executed remains the same. Considering the steps given above, 1, 2, 3 and 4 must be performed in the call sequence
but steps 5, 6 and 7 are left to the entry sequence.
The stack before the call is shown in figure 12.12. The code for the call sequence is:
o Evaluate and stack the parameters as described in section 12.4.
o Compute the new static link and store it in XFB. The new link depends on
the relative static level of the calling and called procedures. If the static
level increases by one: use FB. If going to the same static level: use the
same static link, at -2(FB) (PDPll) or -4(FB) (68000). When decreasing
the static level by one: use the next link in the chain, and so on.
o JSR, or BSR on 68000, procedure entry point.
o Return address: adjust SP, if necessary, to recover parameter space.

12.4 Results and parameters
The method used by the P compiler in chapter 4 to deal with the results of functions
was to leave them in the accumulator register on exit. They can then be used
in an expression without any extra instructions being planted. This method is
convenient, and possible for Pascal because only simple types may be returned
as the results of functions. However, there may be other statements between the
assignment of the result and the return from the function. Therefore, in general,
the compiler must create a local variable within the function to contain the value
of the result between assignment and exit.
In the case of languages which allow structured values to be returned as the
result of a function, such as Ada, space must be created on the stack before the
first parameter sufficient to hold the structured result. Then after exit from the
function the value is available on the stack to be manipulated in whatever way the
calling sequence requires.
In the previous section it was noted that parameters are pushed onto the run
time stack. Consider value parameters first. In the context of:
PROCEDURE q (a, b: integer; VAR c, d: integer);
The code to push the value parameters a and b onto the stack, in an example call of
q, is shown in figure 12.15. Inside the procedure, the parameters are accessed just
like local variables, except that the offsets from frame base are positive instead of
negative. No side effects, such as changes to the values of i or j, can occur using
value parameters.

Compiled code: run time stack organisation
q (i,j+1, x, z);
PDP11
MOV i(FB), R3
MOV R3, -(SP)
MOV j(FB), R3
ADD#1,R3
MOV R3, -(SP)

Figure 12.15

MOVE.L j(FB), DO

MOVE.L DO, -(SP)
