comments
formal parameter a
formal parameter b

Passing value parameters in procedure call

Now consider the var parameters. When the formal parameter is modified,
the actual parameter passed must also be altered. There are two possible implementations for this mechanism. The first is to copy the initial value, treat it like a
value parameter, and to then copy back the modified value on procedure exit. The
second is to treat the parameter as a pointer; that is, passed by reference. After
some debate about whether the implementor should choose the method, the Pascal
standard eventually insisted that varparameters be passed by reference.
In the latter scheme the access of the parameter inside the procedure body is
just like a pointer access (see chapter 13). The actual parameter passed is thus
the computed address of the variable, as shown in figure 12.16 for the previous
example call of q.

PDPll
MOVFB,RO
ADD#x,RO
MOV RO, -(SP)
MOVFB,RO
ADD#z,RO
MOV RO, -(SP)

68000
PEA x(FB)

comments
formal parameter c

PEA z(FB)

formal parameter d

Figure 12.16

Passing ofvar parameters

Structured parameters used unwisely may lead to inefficiency. For example,
if a complete array is passed by the user as a value parameter, a lot of code and
time may be needed to stack it. This is an unfortunate overhead if the user is just
using the value parameter to indicate that no result is returned in the array. In some
cases it might be possible for an optimising compiler to detect that the array is not
changed inside the procedure and then treat the parameter as a var, just stacking
a reference to the array. However detecting this situation can in general require
rather elaborate analysis, because of the possibility of aliasing; that is an element
of the array also being known by another name.
Conformant array parameters also present apparent difficulties. In fact, for var
parameters, all that is required is that the index ranges are passed as parameters

170

Compiler Engineering using Pascal

together with a reference to the array itself. For value parameters, the Pascal
standard insists that a copy of the array is generated by the calling procedure, and
then the index ranges and a reference to the copy passed as above. The size of the
array to be copied is not always known at compile time because it may itself be a
conformant array. In this case the size must be computed at run time. Therefore
the copy should be placed not on the stack, but elsewhere in memory, on the
heap with other dynamic objects (see section 13.3). The sequence to access the
conformant array then obtains the index scaling and range checking information
from these additional parameters rather than from constant values. This involves
only minimal changes to the sequences given in chapter 13.

Procedures and functions passed as parameters
Consider now the passing of procedures or functions as parameters. In section 12.2
it became apparent that not only must the address of the procedure be passed, but
also the correct environment in which the procedure is to be run. Thus procedure
parameters can be considered as a record of two words. The environment is given
either by the static chain (if using that scheme for non-local accessing) or a pointer
to a copy of the display giving the correct stack frame for each static level. The
display copy might be placed on the heap. For static chains, the code sequences
are very similar to those for a normal procedure call. The major difference is that
the static link is generated when the procedure parameter is stacked, and the call
sequence just uses this value, rather than generating the new static link during the
call.
If a display is used a considerable number of extra instructions are needed for
the necessary copying of the display on parametric procedure entry and exit.

12.5 Goto non-local labels
The problem of how to treat goto's to non-local labels, which was deferred from
chapter 11, can now be considered. Because the goto implies one or more procedure exits, it is insufficient just to branch to the label. The frame base and stack
pointer (FB and SP) and anyadditional pointers used for non-local accessing must
also be reset. The static level of the procedure in which the label is declared must
be noted. The goto is translated as:
o Find correct stack frame (in a similar way to finding a non-local variable).
o Reset FB and SP to this frame.

o Branch to label.
If static links are used for non-local variable accesses the non-local jump is quite
simple and fast, as there are no additional pointers to reset. Because non-local
frame bases are saved, but non-local stack pointers are not, it is simplest to reset the
frame base and then jump to the label, where code can be planted to reset the stack

Compiled code: run time stack organisation

171

front, given the frame base, in the same way as for procedure entry (section 12.3).
If the display method is used, resetting the display is so complicated that it is
usually performed by calling a procedure in the run time system, the non-local
goto interpreter, rather than planting large amounts of code inline.

13 Compiled code: types and variables
This chapter discusses the amount of memory needed to hold the various types
of variables available in Pascal, and gives code sequences for their access. The
first section deals with scalars, the second section with records, arrays, sets and
files, and the third section deals with pointers and the heap. The possible effects
of packing records and arrays are also discussed in section 13.2.

13.1

Scalar types

Scalar types in Pascal fall into two groups. Those in the first group are known
as ordinal types and comprise integer, char, Boolean, enumerated, and subrange
types. The usual implementation of Pascal types, as assumed for chapter 11, maps
ordinal types onto various lengths of the basic arithmetic provided in the computer.
In the computers considered here, this is two's complement fixed point arithmetic.
Effectively, the value stored for any scalar of an ordinal type is the value returned
by applying the operator ord to it. The ord of a value must be the same whether it
comes from a variable of subrange or full range type, and it is this value which is
actually stored.
The choice of a value for maxint, which determines the maximum range of
fixed point values, is left to the implementor by the Pascal standard and is clearly
influenced by the target machine. The natural choice on the PDP11 would be to
make the integer type correspond to a one word or 16 bit value but this gives a
very small value of 32767 for maxint. Because it is so simple to define and use
subrange types in Pascal, an equally valid choice would be to use 32 bit values.
This suffers from space, and possibly time, penalties but gives a much larger value
of maxint, while allowing the user access to single length integers by declaring
the corresponding subrange (-32768 .. 32767). In a similar way, the user could
declare single byte values by using the subrange 0 . . 127. It may be possible
to treat subranges of -128 .. 127 or 0 .. 255 as bytes but the compiler must
take care with the sign when bytes are extended to words. For example the PDP II
usually automatically sign extends bytes to 16 bit values on access, so -128 ..
127 is preferable to 0 . . 255. With the 68000 a choice is possible, as an extra
instruction has to be planted to extend either a signed or unsigned value obtained
172

Compiled code: types and variables

173

from memory. The disadvantage of using single byte values is, as indicated in
chapter 11, that extra orders are often needed to perform arithmetic. However,
when it is considered that bytes may conveniently be used for character variables
and arrays, the balance of the argument favours the use of single bytes for small
integer subranges.
In the examples given, for simplicity, the maximum range chosen for an integer
corresponds to a single signed 16 bit word on the PDP11 and a 32 bit longword on
the 68000. Subranges that are small enough are stored in a single signed byte; 16
bit subranges are also allowed on the 68000. Char and Boolean fit in a byte when
the ord representation is used. Similarly, user defined enumerated types with 128
or fewer distinct values are stored in a single byte, as are small enough subranges
of any ordinal type.
Consider for example an enumerated scalar type:
TYPE primary = (red, yellow, blue);
VAR colour : primary;
The elements of the scalar type are just numbered from 0, so that red, yellow and
blue are represented by 0, 1 and 2, respectively. As the largest enumeration value
is two the space allocated to the variable colour is just one byte.
At this point it is worth considering whether Booleans should be packed into
single bits rather than wasting 7 bits per byte. Instructions for manipulating single
bits are non-existent in the PDP11 and quite clumsy in the 68000. Consequently
significantly more instructions are neededto manipulate single bit objects. Similar
considerations apply to small enumerated types and subranges which might fit
into a few bits. The overhead of the extra instructions can be justified if the user
explicitly asks for a packed structure, but it is unreasonable to apply this packing
elsewhere.
The second group of scalar types contains just the real type and is usually
implemented using floating point operations. Floating point operations are often
provided by the computer hardware but, particularly in smaller machines, the operations may need to be emulated using a software package. The choice of variable
size is usually dictated by the facilities available, and so is usually either a 32 or 64
bit value in some standard representation. The IEEE floating point standard representations are generally preferred unless the particular computer provides some
incompatible facilities. This standard allows both lengths; 64 bit is more accurate but more space and time consuming. A compromise is to choose 32 bits by
default, but provide a compiler option to allow the user to select 64 bits.
Consider now the allocation of memory locations, in stack frames, to represent
declared variables. A naive allocation algorithm might waste a few bytes. In
figure 13.1 the variables are positioned on the stack in the order in which they
are declared. In this figure each cell represents 16 bits. 22 bits, represented by
xs, are wasted. The effort required in the compiler to overcome the wastage is
quite considerable; in any case some wastage of space is often inevitable. The
differences between the two representations are that the two machines order the

13.2 Structured types
Records

the compiled code is:

where enjoyed is the displacement of the field from the start of record, 10 bytes,
and toy 1is the displacement of the start of the record from frame base (PDP11:
-14, 68000: -16, the difference being the consequence of 32 bit linkage words in
the 68000). Observe that, because the relative position of the fields is known at
compile time, the compiled code for accessing fields of records is as good as that
for simple variables. No loss of efficiency results when the user chooses a record
structure in preference to a sequence of simple variables.
Records with variant parts must have space allocated for the variant with the
largest total field size. The other variants can then be mapped into part of the space
allocated to the largest variant.

Packed records
When the user specifies that a record is packed the implementor has the choice of
attempting to arrange the fields in a way more economical of memory but possibly
more expensive in access time. On a byte orientated computer it is quite easy to
achieve a small saving. The fields of a record can be reordered so that all those
belonging on word boundaries come first, and those that can be on byte boundaries
come after. Thus several byte sized objects scattered through a record are collected
into one or more words of memory. This requires no extra instructions to access

176

Compiler Engineering using Pascal

the fields. In fact, this scheme is so simple that a compiler may do it for all
records, packed or not. This scheme may also be used while allocating memory
for variables, to minimise any space wasted.
If the computer has no bit field manipulation instructions this is probably the
best that can be realistically achieved. If, however, as in the 68020, instructions
are available to extract an arbitrary number of bits from a word then packing to the
bit level may be attempted. This will inevitably cost the extra instruction needed
to extract or replace the bit field but it will result in the most economical memory
use. In the example given previously, in figure 13.2, kind could occupy 2 bits, day
5 bits, month 4 bits, year 12 bits, enjoyed 2 bits and broken 1 bit. These 26 bits
can be held in 4 bytes with 6 bits wasted and the overall record size reduced from
12 bytes unpacked to 8 bytes packed.

Arrays
A Pascal array declaration is of the form:
ARRAY [index type] OF element type
The space occupied by the array is simply the cardinality of the index type (the
number of different values the index can legally take) times the space taken up by
a single item of element type. Thus:
ARRAY [1..10] OF integer
takes up 10 (the cardinality of the type 1 .. 10) times 1 word (the space occupied
by an integer), or 10 words. More complicated cases can be dealt with in a similar
manner. In particular, a multidimensional array such as:
ARRAY [1..10, 1..5] OF integer
is equivalent in Pascal to the declaration:
ARRAY [1..10] OF ARRAY [1..5] OF integer
The space taken is ten times the space occupied by the smaller array, which is five
times one word. Thus the complete array occupies fifty words.
Arrays in Pascal are static; that is the ranges of the indices, which define the
array bounds, are known at compile time, and hence the size is compile time
known and the array elements can be placed on the run time stack, alongside other
variables. The one exception, conformant array parameters passed by value, was
considered in chapter 12. An example of the allocation of space on the stack is
shown in figure 13.4. Note that the elements are placed in reverse order in relation
to frame base (FB). This is because the stack is negative going; that is each new
item on the stack is at a lower address than the previous item. However, it is
simplest to access array elements by adding the (scaled) index to the address of
the zeroth element, so that each element is at a higher address than the previous
element.
The sequence for accessing an element of an array is:

Multi-dimensional arrays
As mentioned before, the Pascal standard insists that a multi-dimensional array
is treated as a nested series of one dimensional arrays. Amongst other things,
this means that rows from two-dimensional arrays can be used exactly as onedimensional arrays. For example, figure 13.6 shows a two dimensional array allocated space as an array of 3 one dimensional arrays, each of 4 integers. A single
element, such as a[iJ], is accessed by two successive indexings as shown in figure 13.7. It may seem excessive to stack the index instead of using another register
or performing other optimisations. The advantage is that this sequence is general
enough to cope with any number of indexes, each of any complexity. Nevertheless, some optimisations could certainly be made to accesses with simple index
expressions. The multipliers used to scale the indices are known at compile time
except for conformant array parameters, where they must be computed from the
index ranges.

Addressing vectors
The method described above makes use of the multiply instruction. If this is relatively slow an auxiliary array, or addressing vector, may be generated which contains the precomputed multiplier for every row of the two dimensional array. The
precomputed vector may be generated at compile time thereby avoiding any need
for multiplication during an access. One way of regarding this is as a transformation of the Pascal source. Figure 13.8 shows a two dimensional array declaration
and access, together with the transformed Pascal.
{Original declaration}
VAR a : ARRAY [1 .. 10, 1 .. 5] OF integer;
{Original access}
x := a [i, j]
{Transformed declaration}
VAR a: ARRAY [1 .. 50] OF integer;
a.mult: ARRAY [1 .. 10] OF 1 .. 50;
FOR i := l TO 10 DO
a.mult [i] := i * 5;
{Can be done at compile time, by storing a.mult in the code}
{Transformed access}
x := a [a.mult [i] + j]

Figure 13.8

Transformation to access arrays using addressing vectors

Arrays of records
Arrays of records are accessed in a similar manner to arrays of scalars. For example, figure 13.9 shows the code generated for an access of a field of an element
of an array of records. In these sequences the index range to be checked is 1..10
and disp is the sum of the field displacement from the start of the record and the
displacement of the hypothetical record 0 from frame base.

asks for packed variables will the packing actually be done, and then only for an
array of Booleans, where there is a chance that more space will be saved in the
array than wasted in the access sequences. Consider the example in figure 13.10.
In this sequence the index is divided by 8; the quotient is used to find the correct
byte to access. The remainder of the division determines the bit position within

entries: 1, 2, 4, 10, 20, 40, 100, 200. The built in procedures pack and unpack
work in a similar way. However, as they access each bit in tum in the array there
is considerable scope for optimisation.

Set types
Set types are implemented as one bit per set element. Consider:
TYPE primary = (red, yellow, blue);
colour = SET OF primary;
VAR hue : colour;
Here red, yellow and blue are represented by bits 0, 1, and 2, respectively of a
byte and hence [red] = 1, [yellow]= 2, [blue]= 4, as in chapter 11. Efficiency
considerations encourage restriction of the number of elements in a set to the word
length of the machine. Unfortunately the 16 bit word of the PDP11 is too small
to hold many useful sets, and the 68000 32 bit longword is only slightly better.
In particular many programs require set of char which usually needs up to 128
elements. This requires four PDP11 words or two 68000 longwords. The best
solution is to vary the representation depending on the number of set elements,
and accept that the code sequences will be rather cumbersome if more than one
computer word is required.

Files
Each file variable f has an associated buffer variable f l referencing the current
file element. A programmer can use read and write but can also use fl rather like
any other pointer to access the buffer variable. The built in procedures get and
put cause information to be moved between buffer variables and files, and also

Compiled code: types and variables

183

cope with any extra buffering expected by the operating system. Read and write
can usually be implemented using the buffer variable and calls on get and put.
The functions eoln and eof can be implemented by checks on the buffer variable.
All of these procedures and functions are implemented as calls on routines which
must be provided as part of the run time system associated with the compiler. In
fact most of the required procedures and functions of Pascal are provided by such
calls.
Pascal distinguishes between external files, which exist in the permanent computer system files tore, and internal files which only exist during a particular run of
a program. The implementation of procedures rewrite and reset for external files is
specific to the filestore provided by the underlying operating system. For internal
files the implementor has a choice, either to provide his or her own implementation or to rely on the same mechanisms used for external files. The latter course
is attractive and simple, provided the operating system is not grossly inefficient
in file handling, and provided it does not impose unreasonable restrictions on the
number of files which can be open at any one time.
Input from a keyboard poses a particular problem. The associated buffer variable, input T, will only contain a valid character after the user has typed the character. It is very inconvenient if the system insists that the user inputs the first character before the program starts to run, and all subsequent characters one ahead of
when they are actually required by the program. Fortunately the Pascal standard
allows so-called lazy input from the keyboard, so that a character is only demanded
when it is actually required. A consequence for the implementor providing lazy
input is that any access to input Tmust first test whether the buffer contains a character. If none is present the program must wait for input. An additional Boolean
flag must be maintained to indicate whether the buffer contains a valid character.

13.3 Use of pointers
A pointer is just the address of a data item. Generating correct code for Pascal
pointers is relatively simple because the type of object pointed to is known at
compile time. Pointers cannot point to named variables but only point to items
created dynamically by the procedure new. This is a very restricted use of pointers
compared with some other languages such as C, where any pointer can be set
pointing to any declared variable, or component of a variable, of the correct type.
Given the declarations:

p : Tinteger;
x: integer;
Pascal can make use of pointers in statements such as:
X :=pT
or:
PT :=x

Compiler Engineering using Pascal

184

The T in this context means dereference p. So, for example, the first statement
causes x to be set to the integer value of the object at which p points.
The general philosophy for code generation for dereferencing is to move the
pointer to a register, and then use the register in a similar manner to frame base.
Another addressing register will be used; in the PDP11 only R 1is available but in
the 68000 A4 can be chosen. Thus for the above assignments the code given in
figure 13.12 would be generated. In fact for scalars the PDPll indexed deferred

as an assignment to a compiler declared pointer, relative to which all subsequent
accesses to components of the data structure can be made.
In Pascal it is helpful to include some run time checking in the implementation
of pointers. The major problem with pointers is that it is possible to inadvertently
access data items that have already been disposed of. One solution might be for
dispose to set its parameter to nil after disposing of the space to which it pointed.
However, as the user can make copies of a pointer before calling dispose, this
would still not be secure. A better method would be to add an extra check word to
each pointer and each item. This check word would be unique to the call of new
that created the item and set the first pointer pointing to it. For example, it could
be simply the number of times new has been called since the program started.
Each time the item was accessed via the pointer, the access would be preceded by
code which would compare the two check words and cause an error if they were
different. Dispose would reset the check word of the item, so any subsequent

13.4 Managing the heap
When space is created dynamically using the procedure new the space must exist
until either the end of the program or it is disposed of. This implies the existence
of a global storage area apart from the stack, as the stack frame associated with a
procedure is removed when the program returns from that procedure. This global

186

Compiler Engineering using Pascal

area is normally called a heap.
A simple implementation, illustrated in figure 13.14 is to keep a global variable: "next free space on heap". New advances this pointer, whenever called, by
the amount of storage required, and yields the address of the memory just allocated. This approach is inadequate on its own because after several news the free
heap:
allocated
space
nfs~

free
space

Figure 13.14

Simple heap implementation

space might be exhausted. Calls of dispose might have released space but such
space will be in a number of separate fragments. This is because Pascal items no
longer required may be disposed in any order. The simple approach has no way
of reusing such space.
Garbage collection is the retrieval of unwanted space. Implementations of list
processing and similar systems pay considerable attention to algorithms for effective garbage collection. Pascal systems usually find a relatively simple method
adequate. One such method is to keep a list of free spaces, each with its size
recorded, so that when new is called it first checks the free list to see if a hole
of suitable size exists. If no suitable retrieved space exists the simple allocation
continues. This method actually works well when the user repeatedly news and
disposes objects of the same size, as often happens in Pascal. The alternative of
actually moving objects on the heap, in order to consolidate the free space, is difficult to implement because as soon as an object is moved all the pointers which
reference it need updating. The information needed to find all such pointers is not
readily available in a Pascal implementation.

13.5 Problems
13.1 Design an implementation of Pascal sets which allows for sets of arbitrary
size.
13.2 Design code sequences to perform the run time checking of pointers, using
the checkword method suggested in section 13.3.
13.3 One method of reallocating space on the heap is to form a list of free spaces
for each size of space available. Discuss the advantages and disadvantages
of this proposed method.

14 Code optimisation
The most important decisions to make about code optimisation are whether it is
worth doing, and if so, what part of a user program to optimise. For example, in a
teaching environment, the majority of programs will fail, either at compile time or
run time, and those that do run should not do so for very long. Any run time saving
would usually be outbalanced by the extra compile time required to optimise the
programs. However, in a commercial environment, where the same program may
be run again and again, most optimisations would be cost effective. A particular
case is optimisation of parts of operating systems and compilers, written in high
level languages, which will pay off in either environment. A rule of thumb for
programs is that 90% of the run time is spent in 10% of the program. If it is
possible to find the 10% then most of the compile time effort can be directed to
optimising it. If a program is mainly iterative, most time will be spent inside
the loops, and in particular the innermost loops. If a program is recursive, it is
worthwhile concentrating on procedure bodies and calls.
It is worth considering why optimisation is important. Why can't the user
put effort into optimising the source program and make the compiler writer's job
easier? There are three main reasons: portability, clarity and cost effectiveness.
Consider first portability. Because high level languages are designed to be machine independent, it is very difficult for the user to exploit the full facilities of
a particular computer. It is probably a mistake to attempt to do so, as many programs will inevitably need to be run on more than one machine. With regard to
clarity, high level languages are explicitly designed to be more abstract, and thus
more understandable than assembly languages. Thus, not only is it counter productive to expect users to contort programs to make them more efficient, it may
be impossible to remove all the obvious redundancies. For example, not only is it
less understandable to invent new variables to hold common subexpressions, such
as:
temp :• i * j;
&[temp] :• b[temp];

it may even be impossible, as for the implicit expression i * row length+ j in:
a[i, j] := b[i, j]

Thirdly, it is much better for the compiler writer to invest a few man years in
producing better code than for hundreds or thousands of users each to spend days
187

188

Compiler Engineering using Pascal

or weeks trying to optimise their programs. The time spent in this latter way would
only occasionally be justified. The only optimisation that users must make is to
select the best algorithms for the job, and then allow the compiler to generate code
for the algorithms as efficiently as reasonably possible.
Much of the research effort in optimisation has been directed to exploiting
the features of supercomputers such as the Cyber 205 or the Cray 2. Much such
optimisation has been directed towards the use of powerful vector machine instructions, which correspond to whole loops of conventional language statements.
Consideration of such systems is outside the scope of the present book, so the
remainder of this chapter will concentrate on techniques appropriate to more conventional computers.
Code optimisations can be classified in several ways. One is machine dependent versus machine independent. Machine dependent optimisations attempt to
exploit specific features of a particular computer, such as the registers provided.
Machine independent transformations attempt to avoid unnecessary work, for example by moving common subexpressions out of a loop. Another classification is
global versus peephole. Global optimisations are usually performed before code
generation, in a separate pass, using a tree or graph representation of the program
to work out the possible flows of the program and the associated data dependencies as variable values are changed. Peephole, or local, optimisations are usually
performed during or just after code generation, perhaps within the routine which
actually puts the code into memory (for example, plant in the P compiler). Each
peephole optimisation involves at most a few object code instructions, whereas
global optimisations transform much larger regions of the program.
Some of the commoner optimisations are given in figure 14.1, classified as
machine dependent or independent, and as peephole or global. Consider first machine dependent optimisations. The peephole optimisations can be implemented
in a straightforward way. For example register reloading is avoided by noting what
is contained in each register and checking this before each register load. This information must be reset at any point where the contents of a register are uncertain
because of a control construct. Similarly, the substitution of simpler functions
such as TST for CMP, or CMPI, #0 results from a simple check on the instructions
to be planted. The exploitation of two address instructions to implement simple
assignments in a single instruction is another example.
Global machine dependent optimisations can include keeping frequently used
variables in registers. In the PDP11 there is little scope for this because of the
limited number of registers but in the 68000 several spare D registers are available. For simplicity, global register optimisations are often restricted solely to
loop constructs, which will be considered further below. The problem of using the
most appropriate length of forward branch was solved in a simple way in chapter 12 but a complete solution requires the branch operands to be filled in after
the length of the code to be branched over is known. Keeping frequently used
operand addresses in spare addressing registers is another optimisation which can

Compiler Engineering using Pascal

190

be performed over the whole program or applied in a more restricted way to the
bodies of loops.
Loop optimisation falls into the global, machine independent category, except
that any particular use of registers is machine dependent. Most of the loop optimisations are to do with reducing the control overhead. Loop jamming does this
by taking adjacent similar loops, checking that the loop bodies do not interact, and
placing all the loop bodies within a single control construct. For example:
FOR 1 := 1 TO 50 DO
a[1*2] := 1*2;
FOR j := 1 TO 50 DO
a[j*2-1] := -j*2;
FOR k := 1 TO 50 DO
b[k] := a[50+k];

can be jammed as:
FOR 1 := 1 TO 50 DO BEGIN
a[1*2] := 1*2;
a[1*2-1] := -1*2;
END;

FOR k := 1 TO 50 DO
b[k] := a[50+k];

The third loop cannot be merged with the previous two as its body is not independent of them.
Loop unrolling consists of duplicating the body of a loop within a control
construct that only involves half as many iterations. A small enough loop repeated
a small enough number of times may be completely unrolled into a linear sequence
of copies of the loop body. For example, the 'jammed' loop above can be unrolled
once as:
FOR 1 := 1 TO 25 DO BEGIN
a[1*4-2] := 1*4-2;
a[1*4-3] := -(1*4-2);
a(1*4] := 1*4;
a[1*4-1] := -1*4;
END;

Further optimisations can now be applied, such as further unrollings, and as indicated below.
An induction variable is a value which changes regularly each time around a
loop. Thus, in a for loop controlling a variable used as an index into an array,
both the controlled variable and an addressing value, equal to some multiple of
the index, are induction variables. The controlled variable changes in unit steps,
the other in increments of the array element size. If the only use of the controlled
variable is as an index, the compiler can remove this induction variable and instead
just make the loop control the addressing variable, using steps equal to the element
size. For example, the loop above has (at least) the induction variables 'i' and 'i *4',
and can be optimised to the pseudo-Pascal:

Code optimisation
FOR i

191

:= 4 TO 100 IN STEPS OF 4 DO BEGIN

a [i-2] : = i-2;

a[i-3] := -(i-2);
a[i] := i;
a[i-1] := -i;

END;

If the address calculations for the array indices were also examined, a further
induction variable of 'i * element size' would be found, but this has been ignored in
the example as it is difficult to represent in Pascal. Even if more than one induction
variable is necessary, the compiler can perform a strength reduction, for example,
calculating the addressing value by incrementation rather than by multiplication,
as multiplication is often a slower operation.
Loop invariants are expressions, or parts of expressions, which have the same
value each time around the loop, and therefore need only be calculated once, before the loop is started. They may consist of constants or of variables unaffected
by the loop. It is important to establish that such a change will actually result
in an improvement; some expressions may be so small that they are not worth
precalculating.
Amongst the remaining machine independent optimisations listed in figure 14.1
range check elimination is probably the most useful. By noting the possible values that the various factors in an expression can take and the way in which they
are combined it may be possible to deduce that a range check on assignment or
array access is not necessary. It may also be possible to limit the number of range
checks over a series of statements. In the loop used as an example above, for
instance, it would be possible to range check just the two extremes, 'i-3' and 'i',
rather than all four indices used in the loop body. It may even be possible to use
the knowledge that 'i' will vary between 4 and 100 to perform a single pair of
range checks before the loop is started.
The choice constructs, case and if, can only select complete statements in Pascal. Thus, a statement like:
IF a = 0 THEN b := c ELSE b := c DIV a

could be optimised by the compiler to something equivalent to the Algol 68 like
statement:
b := (IF a = 0 THEN c ELSE c DIV a)

or even:
b : = c (IF a <> 0 THEN DIV a)

which is not legal even in Algol 68. Instead of planting duplicated code for things
common to both choices, a single copy of the code is planted outside the choice
construct. Another machine independent optimisation is to eliminate the branches
to branches which can occur as a result of a sequence of procedure declarations
or control constructs. Branches to branches can be replaced by a branch to the
final destination, subject only to the previous consideration of branch distance.

Compiler Engineering using Pascal

192

Sometimes procedures are introduced into a program merely to clarify the program
structure. If such procedures are called only once then the code of the body of
the procedure can replace the call, rather like a so-called macro expansion. This
removes the overhead of the call, entry and exit sequences.
Sometimes the various global optimisations need to be applied repeatedly for
optimum effect. For example, loop jamming and unrolling will radically change
the bodies of the loops, giving scope for further optimisations such as common
subexpression removal.
Because global optimisation can implicitly involve reordering parts of the
source, it can disrupt the reporting of the location of run time errors in terms
of source lines. Similarly, if values are held in registers, the run time diagnostic
routines may be unable to print accurately the contents of variables. For this reason most systems allow the user to inhibit optimisation while the program is under
development.
Consider the application of optimisation techniques to the fragment of a Pascal
program in figure 14.2 which multiplies two matrices a and b together giving the
result in c. A major optimisation is for the compiler to notice that i, j and k all take
FOR i := 1 TO n DO
FOR j := 1 TO n DO BEGIN
c[i, j] := 0;
FOR k := 1 TO n DO
c[i,j] := c[i,j] + a[i,k]
END;

Figure 14.2

* b[k,j];

Array accesses susceptible to optimisation

values in the range 1 to n. Thus, instead of planting a range check for each index
of each array access, the compiler can plant a single check, using the extreme
values 1 and n, obeyed once before the loops are started. Better yet, the compiler
can perform the checks at compile time if n is a constant and the array bounds are
known. A second major improvement is for the compiler to recognise that mueh of
the array indexing is invariant in the inner loop and can be performed earlier. Thus,
the addresses of the start of the required rows, that is the hypothetical elements
c[i,O] and a[i,O], could be calculated within the outer (i) loop, and the addresses
of c[ij] and b[OJJ in the middle (j) loop. In this way the number of instructions
executed in the most frequent (k) loop is significantly reduced.
Another optimisation is to note common addressing expressions and replace
the induction variables i, j and k by variables moving in increments of the corresponding array element sizes. Thus, supposing the array elements each occupy
two bytes, i might be replaced by a variable moving in steps of 2n, j replaced by
a variable moving in steps of 2, and k replaced by two variables moving in steps
of 2 and 2n. Some of these might be held in registers. It might be possible to hold

Code optimisation

193

the address of the current element of a in a register, instead of the first k induction
variable, and use autoincrement mode in addressing this array. The final optimisations would be to hold c[ij] in a register around the innermost loop, to reorder
the expression to perform the multiplication first, and to exploit the ability to add
the result directly into the register holding c[ij].
It will be apparent that comprehensive optimisation requires the design of a
substantial additional phase of the compiler. It is not uncommon for such optimisation to increase the size of the compiler by 50%. It is not surprising, therefore,
that optimisation is often regarded as a dispensable luxury by compiler writers.

of a tree representation of the program to find which operands occur most
frequently. A better technique might attempt to identify frequently executed
parts of the program and concentrate on these.
14.2 A common way of optimising a for statement is to keep the value of the
control variable in a register throughout the loop. What restrictions does

15.1 Run time fault monitoring
A program can perform unexpectedly at run time, either by giving incorrect results
or by failing because of some definite error. The latter is known as a run time fault
and may be detected in one of several ways. A fault such as arithmetic overflow
or illegal address might be detected by the hardware. An attempt to reset a nonexistent external file, or other input or output error, would be detected by the
operating system. A range check or undefined case index value could be detected
by extra instructions planted in the compiled code. For example, in the Pascal
program in figure 15 .1, the value -10 to be assigned to d at line 6 is out of range.
There should therefore be a run time error, detected by a compiler generated range
check. Whenever such a run time fault occurs it is desirable that, either indirectly
program silly;
var a, b, c: integer;
d: o •• maxint;
procedure p (x, y: integer);
begin
d :=X - y;
end;
begin
a := 10;
b := 20;
p (a, b);
c
d;
end;

=-

Figure 15.1

Faulty program

via an interrupt or directly from the compiled code, a fault monitor routine is
called, which should:

1. Output information describing the actual fault. The fault will usually be
indicated to the routine by a fault number passed as a parameter. In the
example above, the message would be:
'range check error.'
194

Final considerations for Pascal

195

2. Output the position in the source program at which the fault occurred. In
the example above, the output would be:
'at line 6 in procedure p.'
3. Output the values of all the parameters and variables of the procedure that
was active at the time of the failure. In the example above, the output would
be:
'x = 10, y = 20.'
4. Repeat the last two steps above for the procedure or function that invoked
the routine just dealt with, and so on back up the chain of calls until the
source position and variables have been printed for each active procedure,
finishing with the main program. In the example above, the output would
be:
'called from line 11 of program silly
a= 10, b = 20, c =undefined, d = -10.'
5. Allow the user to continue, usually just by deleting the program and reverting back to the operating system to allow the typing of a new command.
However, some systems allow the values of variables to be manipulated, or
even the program source text modified, and the program restarted.
For the run time system to perform a reverse mapping from the body of a
crashed object program back to the original source program, the compiler must
generate information superfluous to its main job of producing the object program.
The information is held with the compiled code, ready for the run time system to
use. The nature of this information and an indication of the way in which it is
used by the run time system, to complete the diagnostic steps outlined above, is
now considered.
It is often difficult to transform the immediate cause of an error into something that the user can relate to the source program. For example, suppose a new
program is being debugged and a procedure recurses infinitely. It is likely that a
memory fault will be detected by the hardware because the run time stack overflows. However, a message saying 'memory fault' would not be as useful as a
message saying 'run time stack overflow' or even 'unable to create stack frame
for procedure p; too many nested calls'. One way of minimising this problem is
for the compiler to generate additional checks in the object code to detect potential
errors before they occur. However, this will be space and time consuming if done
exhaustively, and so the checks are often omitted. A second best solution is to put
sufficient information into the rest of the diagnostic output so that the context of
the error will become apparent. Thus, for example, if the user could be informed
that the run time stack overflow actually happened in a particular routine, which
had been called by the same routine, which had been called by the same routine,
and so on, it should be reasonably apparent that the routine was stuck in a recursive
loop.
The output of the location of the error in the code is also easy in machine
terms, as an address in the object code, but more difficult in terms of the source

196

Compiler Engineering using Pascal

program, as a particular line in a particular routine. For the run time system to be
able to convert an object code address to a source line number, the compiler must
generate a table containing (code address, line number) pairs. In this table each
line number is associated with the starting address of the corresponding compiled
code fragment. This table may be modified or added to by the linker as it relocates
code and attaches library routines to the compiled program. When the object
program fails and the diagnostic routine is entered, it must pick up the object code
address at which the fault occurred from the linkage information in the current
stack frame, assuming that its dynamic link points back to the last user stack frame.
The diagnostic routine then searches the table for a code address which is, or just
precedes, the address being searched for, and uses the corresponding line number
as the position in the source program. For the example program above, the table
would contain 13 entries, one for each source line.
A similar table must be generated by the compiler to relate source program line
numbers to procedure names. However, because procedures can textually enclose
each other, it is necessary to note the line numbers corresponding to the start and
end of each routine. This table can then be searched by the diagnostic routine to
locate the start and end line number pair which most closely contain the source line
number already found and so give the correct procedure name. For the example
program above the table would contain 2 entries, one for each routine, as shown
in figure 15.2. Some systems embed the names of procedures in the table of code

addresses. This makes it possible to perform both searches at once but it may be

When it comes to the output of the values of the variables, it is simplest to use
machine terms, perhaps printing all the values in memory between the frame base
and the top of stack. Once again, what is really required is information relating to
the original source program, such as the names and corresponding values, output
as the correct types, of the variables of the procedure. For the diagnostic routine
to be able to output all the variables and parameters of the now-located procedure,
the compiler must create lists of the names, types and offsets of the variables of
each procedure. These lists will be a subset of the information contained in the
name and property lists. The diagnostic routine must have a copy of the value of
frame base of the failing procedure, picked up from the dynamic link in a similar
way to the code address. This is combined with the offsets in the table to give the
actual addresses of the variables of the procedure. The values of these variables
can then be picked up from store and output with the variable names, in a format

The diagnostic routine can then use the information in the linkage area of the
stack frame pointed at by the frame base value already picked up to locate the
values of program counter and frame base for the previous active routine, and
repeat the two steps above as often as necessary.
The diagnostic process will usually finish by calling on the normal program
termination sequence to delete both run time system and user program, and allow
control to revert back to the operating system. However, as the addresses and types
of all variables can be found, it is possible for the user to modify the variables, and
it may also be possible to restart the program from the address at which it stopped.
As the starting code address of each line of the source program is known, it may
also be possible to restart at other locations, even removing stack frames so that
the program can be restarted in a different routine.
With on-line program development it is often more convenient to operate the
diagnostic routine in an interactive manner. Typically the diagnostic routine would
indicate the reason for failure and position at which the fault occurred, as in 1) and
2) above. Further information is then printed selectively on user request. An interactive system might allow the user to change the values of variables as in 5) above.
It could also allow the user to set breakpoints at particular lines or procedures.
When the executing program encounters such a breakpoint the diagnostic routine
is invoked, enabling the user to check values or alter variables before allowing
the program to continue. With this sort of information available, the programmer
should be able to track down the run time error quite straightforwardly.

15.2 Testing and validating the compiler
Before a compiler can be released for general use it must be established that it is
functioning correctly. The compiler writer must run test programs to establish that

198

Compiler Engineering using Pascal

correct programs run satisfactorily and that programs containing errors are treated
in a way that is helpful to the user. This can be an extremely laborious process
and, even when it is complete, it is likely that some cases will have remained
untested. It is impossible to prove, merely by testing, that a system will process all
programs correctly. It is nevertheless possible, with systematic testing, to reduce
the likelihood that any significant compiler errors remain.
Sometimes imprecision in the definition of a language results in different systems implementing different dialects. This is unfortunate as it makes programs
less portable between systems than they might otherwise be. In the case of Pascal
a standard definition exists and any implementation should attempt to conform to
this standard.
In order to simplify the process of establishing whether a particular Pascal implementation is satisfactory, a standard suite of programs, the 'Pascal Validation
Suite', has been devised. A compiler which deals correctly with all these programs is said to conform to the language standard. The suite comprises several
hundred programs, and although these can never be an exhaustive test a compiler
which passes the tests is unlikely to contain serious errors. An example of a simple program from the test suite is given in figure 15.4. This program will print
'PASS ...6.2.1-1' if it compiles without error and then runs to completion. If it
fails to do this the implementor must investigate and remedy the cause.
The programs in the test suite are subdivided into several classes. The first
class, conformance, comprises correct standard programs which must work correctly for the compiler to be acceptable. The second class, deviance, comprises
programs which deviate from the standard in some way. A simple example is
writing a constant as 1234E +2 when the standard does not allow a space after
the 'E'. The compiler should indicate the nature of the deviation even if it is still
possible to run the program. The class implementation defined is used to discover
information specific to a particular implementation, such as the ranges of integer
and real numbers permitted. The class implementation dependent explores areas
where the standard legitimately allows variations in the implementation, such as
the order of evaluation of index-expressions or actual-parameters. The class error
handling tests the ability of the system to deal satisfactorily with programs which
are known to fail at run time. The final major class is quality which is used for
two main purposes. The first is to check the accuracy of the real arithmetic. The
second is to check that there are no unreasonably small limits on the program
structure, for example the depth of statement nesting or the depth of recursion.
It is clear from the variety of classes and the number of test programs in the test
suite that validating a compiler, even for a relatively simple language like Pascal,
is by no means a trivial task. The subject is discussed in more detail in the book
"Pascal compiler validation" [Wichmann83].

Final considerations for Pascal

199

{TEST 6.2.1-1, CLASS=CONFORMANCE}
{ This program includes a sample of each declaration
part in its minimal form. Every possibility is covered elsewhere
in the validation suite, but the test is made here. }
program t6p2p1d1(output);
label
1;
const
one
1;
type
small = 1 .. 3;
var
tiny : small;
procedure p(var x
small);
begin

x:=1

end;
begin
goto 1;
1: p(tiny);
if (tiny
one) then
writeln(' PASS ... 6.2.1-1')
end.

=

Example validation suite program

Compiler Engineering using Pascal

15.3 Implementation language
Most of the source code for compilers presented in this book has been written in
Pascal. The compiler writer is always faced with the choice of what implementation language to use. This choice, which will be influenced by what is available, is
between one of three major possibilities. These are firstly, the assembly language
for the machine on which the compiler runs. The second possibility is a high level
language and the third some special compiler writing tool.
Consider each of these in turn. Assembly language is always available on the
target computer but unless it is impossible to use any other programming language
this is not a very good choice. A typical Pascal compiler producing reasonable
code and diagnostics is 10,000 to 15,000 lines of Pascal, which corresponds to a
large amount of assembly level code. The problems of developing and maintaining
large programs written in assembler far outweigh any of the possible advantages.
A high level language is a good choice for obvious reasons. Many languages
have been used and Pascal and C are quite popular. All high level languages
make algorithms easier to understand, debug, maintain, modify and port to other
machines. The disadvantage, that the compiler might be larger and less efficient
than one written well in an assembly language, is of little consequence now that
processor power and memory are so cheap. There is disagreement about whether
Pascal offers sufficient facilities to write a good compiler. The usual objections
are firstly, that Pascal does not have facilities to break a large program into independent self-contained modules and secondly, that the facilities for data structures
and string handling are too inflexible. Ideally an implementation language with
these features would be chosen. Languages which encourage explicit modularisation, including Modula 2 and Ada, are therefore gaining in popularity. Explicit
modularisation is not essential and many good Pascal compilers have in fact been
written in Pascal.
If the compiler is written in the language that it compiles, the use of the old
compiler to compile a new compiler which supersedes the old provides a convenient upgrade route. One can easily port the compiler or continue changing
and improving it once the initial binary exists. However, there is a fundamental
problem, known as bootstrapping: how is the first compiler compiled?
The simplest solution to the bootstrap problem for a compiler, written in Pascal,
relies on an existing Pascal compiler, usually on a second computer. The new
Pascal compiler is first compiled using the existing compiler, to give a version of
the compiler which can be run on the second computer. The new Pascal compiler
is then compiled by this first version, to give a compiler which can be run on the
target machine. If no Pascal compiler is available, but another high level language
compiler is, the new compiler can first be hand translated to the other language
and then compiled, again giving a first version of the compiler running on a second
computer. The Pascal source for the compiler is again compiled by this first version
to give a compiler which can be run on the target machine. These methods, which

Final considerations for Pascal

201

rely on the pre-existence of a high level language compiler, are known as half
bootstraps. They are widely used in compiler production.
In the unlikely event of no high level language compiler being available, a
full bootstrap method must be used. This involves hand translating the compiler
into assembly language. Because this is such an enormous task, a possible variant of this method is to invent an intermediate language, just powerful enough to
write a compiler in, but simple enough for a compiler for it to be written in an
assembly language. The full compiler is then translated by hand into the intermediate language, and a process similar to the half bootstrap above followed. With
widespread availability of computer resources, the use of any full bootstrap method
is nowadays likely to be the least effective way of producing a good compiler.
The third possibility, the use of any compiler writing tools that are available, is
a good idea as it will save time and hence money. It is also likely to result in a more
reliable and error free compiler. The exploitation of existing tools, provided they
are suitable for the task in hand, is probably the most effective route to constructing
a properly engineered compiler.
15.4

Problems

15.1 Investigate the run time debugging facilities provided with a Pascal system
to which you have access. In what ways are the facilities worse or better
than those described in this chapter?
15.2 Consider whether the provision of implementation defined features in the
Pascal standard is desirable or merely unavoidable. What problems do such
features present for the user wishing to move programs from one system to
another?
15.3 Compare the facilities of Pascal with any other high level language. Consider, in particular, which features of the other language would make the
compiler writer's task harder and which would make it easier compared
with writing in Pascal.

16 Modula-2
This book has dealt almost exclusively with Pascal. Since the definition of Pascal
many new computer languages have been invented. Some of these have provided additional features while retaining the general framework of a Pascal-like
approach. Two such languages are Modula-2 and Pascal Plus. They both have
many similarities to Pascal but provide new facilities in areas where Pascal is
considered deficient. The two main areas addressed by these languages are the
modularisation of programs into manageable independent parts and the expression of concurrency, or several simultaneous activities. The principles, and much
of the detail, of the Pascal compiler presented so far apply equally wellto these languages. The extensions needed to implement the additional features for Modula-2
are quite limited and are described below.
The most notable language derived from Pascal is Ada [Bames84]. This language cannot be described as a few small additions to Pascal as the changes and
new features are substantial. Nevertheless the compiling techniques described are
still applicable in general terms, despite the fact that a substantially different compiler will be needed to implement all the facilities of Ada.

16.1 Towards a Modula-2 compiler
The reader of the remainder of this chapter may find it helpful to have some familiarity with Modula-2, which could be obtained by reading [Wirth82]. There are
five main differences between Modula-2 and Pascal:
o Syntactic changes.
o The type 'procedure' which allows procedures to be treated as variables.
o Low level facilities.
o The module concept.
o The concept of a process to express concurrency.

Consider first the syntactic changes. The main syntactic change is that every
structure starting with a keyword, such as a while or if statement, also ends with
a keyword such as end. Thus the Pascal:
IF x > y THEN a := b ELSE a := 0

202

203

Modula-2

becomes in Modula-2:
IF x > y THEN a := b ELSE a := 0 END

These changes actually make a parser for Modula-2 easier to write than one for
Pascal, mainly because error recovery is simpler. They are easily incorporated
into the syntactic phase of the compiler and will not be considered further.
The declaration of a procedure type as in:
TYPE f f = PROCEDURE (REAL, INTEGER)

: REAL

causes symbol table and property entries to be created for the type ff. These contain
the number and type of the parameters of the procedure. They also note whether
the procedure is a function procedure and if so the result type. When a procedure
is assigned to a procedure variable the compiler must apply a semantic check that
the assigned procedure and the variable are of the same type. The same type means
having the same result type and number and type of parameters. Therefore, in the
example below, where the actual procedure gg is assigned to the variable pv, gg
and pv both have one real and one integer parameter and both return a real result.
TYPE f f = PROCEDURE (REAL, INTEGER) : REAL;
VAR pv :
ff;
PROCEDURE gg (x : REAL; 1 : INTEGER) : REAL;
BEGIN

{ body of gg }
END gg;

pv := gg;

In the compiled code the concept of a procedure variable, such as pv, is a slight
generalisation of the procedure passed as parameter, encountered at the end of
chapter 12, and is treated in a similar way. Thus, assuming static chains are being
used for non-local accesses, a procedure variable comprises the address of the start
of the procedure together with a static link value giving the stack environment in
which the procedure will run.
The low level facilities comprise type transfer functions and memory addressing. The type transfer functions allow the programmer to transfer bit patterns
between different types in a machine dependent way. They are a relaxation of the
strict typing of the language. For example given:
VAR 1

INTEGER;

o :

CARDINAL;

the assignment:
1 := 0

should cause a semantic error, whereas:
1 :• INTEGER(o)

compiles with no error. These functions are similar to ord and chr in Pascal.
Their effect is to inhibit the usual semantic type checking in the compiler. Their
implementation is straightforward and causes no extra instructions to be compiled.

204

Compiler Engineering using Pascal

The provision of a facility to access specific memory locations causes the introduction of the low level data types word and address. A word is the contents of
some memory location which may be copied into a variable of an ordinary type
using the type transfer functions discussed above. An address is the address of
a memory word and is treated as a pointer to word. There are two differences
between an address and an ordinary pointer in Modula-2. Firstly arithmetic on
addresses, similar to the arithmetic on cardinal numbers, is permitted; this is not
allowed for ordinary pointers. Secondly an address is assignment compatible with
any other pointer, so a pointer to any object can be copied into an address, or viceversa, without any semantic check on the type of the object. The function adr(x)
delivers the address of variable x, which requires code similar to that used when
passing a var parameter to compute the address. All these facilities are straightforward for the compiler writer to implement but machine dependent in the effect
seen by the user.
16.2 Modules
A complete Modula-2 program comprises a number of definition modules, anumber of implementation modules, each associated with a definition module, and a
main program module. The names used in each module are, by default, independent of those in other modules, so each module needs a separate symbol table and
property lists. In this way the data used within a module is hidden from other
modules. However some definitions are explicitly exported from definition modules so they can be imported into other modules. Figure 16.1 shows a definition
module, buffer, which exports two procedures and two Boolean variables. The
associated implementation module, shown in outline, gives the actual code for the
procedures and uses the variables without redeclaration. The main program module main imports and uses one of the procedures defined and exported by buffer.
The symbol and property tables for the various modules must be manipulated
to reflect the export and import of identifiers. In the properties for the definition
module every exported item is marked as such, and therefore available for import
into other modules. When an implementation module is compiled the corresponding definition module properties must be available. New symbols declared in the
implementation module are effectively added to these, but never visible to other
modules. Where the implementation module repeats the headings of the procedures being declared a consistency check must be made.
When an import is specified, as in the import of put to the main program,
the properties for the module specified (here buffer) must be located in order to
find the correct properties for the imported identifier. An entry for put is made
in the properties of main which references the corresponding properties in buffer.
When a call of put is encountered, the compiler must check on the number of
parameters and must use the correct parameter types in generating code for the
call. These properties make this possible. An alternative method of importation

Modula-2

205

DEFINITION MODULE buffer;
EXPORT QUALIFIED put, get, nonempty, nonfull;
VAR nonempty, nonfull: BOOLEAN;
PROCEDURE put(x: CARDINAL);
PROCEDURE get(VAR x: CARDINAL);
END buffer.
IMPLEMENTATION MODULE buffer;
PROCEDURE put(x: CARDINAL);
BEGIN
nonempty := TRUE;
{ etc }
END put;
{ Similar declaration of get }
END buffer.
MODULE main;
FROM buffer IMPORT put;
BEGIN
put(l23)
END main.

Figure 16.1

Modula-2 modules

is to import all the exported identifiers of a module. For example, import buffer
could be specified in main, causing buffer to be given properties of type module
within main. These properties cross reference the actual property lists for buffer.
When using this method, a call of get in main must be specified as buffer.get.
The compiler accesses the properties of main to find the cross reference to the
properties of buffer. The compiler can then locate the properties of get, within
those of buffer, and use them in compiling the call of buffer.get.
Modula-2 allows a module to be nested inside another module, or even inside
a procedure. Such a nested module is known as a local module. Where local
modules are used there is one difference. The exported symbols are only available
in the surrounding scope, that is the procedure or module in which the local module
was declared. Consequently the exported symbols of the local module are only
made available for import to the surrounding scope rather than being made globally
available.
The Modula-2 module system has been designed so that separate compilation
of modules is optionally available. This is possible provided that the properties
from the definition modules specified in the import list are available. When an implementation module is compiled the properties from the corresponding definition
module must also be available.
When separate compilation is used, it is the responsibility of the link-loading
system to ensure that the code for the various separately compiled modules required for a particular program are linked together and loaded into memory. The

compiler will not know the final memory addresses for code, so should generate
relocatable code whenever possible. Any remaining addresses that are not relative
within the module will have to be finalised by the linking process. The addresses
of procedures in other modules and of global variables of the various modules will
not be finalised until link time. The final action of the link-loader is to ensure that
the initialisation code for the various modules is executed on entry to the program.
16.3 Processes

A concurrent process is usually defined as an independent thread of execution
which may be executed in parallel with other processes. Modula-2 implements a
very restricted form of concurrent process compared with that of other languages
for expressing concurrency. It assumes that only one processor is being used. It is
therefore not necessary for the implementation to provide for genuine concurrency,
where two processes are actually running simultaneously on two processors, but
only for pseudo-concurrency in which the different processes take turns to use the
processor. This is simpler to implement than genuine concurrency. The Modula2 implementation is based on the primitive concept of coroutines. At any time
the executing process can create a new process, or thread of execution, called
a coroutine. The new coroutine remains suspended until an explicit transfer of

207

Modula-2

(FB ~)

control from the executing process. At this point execution of the new coroutine
replaces that of the previous one until another explicit transfer between coroutines.
Execution of a coroutine always resumes at the instruction after the suspending
transfer. The implementation does not therefore have to make any decisions about
when to run each process as the user controls the transfers between coroutines.
The procedure newprocess is used to create a coroutine. It also allows a
workspace to be created for the variables of the coroutine. This workspace is
needed because each coroutine performs a sequence of procedure calls and builds
its own stack independently of other coroutines. The creation of workspace by
newprocess requires the caller to specify the size and memory address of the new
workspace. Thus the primitive facility provided does not hide memory organisation issuf.".s from the caller. newprocess returns a var parameter of a type known
as process which is used to reference the coroutine in subsequent calls of transfer.
The procedure transfer causes a switch from one coroutine to another. Each coroutine has an independent stack within its own workspace. Hence when a transfer
takes place the stack pointers SF and FB must be noted and reset along with the
current program counter. The caller of transfer provides two var parameters of
type process. The first of these is used to store SF, FB and PC for the calling
coroutine, and the second contains the values for the new coroutine, which were
returned by a previous call of transferor by the initial call of newprocess. It follows that newprocess must return initial values of SF, FB and PC for the coroutine
it creates.
Figure 16.2 shows the skeleton of a program which creates a coroutine from
the procedure coroutn. After entering other, control is transferred to the coroutine

Compiler Engineering using Pascal

208

and will be transferred back sometime later. Figure 16.3 shows the stack for the
program module and the procedure other on the left; it is just the same as a Pascal
stack. On the right is the separate stack for coroutn, starting in this case at memory
location 100000, and of maximum size 1000, as specified in the call of newprocess. While coroutn is being executed, SP and FB relate to the stack for coroutn.
The bracketed values of SP and FB for procedure other are stored in the process
variable main ready to be restored when a transfer to main takes place. The values
of oldFB and static link in both coroutn and other point to the framebase of the
program module trivialprocesses.
